      From Vue developer to frontend engineer.

      A handbook for a frontend engineer with around three years of experience: rebuild the foundations properly (HTML, CSS, JavaScript, the web platform), go deep on Vue and learn React and Next.js for the job market, then the mid-level layer: architecture, testing, performance, UX, SQL, design systems, AI features and system design. Ends with 2026 project ideas and the job switch. Pick a part below; the sidebar then shows only that part.

      Start with part 1
      From the lectures explained in your Sheryians videosOther tags name the topic area
      Part 1

      Start here

      What companies expect from a 3-year frontend engineer, your fast path as a Vue developer, what JavaScript is and where it runs, and how to study.

      The 3-year frontend engineer: what's expected

      At ~3 years, companies stop hiring you for "can build screens" and start hiring you for judgement: you understand why things work (JavaScript, the browser, rendering), you own features end to end, you make sound architecture and performance decisions, you test, and you make the people around you better.

      Junior vs mid-level (SDE-2 / Frontend Engineer II)

      AreaJuniorMid-level (what interviews test for you)
      JavaScriptUses itExplains closures, this, prototypes, event loop, promises; writes polyfills; predicts output
      FrameworkBuilds componentsKnows how reactivity/rendering works internally, avoids unnecessary renders, designs reusable component APIs
      CSSMakes it look rightBuilds robust responsive layouts, understands the cascade/stacking contexts, builds a themeable design system
      ArchitectureFollows the structureStructures features, API layers and state; picks rendering strategy (SPA/SSR/SSG); justifies trade-offs
      QualityFixes bugsWrites unit/component/E2E tests, owns Core Web Vitals, accessibility and security basics
      DeliveryCompletes ticketsBreaks down features, writes small tech specs, estimates, reviews PRs, handles incidents
      PeopleAsks for helpMentors juniors, works directly with design/product/backend, communicates trade-offs

      The typical 3-YOE interview loop

      1. JavaScript deep-dive — event loop, closures, prototypes, this, async patterns, output questions, polyfills (debounce, Promise.all, bind, deep clone).
      2. Machine coding (60–90 min) — build an autocomplete, nested comments, infinite scroll, data table, kanban, in Vue or React (many companies accept either; some require React).
      3. Framework internals — Vue: reactivity (Proxy, track/trigger), computed vs watch, rendering and patch flags, nextTick, Pinia, Nuxt SSR/hydration. React: reconciliation, hooks, re-renders, server components.
      4. Frontend system design — design a news feed, chat, e-commerce listing page, design system; state, API, caching, performance, accessibility.
      5. Project / experience deep-dive — architecture of what you built at work, your biggest performance or quality improvement, a production incident, a decision you'd change.
      6. DSA (product companies) — easy/medium arrays, strings, maps, trees.
      7. Hiring manager / behavioural — ownership, conflict, mentoring, impact.

      Recommended: learn by building

      Follow the Build-to-learn project (Part 14, it unlocks once you finish React, Next.js and Testing): 11 milestones of a real React app, each linking to the concepts, tests and interview questions it needs. Pair it with a daily 1.5-hour fundamentals drill from the JavaScript parts and Interview prep. The order below is the reference path if you prefer studying part by part.

      Your fast path (Vue developer, shaky foundations)

      1. Foundations first: HTML & CSS, JavaScript core, Browser & DOM, Advanced JavaScript, How the web works. Don't skip because you "already use it" — this is where 3-YOE candidates most often fail.
      2. Vue, properly: the Vue part to fill gaps, then Vue in depth for internals, performance, patterns and Nuxt.
      3. TypeScript if your Vue work was mostly JavaScript.
      4. React + Next.js: most job posts want React. Your Vue knowledge transfers; the React parts show the differences.
      5. Engineering + Mid-level skills: testing, tooling, performance, UX for engineers, architecture, design systems, AI features, and enough SQL to work full-stack.
      6. Interview prep + 2026 projects, in parallel from week 3 onwards.

      What JavaScript is

      In one line: JavaScript is the language that gives web pages behaviour. HTML is the structure, CSS is the look, JavaScript is the logic (clicks, calculations, fetching data, changing the page).

      A short history (as told in the lecture)

      • 1995: Brendan Eich, working at Netscape (a browser company), built a language in about 10–15 days. It was first called Mocha, then LiveScript.
      • Java was famous at the time, so it was renamed JavaScript for marketing. JavaScript has nothing to do with Java. People assumed it was "advanced Java"; it wasn't, but the name stuck.
      • Different companies started making their own versions, which was painful for developers. So a standards body, ECMA, took over. The official standard is called ECMAScript (ES). JavaScript is the most famous implementation of it.
      • Because it was built so fast, early JS had loopholes. ES5 (2009) fixed some. ES6 / ES2015 was the huge update: let, const, arrow functions, classes, promises, modules, template strings, destructuring. Since then a new version comes every year (ES2016, ES2017 …).

      When a tutorial uses var it's "old" JS (ES5). When it uses let/const it's "modern" JS (ES6+). You should know both, but write modern.

      Where JavaScript runs

      • In the browser. Every browser has a JS engine: V8 (Chrome, Edge, Brave), SpiderMonkey (Firefox), JavaScriptCore (Safari). The engine reads your code and runs it.
      • Outside the browser with Node.js (V8 + extra features like file system access). This is how JS is used for servers, CLIs and tools.
      • JS is single-threaded (does one thing at a time), dynamically typed (a variable can hold any type), interpreted / JIT-compiled, and supports multiple styles: procedural, object-oriented and functional.

      Words vs keywords

      A keyword is a reserved word that means something to JavaScript: var, let, const, if, else, for, function, return, class… A normal word (like harsh or total) means nothing until you give it meaning, e.g. by making it a variable name. You cannot use keywords as variable names.

      How to run JavaScript (VS Code setup)

      Three ways, and you'll use all of them. Details and shortcuts are in the next topic, Your VS Code workflow.

      1. A .js file run with Node, straight from VS Code

      Best for practising the language itself: variables, loops, functions, arrays, objects. Create practice.js, press Ctrl+` to open the integrated terminal, and run it.

      js
      // practice.js
const nums = [5, 12, 8];
console.log(nums.map(n => n * 2));      // [ 10, 24, 16 ]
      bash
      node practice.js          # run once
node --watch practice.js  # re-run automatically on every save

      2. A script file linked to HTML, opened with Live Server

      Needed for anything touching the page: document, events, DOM, localStorage. Install the Live Server extension, right-click index.html → "Open with Live Server". The page reloads on every save, and your console.log output appears in the browser's console (F12).

      html
      <!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
  <head>
    <title>My page</title>
    <!-- defer = download now, run after the HTML is parsed -->
    <script src="script.js" defer></script>
  </head>
  <body>
    <h1>Hello</h1>
  </body>
</html>
      js
      // script.js
document.querySelector("h1").textContent = "Changed by JavaScript";

      3. The browser console

      F12 → Console, for one-off experiments and inspecting a live page. Nothing is saved, so keep real practice in files.

      Browser-only vs Node-only code. document, window, localStorage, alert, prompt and confirm exist only in the browser: running them with node file.js throws ReferenceError: prompt is not defined. require, process and fs exist only in Node. Everything else, the language itself, is the same in both.

      AttributeDownloadsRunsOrder kept?
      (none)Blocks HTML parsingImmediatelyYes
      deferIn parallelAfter HTML is fully parsedYes
      asyncIn parallelAs soon as it downloadsNo
      type="module"In parallelDeferred by defaultYes

      Without defer, put the <script> tag just before </body>. Otherwise your code runs before the HTML exists and document.querySelector returns null.

      Output and comments

      js
      console.log("normal message");
console.info("info");  console.warn("warning");  console.error("error");
console.table([{ name: "Harsh", age: 26 }]);   // pretty table
console.clear();

console.dir(someObject, { depth: null });      // full object tree (handy in Node)
console.time("load"); /* … */ console.timeEnd("load");   // measure duration

// Browser-only dialogs — they don't exist when you run a file with Node,
// and real apps use custom UI instead of these:
// alert("A popup message");  confirm("Are you sure?");  prompt("Your name?");

// single-line comment
/* multi-line
   comment */

      Statements end with a semicolon ;. JS will usually insert missing ones for you (ASI), but writing them avoids rare bugs.

      Your VS Code workflow

      Set this up once and every example in this handbook runs in seconds. One practice folder, the integrated terminal for Node, Live Server for anything touching the page, and the debugger instead of scattered console.logs.

      Install

      • VS Code and Node.js LTS (nodejs.org). Check in VS Code's terminal (Ctrl+`): node -v and npm -v should print versions. Node also gives you a REPL: type node, experiment, .exit to quit.
      • On Windows, if npm scripts fail with an execution-policy error in PowerShell, switch the integrated terminal to Git Bash or Command Prompt (Terminal: Select Default Profile).

      Extensions worth having

      ExtensionWhy
      ESLint + PrettierCatch mistakes as you type; format on save
      Live ServerServe HTML with auto-reload for DOM practice
      Vue - Official (Volar)Vue SFC support, template type-checking
      Error LensShows errors inline on the line instead of only underlined
      Tailwind CSS IntelliSenseClass autocomplete and colour previews
      GitLensBlame, history and diffs inside the editor
      REST Client or Thunder ClientCall APIs from a .http file without leaving VS Code
      Vitest and Playwright TestRun and debug tests from the Testing sidebar
      Auto Rename Tag, Path Intellisense, Code Spell CheckerSmall daily time savers
      ES7+ React/Redux snippetsSnippets like rafce when you start React

      Skip "Code Runner": running node file.js in the terminal is closer to how you'll work in a real project.

      Keep it in Git from day one: git init, commit after each topic. Your commit history becomes proof of consistent practice, and you'll need Git fluency anyway.

      Running things

      bash
      node 01-variables.js         # run a file
node --watch 01-variables.js # re-run on save (great while practising)
node                         # REPL for quick experiments

npx serve .                  # quick static server if you skip Live Server
npm create vite@latest my-app     # scaffold a real project later
npm run dev                  # Ctrl+click the printed localhost URL to open it

      Running TypeScript files directly: npx tsx script.ts (or node script.ts on recent Node versions). Type-check a project with npx tsc --noEmit.

      Debug instead of logging

      1. Click to the left of a line number to set a breakpoint (red dot).
      2. For Node files, open the JavaScript Debug Terminal (Ctrl+Shift+P → that name) and run node file.js: breakpoints just work, no config.
      3. Execution pauses there. Inspect Variables, hover values, use the Debug Console to run expressions, and Watch to track one value.
      4. Step with F10 (over), F11 (into), Shift+F11 (out), F5 (continue).
      5. Right-click a breakpoint for a conditional breakpoint (i === 42) or a logpoint (prints a message without editing code).
      json
      // .vscode/launch.json — debug a browser page served by Live Server
{
  "version": "0.2.0",
  "configurations": [
    { "type": "chrome", "request": "launch", "name": "Debug page",
      "url": "http://127.0.0.1:5500", "webRoot": "${workspaceFolder}" },
    { "type": "node", "request": "launch", "name": "Debug current file",
      "program": "${file}" }
  ]
}

      Learning to read the Variables panel and the call stack is the single biggest speed-up for understanding closures, this and async code later in this handbook.

      Emmet in HTML files: type ! + Tab for a page skeleton, ul>li.item*5 + Tab for a list, div.card>h3+p + Tab for nested markup.

      Git inside VS Code

      The Source Control panel (Ctrl+Shift+G) stages, commits, branches and resolves merge conflicts with an inline merge editor. Learn the buttons, but also learn the commands in the Tooling part: interviews ask about rebase vs merge, not about which button you click.

      AI assistants

      Copilot, Claude Code or Cursor are normal parts of the workflow now, but while you're rebuilding foundations, type the code yourself first and use AI to explain, review or generate boilerplate you already understand. You'll be asked to write debounce and a polyfill for Promise.all on a shared screen with no autocomplete.

      How to study JavaScript

      The lecturer's main point: most students can read a loop or a function but can't write the logic inside it. These notes follow the same fix the course uses: concept → confusion points → practice.

      • Understand, don't memorise. You can't cram 200 lines of code. You can understand small pieces and learn to write them in parts.
      • Break problems into steps in plain English first ("first the plus button is clicked, so select the plus button…"). Then translate each step into code.
      • Every loop has a start, an end and a change. If you can say those three, you can write the loop.
      • Every operator and method has a use case. Remember the use case, not the syntax, and the syntax follows.
      • Practice after every topic. Every section ends with questions; write the answers in a real file in VS Code (01-javascript/07-loops.js) and run them with node, rather than reading the solution and nodding.
      • Type it, don't copy it. Copy-pasting an example teaches your clipboard, not you. Retype it, break it on purpose, and predict the output before running.
      • Using AI: if you already know how something works (e.g. document.createElement), let AI type the boilerplate. If you're learning it for the first time, write it yourself.
      • Consistency beats intensity. Don't try to finish a topic a day. Spend the time, it adds up.
      Part

      Build-to-learn project: JobPilot

      One React + TypeScript project, built in 11 milestones. Each milestone teaches the concepts it needs (why they exist, the syntax, the Vue equivalent), adds tests, and ends with the interview questions it prepares you for. By the end you have a deployed, tested app and a story for every round.

      What you'll build, and how to use this part

      JobPilot is a job-application tracker: save jobs, move them across a board (Wishlist → Applied → Interview → Offer / Rejected), search real job listings, set follow-up reminders, see your response rate, and later let AI extract details from a job description. You'll use it for your own job search, so it gets real use and real feedback from day one.

      Why this project

      • It forces every core concept: state, forms, lists, reducers, effects, storage, routing, fetching, async, context, performance, accessibility, testing, then Next.js, SQL and AI.
      • It's React, which most job posts ask for, while you already know the same ideas from Vue. Every milestone shows "in Vue you'd write…" so you learn by contrast, which is fast.
      • It's relevant: interviewers immediately understand it, and "I built the tool I used to run my own job search" is a memorable story.

      Each milestone follows the same loop

      1. Build the listed features. Try first; look at the code in the milestone only when stuck.
      2. Learn the concepts as they appear. Each has a short "why" and the syntax; links go to the full handbook topic.
      3. Write the tests listed. Tests are part of "done", never "later".
      4. Answer the interview questions out loud without looking.
      5. Commit and post: one commit per feature, one LinkedIn post per milestone or two.

      The roadmap

      #MilestoneMain conceptsDays*
      1Setup and first componentsVite, TypeScript types, JSX, components, props, modules, first tests1–2
      2Add and list applicationsuseState, controlled forms, events, lists and keys, immutable updates2
      3The board: move, edit, delete, undouseReducer, pure functions, drag and drop, closures and timers, refs2–3
      4PersistencelocalStorage, sessionStorage, cookies, JSON, useEffect, custom hooks, runtime validation2
      5Global state and themeContext, provider pattern, prop drilling, when to use a store1–2
      6Routing and URL stateReact Router, dynamic routes, search params, lazy loading, Suspense, error boundaries2
      7Job search from an APIfetch, promises, async/await, event loop, debounce, AbortController, CORS, error classes, MSW3
      8Modal, validated forms, accessibility<dialog>, refs, useId, React Hook Form + Zod, keyboard support, prototypes (test polyfill)2–3
      9Dashboard and performanceDerived data, reduce, useMemo, memo, profiling, virtualisation, code splitting2–3
      10End-to-end tests, CI, deployPlaywright, accessibility checks, GitHub Actions, SPA hosting2
      11Phase 2: Next.js + Supabase + AIServer components, server actions, SQL schema and RLS, auth, AI structured output and streaming7–10

      *Full-time days. Phase 1 (milestones 1–10) is about 3–4 weeks; phase 2 about 1.5–2 weeks.

      What this project won't drill enough, so keep the 1.5-hour morning routine going alongside it: this and prototypes in depth, output-prediction questions, writing polyfills from scratch, CSS layouts from a blank file, and DSA. Use Write it yourself, Predict the output and the JavaScript parts for those.

      M1 · Setup, types and first components

      Build

      • The Application type and a STATUSES list.
      • ApplicationCard and StatusBadge components rendering hard-coded sample data.
      • A daysSince() date helper ("Applied 5 days ago").

      Concepts: why and syntax

      TypeScript types for your data (TypeScript)

      Why: the shape of an application is used in 30 places. A type makes the editor catch typos and missing fields everywhere at once. A union of literals prevents invalid statuses.

      ts
      // src/features/applications/types.ts
export const STATUSES = ["wishlist", "applied", "interview", "offer", "rejected"] as const;
export type Status = (typeof STATUSES)[number];      // "wishlist" | "applied" | ...

export interface Application {
  id: string;
  company: string;
  role: string;
  status: Status;
  url?: string;           // ? = optional
  location?: string;
  followUpOn?: string;    // ISO date string
  notes?: string;
  tags: string[];
  createdAt: string;
  updatedAt: string;
}
export type NewApplication = Pick<Application, "company" | "role"> & Partial<Pick<Application, "url" | "location">>;

      Components and props (Components and props)

      Why: a component is a reusable function from data (props) to UI. Props are read-only inputs; the parent owns the data.

      tsx
      // src/features/applications/components/ApplicationCard.tsx
import type { Application } from "../types";
import { StatusBadge } from "./StatusBadge";
import { daysSince } from "../../../shared/lib/dates";

type Props = { app: Application; onSelect?: (id: string) => void };

export function ApplicationCard({ app, onSelect }: Props) {
  return (
    <article className="card" onClick={() => onSelect?.(app.id)}>
      <h3>{app.role}</h3>
      <p>{app.company}{app.location && ` · ${app.location}`}</p>
      <StatusBadge status={app.status} />
      <small>Added {daysSince(app.createdAt)} days ago</small>
    </article>
  );
}
      tsx
      // StatusBadge.tsx — a lookup object instead of a chain of if/else
const LABELS: Record<Status, string> = {
  wishlist: "Wishlist", applied: "Applied", interview: "Interview", offer: "Offer", rejected: "Rejected",
};
export function StatusBadge({ status }: { status: Status }) {
  return <span className={`badge badge-${status}`}>{LABELS[status]}</span>;
}

      Pure helpers and modules (Functions, Modules)

      ts
      // src/shared/lib/dates.ts
const DAY_MS = 86_400_000;
export function daysSince(iso: string, now: Date = new Date()): number {
  return Math.floor((now.getTime() - new Date(iso).getTime()) / DAY_MS);
}

      Passing now as a parameter (with a default) keeps the function pure and testable: the same input always gives the same output.

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      function Card({ app }: Props)defineProps<{ app: Application }>()
      {app.role}{{ app.role }}
      className, onClick={…}class, @click="…"
      {cond && <X />}<X v-if="cond" />

      Tests

      ts
      // src/test/factories.ts — build test data quickly
import type { Application } from "../features/applications/types";
export function makeApp(overrides: Partial<Application> = {}): Application {
  const now = "2026-09-01T10:00:00.000Z";
  return { id: crypto.randomUUID(), company: "Acme", role: "Frontend Engineer", status: "applied",
           tags: [], createdAt: now, updatedAt: now, ...overrides };
}
      ts
      // dates.test.ts
import { daysSince } from "./dates";
it("counts whole days between two dates", () => {
  expect(daysSince("2026-09-01T00:00:00Z", new Date("2026-09-11T12:00:00Z"))).toBe(10);
});
      tsx
      // ApplicationCard.test.tsx
import { render, screen } from "@testing-library/react";
import userEvent from "@testing-library/user-event";
import { ApplicationCard } from "./ApplicationCard";
import { makeApp } from "../../../test/factories";

it("shows the role, company and status", () => {
  render(<ApplicationCard app={makeApp({ role: "UI Engineer", company: "Razorpay", status: "interview" })} />);
  expect(screen.getByRole("heading", { name: "UI Engineer" })).toBeInTheDocument();
  expect(screen.getByText("Razorpay")).toBeInTheDocument();
  expect(screen.getByText("Interview")).toBeInTheDocument();
});
it("calls onSelect with the id when clicked", async () => {
  const onSelect = vi.fn();
  const app = makeApp({ id: "a1" });
  render(<ApplicationCard app={app} onSelect={onSelect} />);
  await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole("article"));
  expect(onSelect).toHaveBeenCalledWith("a1");
});

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      type vs interface?

      Both describe object shapes; type also handles unions, tuples and mapped types, interface can be extended and merged. Pick one convention. See TypeScript.

      What's the difference between props and state?

      Props are inputs passed by the parent and read-only; state is data the component owns and changes over time.

      What is a pure function and why do reducers and components need to be pure?

      Same input → same output, no side effects. React may call components more than once and relies on purity to skip work and stay predictable.

      Done when

      Cards render from sample data, npm run test:run passes, npm run typecheck is clean, and it's committed.

      M2 · Add and list applications

      Build

      • An "Add application" form (company, role, optional link) with an inline error for empty fields.
      • A list of applications, newest first, with a live text filter.
      • A count: "12 applications · 3 shown".

      Concepts: why and syntax

      useState and immutable updates (State with useState)

      Why: React re-renders when state changes, and it detects change by reference. Mutating the array (push) keeps the same reference, so React may not update. Always create a new array/object.

      tsx
      const [apps, setApps] = useState<Application[]>([]);

function addApp(input: NewApplication) {
  const now = new Date().toISOString();
  const app: Application = { id: crypto.randomUUID(), status: "wishlist", tags: [], createdAt: now, updatedAt: now, ...input };
  setApps(prev => [app, ...prev]);          // updater function: always uses the latest state
}

      Controlled form, events, computed keys (Forms, Handling events)

      tsx
      const EMPTY = { company: "", role: "", url: "" };

export function AddApplicationForm({ onAdd }: { onAdd: (input: NewApplication) => void }) {
  const [form, setForm] = useState(EMPTY);
  const [error, setError] = useState("");

  function handleChange(e: React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>) {
    const { name, value } = e.target;                 // destructuring
    setForm(prev => ({ ...prev, [name]: value }));    // spread + computed key: one handler for all inputs
  }

  function handleSubmit(e: React.FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>) {
    e.preventDefault();                               // stop the page reload
    const company = form.company.trim(), role = form.role.trim();
    if (!company || !role) return setError("Company and role are required");   // early return
    onAdd({ company, role, url: form.url.trim() || undefined });
    setForm(EMPTY);
    setError("");
  }

  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit} aria-label="Add application" noValidate>
      <label htmlFor="company">Company</label>
      <input id="company" name="company" value={form.company} onChange={handleChange} />
      <label htmlFor="role">Role</label>
      <input id="role" name="role" value={form.role} onChange={handleChange} />
      <label htmlFor="url">Job link (optional)</label>
      <input id="url" name="url" type="url" value={form.url} onChange={handleChange} />
      {error && <p role="alert">{error}</p>}
      <button type="submit">Add application</button>
    </form>
  );
}

      Lists, keys and derived data (Rendering lists, Arrays)

      tsx
      const [query, setQuery] = useState("");

const visible = q                                          // derived: computed during render, NOT stored in state
  ? apps.filter(a => `${a.company} ${a.role}`.toLowerCase().includes(q))
  : apps;

<p>{apps.length} applications · {visible.length} shown</p>

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      Why must state be updated immutably in React?

      React compares references to decide whether something changed. Mutating keeps the same reference, so updates can be skipped and memoisation breaks.

      Why not use the array index as a key?

      When items are inserted, removed or reordered, indexes shift, so React attaches state and DOM to the wrong item. Use a stable id.

      setCount(count + 1) vs setCount(c => c + 1)?

      The first uses the value from this render's snapshot; the updater always receives the latest queued value, so multiple updates in one event all apply.

      Controlled vs uncontrolled inputs?

      Controlled: React state is the source of truth (value + onChange). Uncontrolled: the DOM keeps the value, read via ref or FormData.

      Why compute the filtered list instead of storing it in state?

      Storing derived data duplicates the source of truth and can go out of sync. Compute it during render (memoise only if it's expensive).

      Done when

      You can add, see and filter applications; the empty state shows helpful text; three tests pass.

      M3 · The board: move, edit, delete, undo

      Build

      • Five columns, one per status, each showing its applications and a count.
      • Move cards by drag and drop and by a status <select> on each card (the keyboard-accessible way).
      • Delete with a 5-second "Deleted · Undo" toast.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      useReducer and pure reducers (useReducer)

      Why: the list now changes in five different ways. A reducer puts every change in one pure, testable function; components just dispatch what happened. It's the same pattern as Redux, and close to Pinia actions.

      ts
      // src/features/applications/reducer.ts
export type Action =
  | { type: "added"; app: Application }
  | { type: "updated"; id: string; changes: Partial<Application>; at: string }
  | { type: "moved"; id: string; status: Status; at: string }
  | { type: "deleted"; id: string }
  | { type: "restored"; app: Application; index: number };

export function applicationsReducer(state: Application[], action: Action): Application[] {
  switch (action.type) {
    case "added":
      return [action.app, ...state];
    case "updated":
      return state.map(a => (a.id === action.id ? { ...a, ...action.changes, updatedAt: action.at } : a));
    case "moved":
      return state.map(a => (a.id === action.id ? { ...a, status: action.status, updatedAt: action.at } : a));
    case "deleted":
      return state.filter(a => a.id !== action.id);
    case "restored":
      return [...state.slice(0, action.index), action.app, ...state.slice(action.index)];
    default: {
      const _exhaustive: never = action;   // compile error if you add an action and forget a case
      return state;
    }
  }
}

      Notice at comes in the action: calling new Date() inside the reducer would make it impure (different output for the same input) and harder to test.

      Grouping with reduce (Arrays)

      ts
      const columns = STATUSES.reduce((acc, s) => {
  acc[s] = apps.filter(a => a.status === s);
  return acc;
}, {} as Record<Status, Application[]>);

      Native drag and drop + an accessible alternative (Events, Accessibility)

      tsx
      // Card: make it draggable and carry the id
<article draggable onDragStart={e => e.dataTransfer.setData("text/plain", app.id)}>
  …
  <label>
    Status
    <select value={app.status} onChange={e => onMove(app.id, e.target.value as Status)}>
      {STATUSES.map(s => <option key={s} value={s}>{LABELS[s]}</option>)}
    </select>
  </label>
</article>

// Column: accept drops. dragover must preventDefault or drop never fires.
<section aria-labelledby={`col-${status}`}
         onDragOver={e => e.preventDefault()}
         onDrop={e => onMove(e.dataTransfer.getData("text/plain"), status)}>
  <h2 id={`col-${status}`}>{LABELS[status]} ({items.length})</h2>
  {items.map(app => <ApplicationCard key={app.id} app={app} onMove={onMove} />)}
</section>

      Drag and drop alone excludes keyboard and screen-reader users; the <select> gives everyone a way to move cards. (Libraries like dnd-kit add keyboard dragging if you want to go further.)

      Closures, timers and refs for undo (Closures, Timers, useRef)

      Why a ref: the timer id must survive re-renders but never appear on screen, so it doesn't belong in state.

      tsx
      const [lastDeleted, setLastDeleted] = useState<{ app: Application; index: number } | null>(null);
const timerRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | undefined>(undefined);

function remove(app: Application) {
  const index = apps.findIndex(a => a.id === app.id);
  dispatch({ type: "deleted", id: app.id });
  setLastDeleted({ app, index });
  clearTimeout(timerRef.current);                                    // restart the countdown
  timerRef.current = setTimeout(() => setLastDeleted(null), 5000);   // closure remembers setLastDeleted
}
function undo() {
  if (!lastDeleted) return;
  dispatch({ type: "restored", ...lastDeleted });
  setLastDeleted(null);
  clearTimeout(timerRef.current);
}
useEffect(() => () => clearTimeout(timerRef.current), []);           // clean up on unmount

{lastDeleted && (
  <div role="status">Deleted {lastDeleted.app.role} at {lastDeleted.app.company}. <button onClick={undo}>Undo</button></div>
)}

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      useReducer + dispatchPinia actions (store.move(id, status)) or a composable
      useRef for the timer ida plain let in <script setup> (setup runs once)
      useEffect cleanuponUnmounted(() => clearTimeout(t))
      onDragOver={e => e.preventDefault()}@dragover.prevent

      Tests

      ts
      // reducer.test.ts — write these FIRST (TDD), they're the easiest tests you'll ever write
const AT = "2026-09-21T10:00:00.000Z";
it("moves an application without mutating the original", () => {
  const a = makeApp({ id: "1", status: "applied" });
  const state = [a];
  const next = applicationsReducer(state, { type: "moved", id: "1", status: "interview", at: AT });
  expect(next[0]).toMatchObject({ status: "interview", updatedAt: AT });
  expect(a.status).toBe("applied");          // original object untouched
  expect(next).not.toBe(state);              // new array reference
});
it("restores a deleted item at its original position", () => {
  const [a, b, c] = ["1", "2", "3"].map(id => makeApp({ id }));
  const afterDelete = applicationsReducer([a, b, c], { type: "deleted", id: "2" });
  const restored = applicationsReducer(afterDelete, { type: "restored", app: b, index: 1 });
  expect(restored.map(x => x.id)).toEqual(["1", "2", "3"]);
});
      tsx
      // Board.test.tsx — fake timers for the undo countdown

  const user = userEvent.setup({ advanceTimers: vi.advanceTimersByTime });

      Tip: screen.getByRole("region", { name: /interview/i }) finds a column because each <section> is labelled by its heading. Accessible markup makes tests easy.

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      When would you use useReducer over useState?

      When state has several related update types or the next state depends on the previous in complex ways. The logic becomes one pure, testable function.

      What's a closure? Give an example from your code.

      A function that keeps access to variables from where it was created. The undo timer's callback closes over setLastDeleted and runs 5 seconds later, after remove has returned.

      State vs ref?

      State triggers a re-render and is shown on screen; a ref holds a mutable value across renders without re-rendering (timer ids, DOM nodes, previous values).

      Why do you need to call preventDefault in dragover?

      By default, elements don't accept drops; cancelling dragover marks the element as a valid drop target so drop fires.

      Undo or confirmation dialog for delete?

      Undo for frequent, reversible actions (less friction); confirmation for rare, irreversible ones. See Designing every state.

      Done when

      Cards move by drag and by keyboard, delete can be undone, reducer tests cover every action.

      M4 · Persistence: localStorage, sessionStorage, cookies

      Build

      • Applications survive a refresh (localStorage).
      • A half-typed "Add application" form survives a refresh in the same tab, but not a new tab (sessionStorage).
      • Two open tabs stay in sync.
      • Corrupted or old-format saved data doesn't crash the app.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Which storage, and why (Storage)

      DataStorageReason
      ApplicationslocalStorageMust survive restarts; only the browser needs it (until phase 2 moves it to a database)
      Unsent form draftsessionStorageUseful after an accidental refresh, but shouldn't reappear days later in another tab
      Theme (M5)cookieIn phase 2, the server can read it and render the right theme with no flash

      Loading with useReducer's initialiser and saving with useEffect (useEffect)

      ts
      // src/features/applications/storage.ts
import { z } from "zod";
const KEY = "jobpilot:applications:v1";           // version in the key → safe format changes later

const ApplicationSchema = z.object({
  id: z.string(), company: z.string(), role: z.string(),
  status: z.enum(STATUSES), url: z.string().optional(), location: z.string().optional(),
  followUpOn: z.string().optional(), notes: z.string().optional(),
  tags: z.array(z.string()), createdAt: z.string(), updatedAt: z.string(),
});

export function loadApps(): Application[] {
  try {
    const raw = localStorage.getItem(KEY);
    return raw ? z.array(ApplicationSchema).parse(JSON.parse(raw)) : [];   // string → object → validated
  } catch {
    return [];                                    // bad JSON or wrong shape: start fresh, don't crash
  }
}
export function saveApps(apps: Application[]) {
  try { localStorage.setItem(KEY, JSON.stringify(apps)); }    // object → string
  catch { /* storage full or blocked (private mode): the app keeps working in memory */ }
}
      tsx
      const [apps, dispatch] = useReducer(applicationsReducer, [], () => loadApps());  // runs once
useEffect(() => { saveApps(apps); }, [apps]);                                     // after every change

      Why validate stored data? localStorage is outside your control: users edit it, old versions of your app wrote a different shape, extensions interfere. JSON.parse alone gives you any; Zod checks it at runtime and gives you a real type.

      A reusable custom hook for any storage (Custom hooks)

      ts
      // src/shared/hooks/useStoredState.ts
export function useStoredState<T>(key: string, initial: T, storage: Storage = localStorage) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState<T>(() => {
    try {
      const raw = storage.getItem(key);
      return raw !== null ? (JSON.parse(raw) as T) : initial;
    } catch {
      return initial;
    }
  });
  useEffect(() => {
    try { storage.setItem(key, JSON.stringify(value)); } catch { /* ignore */ }
  }, [key, value, storage]);
  return [value, setValue] as const;               // as const → a typed tuple, like useState
}

// the form draft: same hook, different storage
const [form, setForm] = useStoredState("jobpilot:draft", EMPTY, sessionStorage);

      Syncing tabs with the storage event

      tsx
      useEffect(() => {
  function onStorage(e: StorageEvent) {
    if (e.key === "jobpilot:applications:v1") dispatch({ type: "replacedAll", apps: loadApps() });
  }
  window.addEventListener("storage", onStorage);       // fires in OTHER tabs, not the one that wrote
  return () => window.removeEventListener("storage", onStorage);
}, []);

      Add a replacedAll action to the reducer (and its test). The cleanup function prevents duplicate listeners: in development, Strict Mode runs effects twice precisely to expose a missing cleanup.

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      useEffect(() => save(apps), [apps])watch(apps, save, { deep: true })
      custom hook useStoredStatecomposable, or VueUse's useLocalStorage / useSessionStorage
      effect cleanuponUnmounted / onScopeDispose

      Tests

      ts

  expect(loadApps()).toEqual([]);
});
it("ignores data with the wrong shape", () => {
  localStorage.setItem("jobpilot:applications:v1", JSON.stringify([{ id: 1 }]));
  expect(loadApps()).toEqual([]);
});
      tsx
      // useStoredState.test.ts — testing a hook directly
import { renderHook, act } from "@testing-library/react";
it("reads and writes sessionStorage", () => {
  const { result } = renderHook(() => useStoredState("k", 1, sessionStorage));
  act(() => result.current[1](2));
  expect(sessionStorage.getItem("k")).toBe("2");
});

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      localStorage vs sessionStorage vs cookies?

      localStorage persists until cleared; sessionStorage lasts for the tab; cookies have an expiry, are small (~4 KB) and are sent to the server with every request. See Storage.

      Why do you need JSON.stringify for localStorage?

      Web Storage only stores strings. Without it an object becomes "[object Object]".

      Should you store an auth token in localStorage?

      Generally no: any XSS can read it. Prefer HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookies set by the server.

      Why does my effect run twice in development?

      Strict Mode mounts, unmounts and remounts to reveal missing cleanups. It doesn't happen in production.

      What's a custom hook and what rules apply?

      A function starting with use that calls other hooks to reuse stateful logic; call hooks only at the top level of components/hooks. Each caller gets its own state.

      Done when

      Refresh keeps your data, the draft survives refresh but not a new tab, two tabs sync, and corrupted storage is handled and tested.

      M5 · Global state and theme with Context

      Build

      • Move applications state out of App into an ApplicationsProvider so any page can use it (routing comes next).
      • A light/dark theme toggle that remembers the choice in a cookie and respects the OS setting by default.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Context + reducer = a small store (Context)

      Why: the board, the detail page and the stats page all need applications. Passing them through every component in between ("prop drilling") gets messy. Context makes a value available to a whole subtree.

      tsx
      // src/features/applications/ApplicationsProvider.tsx
type Ctx = { apps: Application[]; dispatch: React.Dispatch<Action> };
const ApplicationsContext = createContext<Ctx | null>(null);

export function ApplicationsProvider({ children, initial }: { children: React.ReactNode; initial?: Application[] }) {
  const [apps, dispatch] = useReducer(applicationsReducer, [], () => initial ?? loadApps());
  useEffect(() => { saveApps(apps); }, [apps]);
  return <ApplicationsContext value={{ apps, dispatch }}>{children}</ApplicationsContext>;  // React 19 syntax
}

export function useApplications() {
  const ctx = useContext(ApplicationsContext);
  if (!ctx) throw new Error("useApplications must be used inside ApplicationsProvider");
  return ctx;
}

// small, intention-revealing helpers instead of dispatching raw actions everywhere
export function useApplicationActions() {
  const { dispatch } = useApplications();
  return {
    move: (id: string, status: Status) => dispatch({ type: "moved", id, status, at: new Date().toISOString() }),
    remove: (id: string) => dispatch({ type: "deleted", id }),
  };
}

      Theme: cookie, matchMedia, CSS variables (Modern CSS)

      ts
      function readCookie(name: string) {
  return document.cookie.split("; ").find(c => c.startsWith(name + "="))?.split("=")[1];
}
function writeCookie(name: string, value: string) {
  document.cookie = `${name}=${value}; max-age=31536000; path=/; SameSite=Lax`;
}
const systemPrefersDark = window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches;
const initialTheme = (readCookie("theme") as "light" | "dark" | undefined) ?? (systemPrefersDark ? "dark" : "light");

// in ThemeProvider: state + effect
useEffect(() => {
  document.documentElement.dataset.theme = theme;   // CSS: :root[data-theme="dark"] { --bg: … }
  writeCookie("theme", theme);
}, [theme]);

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      createContext + provider + useContextprovide / inject, or a Pinia store
      custom hook useApplications()useApplicationsStore() (Pinia)
      every consumer re-renders when the context value changesonly components reading the changed property update

      When to reach for Zustand/Redux instead: when many components need different slices of frequently changing state. Context re-renders every consumer on each change. Try refactoring this provider to a Zustand store later and compare; it's a great interview talking point. See State libraries.

      Tests

      ts
      // src/test/setup.ts — jsdom doesn't implement matchMedia; add a stub once for all tests
Object.defineProperty(window, "matchMedia", {
  writable: true,
  value: (query: string) => ({
    matches: false, media: query, onchange: null,
    addEventListener: () => {}, removeEventListener: () => {},
    addListener: () => {}, removeListener: () => {}, dispatchEvent: () => false,
  }),
});
      tsx
      // src/test/renderWithProviders.tsx — every future test uses this
export function renderWithProviders(ui: React.ReactElement, { apps = [] as Application[] } = {}) {
  return render(<ThemeProvider><ApplicationsProvider initial={apps}>{ui}</ApplicationsProvider></ThemeProvider>);
}

it("throws a helpful error outside the provider", () => {
  vi.spyOn(console, "error").mockImplementation(() => {});     // silence React's error log
  expect(() => renderHook(() => useApplications())).toThrow(/inside ApplicationsProvider/);
});
it("toggles and remembers the theme", async () => {
  renderWithProviders(<ThemeToggle />);
  await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: /dark mode/i }));
  expect(document.documentElement.dataset.theme).toBe("dark");
  expect(document.cookie).toContain("theme=dark");
});

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      What is prop drilling and how do you avoid it?

      Passing props through components that don't use them. Fix with composition (children), context, or a store.

      What are the performance downsides of Context?

      All consumers re-render when the value changes. Split contexts (state vs actions), memoise the value, or use a store with selectors for fast-changing shared state.

      Context vs Redux/Zustand vs TanStack Query?

      Context passes values down; Zustand/Redux manage client state with selective subscriptions; TanStack Query manages server state (caching, refetching). See Architecture decisions.

      Done when

      No component receives applications through props it doesn't use; theme persists and follows the OS by default; all earlier tests still pass using renderWithProviders.

      M6 · Routing and URL state

      Build

      • Routes: / (board), /applications/:id (detail and notes), /stats (lazy-loaded), /jobs (search, next milestone), and a 404 page.
      • Board filters stored in the URL: /?q=react&status=interview, so a filtered view survives refresh and can be bookmarked.
      • An error page for crashes inside a route.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Client-side routing (React Router)

      Why: one HTML page, many URLs. The router swaps components without a full page reload, while back/forward, refresh and shared links still work.

      bash
      npm install react-router
      tsx
      // src/app/routes.tsx
import { createBrowserRouter } from "react-router";
export const routes = [
  {
    path: "/",
    element: <Layout />,                 // header + nav + <Outlet />
    errorElement: <RouteError />,        // rendered if any child throws while rendering
    children: [
      { index: true, element: <BoardPage /> },
      { path: "applications/:id", element: <ApplicationDetail /> },
      { path: "jobs", element: <JobsPage /> },
      { path: "stats", lazy: async () => ({ Component: (await import("../features/stats/StatsPage")).default }) },
      { path: "*", element: <NotFound /> },
    ],
  },
];
export const router = createBrowserRouter(routes);

// main.tsx
createRoot(document.getElementById("root")!).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <ThemeProvider><ApplicationsProvider><RouterProvider router={router} /></ApplicationsProvider></ThemeProvider>
  </StrictMode>
);
      tsx
      // Layout.tsx
export function Layout() {
  return (
    <>
      <nav aria-label="Main">
        <NavLink to="/" end>Board</NavLink>
        <NavLink to="/jobs">Find jobs</NavLink>
        <NavLink to="/stats">Stats</NavLink>
      </nav>
      <main><Outlet /></main>         {/* child route renders here */}
    </>
  );
}

      Dynamic segments and navigation

      tsx
      export function ApplicationDetail() {
  const { id } = useParams();                          // "/applications/abc" → id = "abc"
  const { apps } = useApplications();
  const navigate = useNavigate();
  const app = apps.find(a => a.id === id);
  if (!app) return <NotFound message="This application doesn't exist or was deleted." />;
  return (
    <article>
      <button onClick={() => navigate(-1)}>Back</button>
      <h1>{app.role} at {app.company}</h1>
      …
    </article>
  );
}
// Card links to it: <Link to={`/applications/${app.id}`}>{app.role}</Link>

      URL search params as state (Types of state)

      Why: filters are "where I am" information. In the URL they survive refresh, work with back/forward and can be shared. In useState they vanish.

      tsx
      const [params, setParams] = useSearchParams();
const q = params.get("q") ?? "";
const status = params.get("status") as Status | null;

function updateParam(key: string, value: string) {
  setParams(prev => {
    const next = new URLSearchParams(prev);
    value ? next.set(key, value) : next.delete(key);
    return next;
  }, { replace: true });                   // replace: typing shouldn't create 20 history entries
}
<input type="search" value={q} onChange={e => updateParam("q", e.target.value)} aria-label="Filter applications" />

      Lazy loading and Suspense (Suspense)

      Why: the stats page will include a chart library. Loading it only when someone visits /stats keeps the first load small. The lazy route option (or React.lazy + <Suspense>) splits it into a separate file.

      In Vue you'd write

      React RouterVue Router
      <Outlet /><RouterView />
      <Link to> / <NavLink><RouterLink to> (active class built in)
      useParams(), useSearchParams()useRoute().params, useRoute().query
      useNavigate()useRouter().push()
      lazy: () => import(…)component: () => import(…)
      errorElementonErrorCaptured / router error handling

      Tests

      tsx
      // test/renderRoute.tsx — render any URL with the real route table
import { createMemoryRouter, RouterProvider } from "react-router";
export function renderRoute(path: string, apps: Application[] = []) {
  const router = createMemoryRouter(routes, { initialEntries: [path] });
  renderWithProviders(<RouterProvider router={router} />, { apps });
  return router;
}

it("shows the detail page for an application", async () => {
  renderRoute("/applications/a1", [makeApp({ id: "a1", role: "UI Engineer", company: "Swiggy" })]);
  expect(await screen.findByRole("heading", { name: "UI Engineer at Swiggy" })).toBeInTheDocument();
});
it("shows not-found for an unknown id", async () => {
  renderRoute("/applications/nope");
  expect(await screen.findByText(/doesn't exist/i)).toBeInTheDocument();
});
it("reads filters from the URL", async () => {
  renderRoute("/?status=interview", [makeApp({ status: "interview", role: "A" }), makeApp({ status: "applied", role: "B" })]);
  expect(await screen.findByRole("heading", { name: "A" })).toBeInTheDocument();
  expect(screen.queryByRole("heading", { name: "B" })).not.toBeInTheDocument();
});

      Deploying an SPA: refreshing /stats on a static host returns 404 unless the host serves index.html for every path. You'll configure this in M10.

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      How does client-side routing work?

      The router listens to the History API (pushState, popstate), matches the URL to a route, and renders the matching components without requesting a new page from the server.

      Why put filters in the URL instead of component state?

      Shareable, bookmarkable, survives refresh, and works with back/forward. It's state the user considers "where I am".

      What does code splitting do and how do you do it in React?

      Splits the bundle so code loads when needed: React.lazy(() => import()) with <Suspense>, or the router's lazy option.

      What does an error boundary catch, and what doesn't it?

      Errors thrown while rendering its children. Not errors in event handlers, async code or the boundary itself; handle those with try/catch and state.

      Done when

      All routes work with refresh and back/forward, filters live in the URL, the stats chunk loads separately (check the Network tab), route tests pass.

      M7 · Job search from an API

      Build

      • A /jobs page: search remote frontend jobs as you type, with loading, empty and error states.
      • A "Save to tracker" button that adds a job to your Wishlist (disabled if already saved).
      • A mock API (MSW) for development and tests, and a dev proxy to a real public API.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Promises, async/await and custom error classes (Promises, async/await, Classes)

      ts
      // src/features/jobs/api.ts
import { z } from "zod";

export class ApiError extends Error {          // a class extending Error: prototypes in practice
  status: number;
  constructor(status: number, message: string) {
    super(message);                            // call the parent constructor first
    this.name = "ApiError";
    this.status = status;
  }
}

const JobSchema = z.object({
  id: z.number(), title: z.string(), company_name: z.string(),
  url: z.string(), candidate_required_location: z.string(), publication_date: z.string(),
});
const JobsResponse = z.object({ jobs: z.array(JobSchema) });
export type Job = z.infer<typeof JobSchema>;

export async function searchJobs(query: string, signal?: AbortSignal): Promise<Job[]> {
  const res = await fetch(`/api/jobs?search=${encodeURIComponent(query)}&limit=20`, { signal });
  if (!res.ok) throw new ApiError(res.status, `Job search failed (${res.status})`);  // fetch doesn't throw on 404/500!
  return JobsResponse.parse(await res.json()).jobs;
}

      Debounce with a closure, as a hook (Debounce, Closures)

      Why: firing a request on every keystroke wastes requests and can show results out of order. Wait until the user pauses typing.

      ts
      // src/shared/hooks/useDebouncedValue.ts
export function useDebouncedValue<T>(value: T, delay = 300): T {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const t = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(t);                 // a new keystroke cancels the previous timer
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

      Fetching in an effect, with cancellation and all four states (useEffect, States)

      ts
      type SearchState =
  | { status: "idle" }
  | { status: "loading" }
  | { status: "success"; jobs: Job[] }
  | { status: "error"; message: string };        // discriminated union: impossible states can't exist

export function useJobSearch(query: string): SearchState {
  const [state, setState] = useState<SearchState>({ status: "idle" });
  useEffect(() => {
    if (query.trim().length < 2) { setState({ status: "idle" }); return; }
    const controller = new AbortController();
    setState({ status: "loading" });
    searchJobs(query, controller.signal)
      .then(jobs => setState({ status: "success", jobs }))
      .catch((err: unknown) => {
        if (err instanceof DOMException && err.name === "AbortError") return;   // we cancelled it: ignore
        setState({ status: "error", message: err instanceof ApiError ? err.message : "Check your connection and try again." });
      });
    return () => controller.abort();              // query changed or page left: cancel the stale request
  }, [query]);
  return state;
}

// JobsPage.tsx
const [input, setInput] = useState("");
const query = useDebouncedValue(input, 350);
const result = useJobSearch(query);

      The event loop in action: typing updates input synchronously; the debounce timer is a macrotask; fetch runs in the browser's network layer; .then callbacks run as microtasks when the response arrives. Explain this flow out loud: it's the event loop question with a real example.

      MSW: a fake API for development and tests (MSW)

      ts
      // src/mocks/handlers.ts
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from "msw";
export const jobs = [
  { id: 1, title: "Frontend Engineer (React)", company_name: "Acme", url: "https://example.com/1",
    candidate_required_location: "India", publication_date: "2026-09-10T00:00:00" },

    candidate_required_location: "Worldwide", publication_date: "2026-09-12T00:00:00" },

export const handlers = [
  http.get("/api/jobs", async ({ request }) => {
    const q = (new URL(request.url).searchParams.get("search") ?? "").toLowerCase();
    await delay(300);
    return HttpResponse.json({ jobs: jobs.filter(j => j.title.toLowerCase().includes(q)) });
  }),

      ts
      // src/mocks/server.ts (tests)

      Real data through a dev proxy, and why CORS matters (CORS)

      ts
      // vite.config.ts → server.proxy: the browser calls /api/jobs (same origin),
// Vite's dev server forwards it to the real API, so no CORS error.
server: {
  proxy: {
    "/api/jobs": {
      target: "https://remotive.com",
      changeOrigin: true,
      rewrite: path => path.replace(/^\/api\/jobs/, "/api/remote-jobs"),
    },
  },
},

      Public APIs change or rate-limit; if this one is unavailable, keep using MSW. In phase 2, a Next.js route handler replaces the proxy in production.

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      useEffect + cleanup that abortswatch(query, …) + onWatcherCleanup(() => ctrl.abort())
      useDebouncedValue hookVueUse refDebounced / useDebounceFn
      TanStack Query useQueryTanStack Vue Query, Pinia Colada, or Nuxt useFetch

      Refactor exercise: after it works, replace useJobSearch with TanStack Query: useQuery({ queryKey: ["jobs", query], queryFn: ({ signal }) => searchJobs(query, signal), enabled: query.length > 1 }). You get caching, deduping and retries for free, and a great answer to "how do you manage server state?".

      Tests

      tsx
      it("shows results for a search", async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  renderRoute("/jobs");

  expect(await screen.findByText(/searching/i)).toBeInTheDocument();   // appears after the debounce
  expect(await screen.findByRole("heading", { name: "Vue.js Developer" })).toBeInTheDocument();
});
it("shows an error state when the API fails", async () => {
  server.use(http.get("/api/jobs", () => HttpResponse.json(null, { status: 500 })));
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  renderRoute("/jobs");
  await user.type(await screen.findByRole("searchbox", { name: /search jobs/i }), "react");
  expect(await screen.findByText(/job search failed \(500\)/i)).toBeInTheDocument();
});

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      Why doesn't fetch throw on a 404?

      It only rejects on network failure; HTTP errors are valid responses. Check res.ok/res.status and throw yourself.

      How do you prevent race conditions when fetching in an effect?

      Abort the previous request in the cleanup (AbortController) or ignore stale responses with a flag; or use a data library that handles it.

      Implement debounce. How is it different from throttle?

      Debounce runs after activity stops; throttle runs at most once per interval. See Write it yourself.

      What is CORS, and how did you avoid it in development?

      The browser blocks reading cross-origin responses unless the server allows it. A dev proxy makes the request same-origin from the browser's view; in production a backend route does the same.

      Walk me through what happens between typing and seeing results.

      Input state updates and re-renders; after 350 ms of no typing the debounced value updates; the effect aborts any previous request and calls fetch; when the response resolves, the promise callback (microtask) sets state; React re-renders with results.

      Done when

      Typing searches after a pause, stale requests are cancelled (check the Network tab: cancelled requests appear), all four states have tests, saving jobs works.

      M8 · Modal, validated forms and accessibility

      Build

      • An "Edit application" modal using the native <dialog> element.
      • A full edit form (company, role, link, location, follow-up date, notes, tags) with React Hook Form and Zod validation.
      • Keyboard support everywhere: focus visible, Escape closes, focus returns to the button that opened the modal.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Refs to DOM elements and <dialog> (useRef, Accessibility)

      Why <dialog>: showModal() gives you a focus trap, Escape to close, the top layer (no z-index fights) and an inert background, all built into the browser.

      tsx
      // src/shared/ui/Modal.tsx
export function Modal({ open, onClose, title, children }: {
  open: boolean; onClose: () => void; title: string; children: React.ReactNode;
}) {
  const ref = useRef<HTMLDialogElement>(null);
  const titleId = useId();                               // unique id for aria-labelledby

  useEffect(() => {                                      // sync React state → imperative DOM API
    const dialog = ref.current;
    if (!dialog) return;
    if (open && !dialog.open) dialog.showModal();
    if (!open && dialog.open) dialog.close();
  }, [open]);

  return (
    <dialog ref={ref} onClose={onClose} aria-labelledby={titleId}>
      <h2 id={titleId}>{title}</h2>
      {children}
      <button type="button" onClick={onClose}>Close</button>
    </dialog>
  );
}

      React Hook Form + Zod (Form UX)

      bash
      npm install react-hook-form @hookform/resolvers
      tsx
      const schema = z.object({
  company: z.string().trim().min(1, "Company is required"),
  role: z.string().trim().min(1, "Role is required"),
  url: z.union([z.literal(""), z.url("Enter a full link, starting with https://")]),
  location: z.string().trim(),
  followUpOn: z.string(),                                     // "" or "2026-10-01"
  notes: z.string().max(2000, "Keep notes under 2,000 characters"),
});
type FormValues = z.infer<typeof schema>;

export function EditApplicationForm({ app, onSave }: { app: Application; onSave: (v: FormValues) => void }) {
  const { register, handleSubmit, formState: { errors, isSubmitting } } = useForm<FormValues>({
    resolver: zodResolver(schema),
    mode: "onBlur",                                           // validate when leaving a field
    defaultValues: { company: app.company, role: app.role, url: app.url ?? "", location: app.location ?? "",
                     followUpOn: app.followUpOn ?? "", notes: app.notes ?? "" },
  });
  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit(onSave)} noValidate>
      <label htmlFor="edit-company">Company</label>
      <input id="edit-company" {...register("company")}
             aria-invalid={!!errors.company} aria-describedby={errors.company ? "edit-company-err" : undefined} />
      {errors.company && <p id="edit-company-err" className="error">{errors.company.message}</p>}
      {/* …same pattern for the other fields… */}
      <button type="submit" disabled={isSubmitting}>Save changes</button>
    </form>
  );
}

      Why a form library here: the M2 hand-written form was fine for three fields. For many fields with rules, RHF avoids re-rendering on every keystroke (uncontrolled inputs under the hood), and Zod keeps validation rules in one schema you'll reuse on the server in phase 2.

      Prototypes, for real: polyfilling in tests (Prototypes)

      jsdom doesn't implement showModal. You add it to the prototype, so every dialog element inherits it. This is exactly how polyfills work.

      ts
      // src/test/setup.ts
HTMLDialogElement.prototype.showModal ??= function (this: HTMLDialogElement) { this.open = true; };
HTMLDialogElement.prototype.close ??= function (this: HTMLDialogElement) {
  this.open = false;
  this.dispatchEvent(new Event("close"));
};

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      useRef<HTMLDialogElement>(null) + ref={ref}useTemplateRef("dialog") + ref="dialog"
      useId()useId() (Vue 3.5)
      React Hook Form + ZodVeeValidate + Zod, or FormKit
      createPortal<Teleport to="body">

      Tests

      tsx
      it("validates the job link on blur", async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  render(<EditApplicationForm app={makeApp()} onSave={vi.fn()} />);
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText("Job link"), "not a link");
  await user.tab();                                                    // blur
  expect(await screen.findByText(/full link/i)).toBeInTheDocument();
  expect(screen.getByLabelText("Job link")).toHaveAttribute("aria-invalid", "true");
});
it("saves valid changes", async () => {
  const onSave = vi.fn();
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  render(<EditApplicationForm app={makeApp({ role: "UI Dev" })} onSave={onSave} />);
  await user.clear(screen.getByLabelText("Role"));
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText("Role"), "Frontend Engineer");
  await user.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: "Save changes" }));
  await waitFor(() => expect(onSave).toHaveBeenCalledWith(expect.objectContaining({ role: "Frontend Engineer" }), expect.anything()));
});
it("opens and closes the modal", async () => { /* click Edit → dialog visible with title; click Close → not visible */ });

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      How do you make a modal accessible?

      Label it (aria-labelledby), move focus in, trap it, close on Escape, return focus to the trigger, make the background inert. Native <dialog> with showModal() handles most of this.

      What's a polyfill? Write one.

      Code that adds a missing feature, usually by defining it on a built-in prototype if absent (like the showModal stub). See Write it yourself.

      When do you validate form fields?

      On blur the first time, live after an error is shown, and all fields on submit with focus on the first error. See Form UX.

      Why does React Hook Form re-render less than controlled inputs?

      It registers uncontrolled inputs via refs and reads values from the DOM, subscribing only to the form state you use.

      Done when

      You can complete every flow with the keyboard only, the modal behaves correctly, validation messages are specific, and form tests pass.

      M9 · Dashboard and performance

      Build

      • A /stats page: totals per status, response rate, applications per week (chart), follow-ups due this week.
      • A dev-only "Load 5,000 sample applications" button, then make the board and list fast with that much data.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      Derived data as a pure, tested function (reduce, Dates)

      ts
      // src/features/stats/computeStats.ts
export function computeStats(apps: Application[], now = new Date()) {
  const byStatus = Object.fromEntries(STATUSES.map(s => [s, 0])) as Record<Status, number>;
  for (const a of apps) byStatus[a.status]++;

  const applied = apps.length - byStatus.wishlist;
  const responded = byStatus.interview + byStatus.offer + byStatus.rejected;
  const weekAhead = new Date(now.getTime() + 7 * 86_400_000);

  return {
    total: apps.length,
    byStatus,
    responseRate: applied === 0 ? 0 : Math.round((responded / applied) * 100),   // avoid dividing by zero
    followUpsDue: apps.filter(a => a.followUpOn && new Date(a.followUpOn) <= weekAhead
                                   && a.status !== "rejected" && a.status !== "offer"),
  };
}

      useMemo, memo, useCallback: only after measuring (Performance)

      tsx
      const stats = useMemo(() => computeStats(apps), [apps]);   // recompute only when apps change

export const ApplicationCard = memo(function ApplicationCard({ app, onMove }: Props) { … });
// memo skips re-rendering when props are the same, so onMove must keep a stable identity:
const onMove = useCallback((id: string, status: Status) => move(id, status), [move]);

      The workflow interviewers want to hear: 1) record with the React DevTools Profiler and Chrome's Performance panel (4× CPU throttling); 2) find what's slow (which components re-render, long tasks); 3) fix the biggest cause; 4) measure again. Memoising without measuring is guessing.

      With the React Compiler enabled (babel-plugin-react-compiler in the Vite React plugin), most manual memo/useCallback becomes unnecessary. Build it manually first so you understand what the compiler does, then try enabling it and compare the Profiler.

      Virtualisation for thousands of rows

      bash
      npm install @tanstack/react-virtual
      tsx
      const parentRef = useRef<HTMLDivElement>(null);
const virtualizer = useVirtualizer({
  count: items.length,
  getScrollElement: () => parentRef.current,
  estimateSize: () => 76,          // approximate row height in px
  overscan: 8,
});
return (
  <div ref={parentRef} style={{ height: 600, overflow: "auto" }}>
    <div style={{ height: virtualizer.getTotalSize(), position: "relative" }}>
      {virtualizer.getVirtualItems().map(row => (
        <div key={items[row.index].id}
             style={{ position: "absolute", top: 0, left: 0, width: "100%", transform: `translateY(${row.start}px)` }}>
          <ApplicationRow app={items[row.index]} />
        </div>
      ))}
    </div>
  </div>
);

      Only ~15 rows exist in the DOM at any time instead of 5,000.

      Sample data generator (Functions)

      ts
      // dev-only: npm install -D @faker-js/faker
import { faker } from "@faker-js/faker";
export function sampleApps(n: number): Application[] {
  return Array.from({ length: n }, () => {
    const created = faker.date.recent({ days: 90 }).toISOString();
    return { id: crypto.randomUUID(), company: faker.company.name(), role: faker.person.jobTitle(),
             status: faker.helpers.arrayElement(STATUSES), tags: [], createdAt: created, updatedAt: created };
  });
}

      A chart, lazy-loaded

      Use a chart library (Recharts, Chart.js via react-chartjs-2) on the stats page only. Because /stats is a lazy route (M6), the chart library lands in a separate chunk. Check with npx vite-bundle-visualizer.

      In Vue you'd write

      ReactVue
      useMemocomputed (cached automatically)
      memo + useCallback to skip child rendersusually unnecessary: Vue tracks dependencies per component; v-memo for big lists
      @tanstack/react-virtual@tanstack/vue-virtual, vue-virtual-scroller

      Tests

      ts
      describe("computeStats", () => {
  const NOW = new Date("2026-09-21T00:00:00Z");
  it("returns zeros for no data", () => {
    expect(computeStats([], NOW)).toMatchObject({ total: 0, responseRate: 0 });
  });
  it("calculates the response rate from non-wishlist applications", () => {
    const apps = [
      makeApp({ status: "wishlist" }), makeApp({ status: "applied" }),
      makeApp({ status: "applied" }), makeApp({ status: "interview" }), makeApp({ status: "rejected" }),
    ];
    expect(computeStats(apps, NOW).responseRate).toBe(50);   // 2 responses / 4 applied
  });
  it("lists follow-ups due within 7 days, excluding closed ones", () => {
    const due = makeApp({ followUpOn: "2026-09-25", status: "applied" });
    const later = makeApp({ followUpOn: "2026-10-10", status: "applied" });
    const closed = makeApp({ followUpOn: "2026-09-22", status: "offer" });
    expect(computeStats([due, later, closed], NOW).followUpsDue).toEqual([due]);
  });
});

      Interview questions this milestone prepares

      A list of 5,000 items is slow. What do you do?

      Measure first; virtualise the list; memoise rows with stable props; debounce filtering; move heavy computation out of render (memo or a worker); paginate on the server if possible.

      useMemo vs useCallback vs memo?

      Cache a computed value; cache a function reference; skip re-rendering a component when props are shallow-equal.

      Why do components re-render in React?

      Their state changes, their parent re-renders, or a context they read changes.

      What does the React Compiler change?

      It memoises components and values automatically at build time, making most manual memoisation unnecessary, as long as components follow the rules (pure, no mutation).

      Done when

      With 5,000 items, filtering and scrolling feel instant (no long tasks over 50 ms in the Performance panel), stats are tested, and you've written down before/after measurements for your LinkedIn post.

      M10 · End-to-end tests, CI and deployment

      Build

      • Playwright tests for the critical journeys, plus an automated accessibility check.
      • GitHub Actions running lint, type-check, unit tests, build and E2E on every push.
      • A deployed app with SPA routing fixed, and a README that sells the project.

      Concepts: why and syntax

      ts
      // e2e/tracker.spec.ts
import { test, expect } from "@playwright/test";
import AxeBuilder from "@axe-core/playwright";

test("track an application from wishlist to interview, and it persists", async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto("/");

      CI with GitHub Actions (CI/CD)

      yaml

name: CI

      Deploying a single-page app

      Vercel, Netlify or Cloudflare Pages: connect the GitHub repo, build command npm run build, output folder dist. Fix deep links so refreshing /stats doesn't 404:

      text
      # Netlify: public/_redirects
