      2. Architecture: "Each board is a Yjs CRDT document. The client writes locally first and persists to IndexedDB, and a Hocuspocus server relays and stores updates over WebSockets. A Hono API with SQLite handles accounts, roles and invites; the sync server checks roles on connect and makes viewers read-only."
      3. The interesting decisions: cards as a flat map of Y.Maps so field edits merge; fractional indexing for order so concurrent moves never duplicate cards; presence in ephemeral awareness, not the document; undo tracking only local origins; a deterministic repair pass for invariants the CRDT can't guarantee.
      4. Quality: "Unit tests simulate devices as separate documents, including a seeded fuzz test with three devices; API tests; Playwright tests with two browser contexts, including offline edits merging on reconnect; all in CI."
      5. Trade-offs and next steps: last-writer-wins on short fields, what happens when access is revoked while offline, scaling with Redis, moving to Postgres.

      Rapid-fire questions to rehearse

      Why local-first instead of a normal client-server app?

      Instant interactions (no waiting on the network), full offline use, resilience to flaky connections, and users keep a copy of their data. The cost: sync and conflict complexity, and eventual consistency.

      How are conflicts resolved in your app?

      Different fields merge automatically; the same short field is last-writer-wins, deterministic everywhere; rich text merges at character level; structural invariants (orphans, duplicate keys) are fixed by a deterministic repair pass.

      What's the difference between document state and awareness?

      Document state is durable, versioned and merged (cards, columns). Awareness is ephemeral per-connection state (cursors, who's online, what someone is dragging) that expires when they leave.

      Does the document grow forever?

      Yjs keeps small tombstones for deleted items so merges stay correct, and garbage-collects deleted content when possible. Documents stay compact in practice; for very long-lived boards you can snapshot or split data (for example, archive old cards to a separate document).

      How would you design this with Postgres as the source of truth instead?

      Use a sync engine (Zero, ElectricSQL, PowerSync) or server-authoritative mutations with optimistic UI and rebasing of pending local changes when server state arrives. You gain relational queries and central authority; you lose some offline freedom and peer-to-peer simplicity.

      How did you test something real-time and non-deterministic?

      Made it deterministic: devices as separate documents with explicit sync in unit tests, a seeded fuzz test, and Playwright tests driven by awaited outcomes and the offline/online events rather than timeouts.

      What breaks at 10,000 concurrent users on one board?

      Broadcast fan-out and presence traffic, server memory per document, and client rendering. Throttle and sample presence, shard documents, scale sync servers horizontally with Redis pub/sub, and virtualise large columns.

      Resume line (once C11 is done)

      "Built SyncBoard, a local-first collaborative kanban (React, TypeScript, Yjs CRDT, WebSockets, IndexedDB, PWA) with offline editing, real-time presence, conflict-safe ordering, role-based access and Playwright tests simulating concurrent offline users."

      Part

      HTML & CSS

      The layer every framework outputs in the end. Semantic markup, the cascade, the box model, flexbox, grid, responsive design and modern CSS.

      Semantic HTML

      Use the element that describes the meaning, not the look. Semantic HTML gives you accessibility, SEO and keyboard support for free.
      html
      <!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
  <meta charset="UTF-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">  <!-- required for mobile -->
  <title>Page title shown in the tab and search results</title>
  <meta name="description" content="One-sentence summary for search engines">
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="styles.css">
  <script src="app.js" defer></script>
</head>
<body>
  <header>
    <nav aria-label="Main">
      <a href="/">Home</a> <a href="/blog">Blog</a>
    </nav>
  </header>
  <main>                              <!-- one per page: the main content -->
    <article>                         <!-- self-contained: blog post, product card -->
      <h1>Only one h1 per page</h1>
      <section>                       <!-- a themed group with a heading -->
        <h2>Subheading</h2>
        <p>Text with <strong>importance</strong> and <em>emphasis</em>.</p>
        <figure>
          <img src="chart.png" alt="Sales doubled from March to June">
          <figcaption>Quarterly sales</figcaption>
        </figure>
      </section>
    </article>
    <aside>Related links</aside>       <!-- tangential content -->
  </main>
  <footer>© 2026</footer>
</body>
</html>
      UseInstead ofWhy
      <button><div onclick>Focusable, works with Enter/Space, announced as a button
      <a href>button that navigatesLinks go places, buttons do things; middle-click, copy link work
      <h1>–<h6> in orderbig bold divScreen reader users navigate by headings
      <ul>/<ol>divs with dashes"List, 5 items" is announced
      <label>placeholder onlyClick target + accessible name
      <table> with <th>grid of divs for tabular dataRow/column relationships are announced
      <dialog>, <details>custom JS widgetsNative modal/disclosure behaviour

      Inline vs block elements

      Block (div, p, h1, section, ul) start on a new line and take full width. Inline (span, a, strong, img) flow within text; width/height and vertical margins don't apply (except replaced elements like img). Don't put block elements inside <p>.

      Images and media

      html
      <img src="cat-800.jpg" alt="Grey cat asleep on a laptop" width="800" height="600" loading="lazy">
<img srcset="cat-400.jpg 400w, cat-800.jpg 800w" sizes="(max-width: 600px) 100vw, 50vw" src="cat-800.jpg" alt="…">
<picture>
  <source srcset="hero.avif" type="image/avif">
  <img src="hero.jpg" alt="…">
</picture>
<video src="demo.mp4" controls muted playsinline poster="poster.jpg"></video>

      Always set width/height (or aspect-ratio) to prevent layout shift. alt="" for decorative images.

      HTML forms

      html
      <form action="/signup" method="post">
  <label for="email">Email</label>
  <input id="email" name="email" type="email" required autocomplete="email">

  <label for="pw">Password</label>
  <input id="pw" name="password" type="password" minlength="8" required autocomplete="new-password">

  <fieldset>
    <legend>Plan</legend>
    <label><input type="radio" name="plan" value="free" checked> Free</label>
    <label><input type="radio" name="plan" value="pro"> Pro</label>
  </fieldset>

  <label for="country">Country</label>
  <select id="country" name="country"><option value="in">India</option></select>

  <label><input type="checkbox" name="terms" required> I accept the terms</label>
  <button type="submit">Create account</button>
</form>

      Input types give the right mobile keyboard and built-in validation: email, tel, url, number, date, time, range, color, file, search, password. Attributes: required, min/max, minlength/maxlength, pattern, placeholder, disabled, readonly, autocomplete, inputmode. CSS can style states with :invalid, :user-invalid, :focus-visible, :disabled, :checked.

      A <button> inside a form defaults to type="submit". Add type="button" to buttons that shouldn't submit.

      Selectors, the cascade and specificity

      Selectors

      css
      p {}                 /* type */
.card {}             /* class */
#main {}             /* id */
*  {}                /* universal */
[type="email"] {}    /* attribute */
.nav a {}            /* descendant */
.nav > a {}          /* direct child */
h2 + p {}            /* next sibling */
h2 ~ p {}            /* all following siblings */
a:hover, a:focus-visible {}          /* pseudo-classes: state */
li:first-child, li:nth-child(2n) {}  /* structure */
input:not([disabled]) {}
.card:has(img) {}                    /* parent selector! card that contains an img */
p::first-line, .btn::before {}       /* pseudo-elements: a part / generated content */

      How the browser picks a winner (the cascade)

      1. Origin and importance — !important beats normal declarations (avoid it).
      2. Cascade layers — rules in later @layers win over earlier ones.
      3. Specificity — the more specific selector wins.
      4. Order — if tied, the rule written last wins.

      Specificity scoring (a, b, c)

      SelectorScore
      Inline style=""beats all selectors
      #id(1,0,0)
      .class, [attr], :hover(0,1,0)
      div, ::before(0,0,1)
      *, :where()(0,0,0)
      nav ul li a.active(0,1,4)
      #header .nav a(1,1,1)

      Compare left to right: one id beats any number of classes. :is()/:not()/:has() take the specificity of their most specific argument; :where() is always zero (great for resets).

      Inheritance

      Text-related properties inherit (color, font-*, line-height, text-align, visibility); box properties don't (margin, padding, border, background, width). Force with inherit, reset with initial/unset/revert.

      Keep specificity low and flat: style with single classes, avoid ids and deep nesting in CSS. Then order decides, and overrides stay easy.

      Box model, display and units

      The box model

      text
      ┌──────────── margin (outside space, transparent, can collapse) ────────────┐
│ ┌──────────── border ──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
│ │ ┌──────────── padding (inside space, gets background) ─────────────┐ │ │
│ │ │                     content (width × height)                     │ │ │
│ │ └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │ │
│ └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
      css
      *, *::before, *::after { box-sizing: border-box; }  /* width INCLUDES padding + border. Always add this. */

      With the default content-box, width: 300px + padding: 20px = 340px wide. With border-box it stays 300px.

      Margin collapse: vertical margins between block siblings (and parent/first child) merge into the larger one instead of adding. Doesn't happen in flex/grid containers or with padding/border in between.

      display

      ValueBehaviour
      blockNew line, full width, respects width/height
      inlineIn text flow, ignores width/height/vertical margin
      inline-blockIn text flow but respects width/height
      flex / gridLayout modes for children
      noneRemoved from layout and accessibility tree
      contentsBox disappears, children remain

      display: none removes the element; visibility: hidden hides it but keeps its space; opacity: 0 keeps it clickable and in the accessibility tree.

      Units

      UnitRelative toUse for
      pxFixedBorders, small details
      remRoot font size (usually 16px)Font sizes, spacing (respects user zoom settings)
      emCurrent element's font sizePadding that scales with the text (buttons)
      %Parent sizeFluid widths
      vw / vhViewport width/heightFull-screen sections
      dvh / svhDynamic/small viewport heightMobile full height (avoids address-bar jumps)
      chWidth of "0"Readable line length: max-width: 65ch
      frFree space in gridGrid columns

      Overflow

      overflow: hidden | scroll | auto | clip; text-overflow: ellipsis (with white-space: nowrap; overflow: hidden) for one-line truncation; -webkit-line-clamp / line-clamp for multi-line.

      Positioning, z-index and stacking

      positionOffsets (top/left…) relative toIn normal flow?
      static (default)Offsets ignoredYes
      relativeIts own normal positionYes (space kept)
      absoluteNearest positioned ancestor (not static)No
      fixedThe viewportNo
      stickyActs relative until a scroll threshold, then sticksYes
      css
      .card { position: relative; }          /* becomes the anchor */
.badge { position: absolute; top: 8px; right: 8px; }
.header { position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: 10; }
.overlay { position: fixed; inset: 0; } /* inset: 0 = top/right/bottom/left 0 */

      z-index and stacking contexts

      z-index works on positioned elements (and flex/grid children). It only competes within the same stacking context. New stacking contexts are created by: position + z-index, opacity < 1, transform, filter, isolation: isolate, position: fixed/sticky… That's why a z-index: 9999 modal can still hide behind something: its parent's context is lower. Render modals near <body> (portals) or use <dialog>, which uses the top layer.

      Centering cheat sheet

      css
      .center-flex { display: flex; justify-content: center; align-items: center; }
.center-grid { display: grid; place-items: center; }
.center-block { margin-inline: auto; max-width: 60ch; }       /* horizontal, block */
.center-abs { position: absolute; top: 50%; left: 50%; transform: translate(-50%, -50%); }

      Flexbox

      One-dimensional layout: arrange items in a row or a column, distribute space, align them. Navbars, toolbars, cards in a row, centering.
      css
      .container {
  display: flex;
  flex-direction: row;          /* row | column | row-reverse | column-reverse */
  justify-content: space-between; /* MAIN axis: flex-start | center | flex-end | space-between | space-around | space-evenly */
  align-items: center;          /* CROSS axis: stretch (default) | flex-start | center | flex-end | baseline */
  flex-wrap: wrap;              /* allow items onto new lines */
  gap: 16px;                    /* space between items (no margin hacks) */
}
.item {
  flex: 1;                      /* shorthand: flex-grow 1, flex-shrink 1, flex-basis 0 */
  flex: 0 0 200px;              /* don't grow, don't shrink, 200px */
  align-self: flex-end;         /* override align-items for one item */
  order: -1;                    /* visual order (doesn't change tab order!) */
  min-width: 0;                 /* lets text truncate inside flex items */
}

      Main axis = the direction of flex-direction; cross axis = perpendicular. When you switch to column, justify-content becomes vertical.

      Patterns

      css
      .navbar { display: flex; align-items: center; gap: 1rem; }
.navbar .spacer { margin-left: auto; }       /* push the rest to the right */
.card { display: flex; flex-direction: column; }
.card .footer { margin-top: auto; }          /* pin footer to the bottom */
.page { display: flex; flex-direction: column; min-height: 100dvh; } main { flex: 1; } /* sticky footer */

      Practise with Flexbox Froggy and Grid Garden (free games).

      CSS Grid

      Two-dimensional layout: rows and columns at the same time. Page layouts, galleries, dashboards, forms.
      css
      .grid {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: 200px 1fr 1fr;             /* fixed + 2 flexible columns */
  grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr);
  grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(220px, 1fr)); /* responsive cards, no media query */
  grid-template-rows: auto 1fr auto;
  gap: 24px 16px;                                    /* row-gap column-gap */
}
.wide { grid-column: span 2; }                       /* take two columns */
.full { grid-column: 1 / -1; }                       /* first line to last line */
.hero { grid-row: 1 / 3; }

/* Named areas */
.layout {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: 240px 1fr;
  grid-template-areas:
    "header header"
    "sidebar main"
    "footer footer";
}
header { grid-area: header; } aside { grid-area: sidebar; } main { grid-area: main; } footer { grid-area: footer; }
@media (max-width: 700px) {
  .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; grid-template-areas: "header" "main" "sidebar" "footer"; }
}

      auto-fit collapses empty tracks (items stretch); auto-fill keeps empty tracks. subgrid lets children align to the parent grid's lines (cards with aligned titles and buttons).

      Choose Flexbox when…Choose Grid when…
      Content decides the size; one row or columnThe layout decides the size; rows and columns
      Navbars, button groups, centering, chipsPage shells, galleries, dashboards, forms with aligned labels

      Responsive design

      • Mobile-first: write base styles for small screens, add min-width media queries for bigger ones.
      • Fluid by default: max-width: 100% on images, percentages/fr, flex-wrap and auto-fit grids.
      • Test at 320px, 375px, 768px, 1024px, 1440px, and with 200% zoom.
      css
      .cards { display: grid; gap: 1rem; }                       /* mobile: 1 column */
@media (min-width: 640px)  { .cards { grid-template-columns: repeat(2, 1fr); } }
@media (min-width: 1024px) { .cards { grid-template-columns: repeat(3, 1fr); } }

@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { :root { --bg: #111827; } }
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) { * { animation: none !important; } }
@media (hover: hover) { .card:hover { transform: translateY(-2px); } }  /* only on devices that hover */

/* Fluid type without breakpoints */
h1 { font-size: clamp(2rem, 1rem + 4vw, 4rem); }           /* min, preferred, max */

/* Container queries: respond to the PARENT's width, not the screen */
.card-wrap { container-type: inline-size; }
@container (min-width: 400px) { .card { display: flex; } }

      Media vs container queries: media queries react to the viewport; container queries react to the component's container, so the same card can be vertical in a sidebar and horizontal in the main column.

      Modern CSS features

      css
      /* Custom properties (CSS variables) — runtime, inherited, changeable with JS */
:root { --brand: #4f46e5; --radius: 12px; --space: 1rem; }
.btn { background: var(--brand); border-radius: var(--radius); padding: var(--space, 16px); }
.dark { --brand: #818cf8; }                         /* theme by overriding variables */
/* JS: document.documentElement.style.setProperty("--brand", "tomato"); */

/* Native nesting */
.card {
  padding: 1rem;
  & h2 { margin: 0; }
  &:hover { outline: 2px solid var(--brand); }
  @media (min-width: 600px) { padding: 2rem; }
}

/* Useful functions and properties */
width: min(100% - 2rem, 70ch);         /* min(), max(), clamp(), calc() */
aspect-ratio: 16 / 9;
object-fit: cover;                     /* images fill their box without distortion */
inset: 0;  margin-inline: auto;  padding-block: 1rem;   /* logical properties (RTL-friendly) */
accent-color: var(--brand);            /* style checkboxes/radios */
scroll-behavior: smooth;  scroll-snap-type: x mandatory;
text-wrap: balance;                    /* nicer headings */
color: color-mix(in oklch, var(--brand) 70%, white);

/* Cascade layers: control order without specificity wars */
@layer reset, base, components, utilities;
@layer components { .btn { … } }

/* :has() — style a parent based on its children */
form:has(input:invalid) button { opacity: .5; }

      Also worth knowing: @supports for feature detection, :is()/:where(), view transitions (view-transition-name), anchor positioning for tooltips, @property for animatable variables. Check support on caniuse.com or Baseline labels on MDN.

      Transforms, transitions and animations

      css
      .btn { transition: transform 150ms ease, background-color 200ms; }
.btn:hover { transform: translateY(-2px) scale(1.02); }

@keyframes fadeUp {
  from { opacity: 0; transform: translateY(12px); }
  to   { opacity: 1; transform: none; }
}
.toast { animation: fadeUp 300ms ease-out both; }
/* animation: name duration timing delay iteration-count direction fill-mode */

.spinner { animation: spin 1s linear infinite; }
@keyframes spin { to { rotate: 360deg; } }
      • Animate transform and opacity — they run on the GPU compositor. Animating width, top, margin triggers layout on every frame (janky).
      • will-change: transform only for elements about to animate; overuse costs memory.
      • Respect prefers-reduced-motion.
      • JS options: Web Animations API (el.animate()), Motion (Framer Motion) for React, GSAP for complex timelines.

      Tailwind, Sass and CSS architecture

      Tailwind CSS

      Utility-first: compose small single-purpose classes directly in markup. The most common choice in React/Next.js jobs.

      html
      <button class="inline-flex items-center gap-2 rounded-lg bg-indigo-600 px-4 py-2 text-sm font-semibold text-white
               hover:bg-indigo-500 focus-visible:outline-2 disabled:opacity-50 md:px-6 dark:bg-indigo-400">
  Save changes
</button>
<!-- p-4 padding, mt-2 margin-top, flex, grid-cols-3, w-full, text-lg, md: = ≥768px, hover:, dark: -->

      Tailwind v4 is configured in CSS (@import "tailwindcss"; and @theme { --color-brand: … }) rather than a JS config file. Extract repetition into components, not @apply everywhere.

      Sass / SCSS

      A preprocessor adding variables, nesting, mixins, functions and partials. Less essential now that CSS has variables and nesting, but common in existing codebases ($primary, @mixin, @include, @use).

      Naming and scoping

      • BEM: .card, .card__title, .card--featured (block, element, modifier). Flat, predictable specificity.
      • CSS Modules: locally scoped class names per component file.
      • Design tokens: colours, spacing and type as variables shared across the app — the basis of a design system.

      A sensible reset

      css
      *, *::before, *::after { box-sizing: border-box; }
* { margin: 0; }
body { line-height: 1.5; -webkit-font-smoothing: antialiased; }
img, picture, video, canvas, svg { display: block; max-width: 100%; }
input, button, textarea, select { font: inherit; }
p, h1, h2, h3 { overflow-wrap: break-word; }
      Part 2

      JavaScript core

      Variables, types, operators, decisions, loops, functions, arrays and objects. This is lecture Part 1, the base everything else sits on.

      Variables: var, let, const

      Rule of thumb: use const by default, let when the value must change, and avoid var. Never create a variable without a keyword.

      A variable is a named box that stores a value so you can use it later.

      js
      var a = 12;      // old way (function-scoped)
let b = 12;      // modern, value can change
const c = 12;    // modern, value can't be reassigned
d = 12;          // ❌ no keyword: creates an accidental global. Never do this.

      Declaration vs initialization

      js
      let age;        // declaration: box created, value is undefined
age = 25;       // initialization / assignment: value put in the box
let city = "Bhopal";  // both at once

      Reassign vs redeclare

      varletconst
      ScopeFunctionBlock { }Block { }
      Reassign (x = 5)✅✅❌ TypeError
      Redeclare (let x again)✅ (dangerous)❌ SyntaxError❌ SyntaxError
      Must initialise on declarationNoNoYes
      HoistedYes, as undefinedYes, but in TDZYes, but in TDZ
      Added to window (global)YesNoNo

      So why does const exist?

      Use const when you already know the value should never change, for example const PI = 3.14. If you (or a teammate) accidentally change it later, JS throws an error instead of silently producing wrong results.

      const protects the binding, not the contents. A const object or array can still be modified:

      js
      const user = { name: "Harsh" };
user.name = "Aman";     // ✅ allowed: we changed what's inside
user = {};              // ❌ TypeError: we tried to point to a new box
const nums = [1, 2];
nums.push(3);           // ✅ [1, 2, 3]

      Naming rules

      • Can contain letters, digits, _ and $.
      • Can't start with a digit, can't contain spaces or other special characters, can't be a keyword.
      • Case-sensitive: name and Name are different.
      • Convention: camelCase for variables/functions, PascalCase for classes, UPPER_SNAKE for true constants.

      Scope, hoisting and the TDZ

      Scope = where a variable can be used

      • Global scope: declared outside everything, usable everywhere.
      • Function scope: declared inside a function, usable only inside that function. var respects only this.
      • Block scope: declared inside any { } (if, for, while, plain braces). let and const respect this.
      js
      if (true) {
  var x = 1;    // leaks out of the block
  let y = 2;    // stays inside the block
}
console.log(x); // 1
console.log(y); // ❌ ReferenceError: y is not defined

function test() {
  var z = 3;    // stays inside the function
}
console.log(z); // ❌ ReferenceError

      Classic bug: var in a loop with timers prints the final value every time, because there is only one shared i. let creates a fresh i for each iteration.

      js
      for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i)); // 3 3 3
for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) setTimeout(() => console.log(j)); // 0 1 2

      Hoisting

      Before running your code, JS scans it and moves declarations to the top of their scope (conceptually). Only the declaration moves, not the value.

      js
      console.log(a); // undefined  (var is hoisted and set to undefined)
var a = 10;

console.log(b); // ❌ ReferenceError: Cannot access 'b' before initialization
let b = 20;

greet();        // ✅ works: function declarations are hoisted fully
function greet() { console.log("hi"); }

sayHi();        // ❌ TypeError: sayHi is not a function (it's var = undefined)
var sayHi = function () {};

      Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)

      let and const are hoisted, but they stay unusable from the start of the block until the line where they are declared. That gap is the TDZ. Accessing them there throws a ReferenceError.

      You turn 18 at a certain age. Before that birthday, asking for the car keys gets you "not yet", even though you'll definitely get them later. The time before initialization is the "not yet" zone.

      Scope chain and shadowing

      When JS can't find a variable in the current scope it looks in the parent scope, then the parent's parent, up to global. That lookup path is the scope chain. A variable in an inner scope with the same name shadows the outer one.

      js
      let name = "outer";
function show() {
  let name = "inner";   // shadows the outer name
  console.log(name);    // inner
}

      Practice

      What prints? var x = 1; { var x = 2; } console.log(x);

      2. var ignores the block, so both are the same variable.

      What prints? let x = 1; { let x = 2; } console.log(x);

      1. The inner x is a different, block-scoped variable.

      Why does console.log(a); let a = 5; throw?

      a is in the Temporal Dead Zone: hoisted but not initialised yet.

      Data types

      Two families. Primitives are single, immutable values copied by value. References (objects, arrays, functions — things with brackets) are copied by reference.

      The 7 primitives

      TypeExampleNotes
      string"hi", 'hi', hi ${x}Text. Immutable.
      number12, 3.14, -5, NaN, InfinityOne type for ints and decimals (64-bit float).
      booleantrue, falseYes / no.
      undefinedlet a;Declared but no value given (JS sets it).
      nulllet a = null;Intentionally empty (you set it).
      symbolSymbol("id")Unique, immutable value. Used for unique object keys.
      bigint12345678901234567890nIntegers bigger than Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER (2⁵³−1).

      Reference types

      Everything else is an object: {} objects, [] arrays, function(){} functions, plus Date, Map, Set, RegExp, etc.

      Copy by value vs copy by reference

      js
      let a = 10;
let b = a;     // copy of the value
b = 20;
console.log(a); // 10  (unchanged)

let o1 = { n: 1 };
let o2 = o1;   // copy of the reference (same object!)
o2.n = 99;
console.log(o1.n); // 99  (changed through o2)

      Primitives are like photocopies: change your copy, the original is untouched. References are like sharing a Google Doc link: anyone with the link edits the same document.

      Symbol

      js
      const id1 = Symbol("id");
const id2 = Symbol("id");
console.log(id1 === id2); // false — every symbol is unique
const user = { [id1]: 101, name: "Harsh" };  // hidden-ish key, won't clash

      BigInt

      js
      console.log(9007199254740991 + 2);   // 9007199254740992 ❌ precision lost
console.log(9007199254740991n + 2n); // 9007199254740993n ✅
// You can't mix: 10n + 5 → TypeError. Convert first: 10n + BigInt(5)

      typeof

      js
      typeof "Harsh"     // "string"
typeof 12          // "number"
typeof NaN         // "number"   (yes, really)
typeof true        // "boolean"
typeof undefined   // "undefined"
typeof null        // "object"   ⚠️ a famous bug from 1995, kept forever
typeof Symbol()    // "symbol"
typeof 10n         // "bigint"
typeof {}          // "object"
typeof []          // "object"   → use Array.isArray([]) instead
typeof function(){} // "function"

      null vs undefined: undefined = "no value yet" (set by JS). null = "empty on purpose" (set by you). null == undefined is true, null === undefined is false.

      Type conversion, truthy and falsy

      Explicit conversion (you do it)

      js
      Number("42")      // 42
Number("42px")    // NaN
parseInt("42px")  // 42   (reads digits until it can't)
parseFloat("3.5kg") // 3.5
Number("")        // 0
Number(true)      // 1
Number(null)      // 0
Number(undefined) // NaN
String(42)        // "42"
(42).toString()   // "42"
Boolean(0)        // false
Boolean("hi")     // true
+"5"              // 5   (unary plus converts to number)

      Implicit conversion / coercion (JS does it)

      js
      "5" + 2     // "52"  → + with a string joins (concatenation)
"5" - 2     // 3     → -, *, / convert to number
"5" * "2"   // 10
true + 1    // 2
"abc" * 2   // NaN
[] + []     // ""
[] + {}     // "[object Object]"

      Truthy and falsy

      An if needs true or false. If you give it something else (like 12), JS converts it. There are exactly 8 falsy values; everything else is truthy.

      Falsy (all 8)Surprising truthy values
      false, 0, -0, 0n, "", null, undefined, NaN"0", "false", " ", [], {}, -1, Infinity, any function
      js
      if ("") console.log("won't run");
if ([]) console.log("runs — empty array is truthy");
!!"hello"   // true  (double NOT = quick boolean conversion)

      NaN

      NaN ("Not a Number") is the result of a failed math operation. It's of type number and it's not equal to anything, even itself.

      js
      NaN === NaN          // false
Number.isNaN(NaN)    // true   ✅ use this
isNaN("hello")       // true   (older, converts first — less reliable)

      Operators

      Every operator has a use case. Remember what it's for and the syntax falls into place.

      Arithmetic

      js
      10 + 3   // 13
10 - 3   // 7
10 * 3   // 30
10 / 3   // 3.333…
10 % 3   // 1   remainder (modulus) — great for even/odd, divisibility
2 ** 3   // 8   exponent (power)

      Assignment

      js
      let x = 10;
x += 5;  // x = x + 5  → 15
x -= 2;  // 13
x *= 2;  // 26
x /= 2;  // 13
x %= 5;  // 3
x **= 2; // 9

      Increment and decrement (pre vs post)

      js
      let n = 5;
console.log(n++); // 5  → post: use first, then increase (n is now 6)
console.log(++n); // 7  → pre: increase first, then use
let count = 5;
console.log(count-- === 5 ? "matched" : "no"); // "matched"

      Comparison

      js
      5 == "5"     // true   loose: converts types first
5 === "5"    // false  strict: type AND value must match  ✅ prefer this
5 != "5"     // false
5 !== "5"    // true
12 > 11      // true
12 >= 12     // true
"apple" < "banana"  // true (compares letter codes)

      Always use === and !==. The only common exception: x == null checks for both null and undefined at once.

      Logical

      js
      true && false   // false  AND: both must be true
true || false   // true   OR: at least one true
!true           // false  NOT: flips

// Short-circuit: they return one of the operands, not just true/false
"Harsh" && "Aman"  // "Aman"   (&& returns first falsy, or the last value)
"" || "Guest"      // "Guest"  (|| returns first truthy)
isLoggedIn && showDashboard(); // runs only if isLoggedIn is truthy

      Nullish coalescing ?? and optional chaining ?.

      js
      let score = 0;
score || 10   // 10  ❌ treats 0 as "missing"
score ?? 10   // 0   ✅ only null/undefined count as missing

user?.address?.city   // undefined instead of crashing if address is missing
user.getName?.()      // calls only if the method exists
arr?.[0]

      Ternary

      js
      // condition ? valueIfTrue : valueIfFalse
const status = age >= 18 ? "adult" : "minor";

      typeof and instanceof

      typeof gives a type name. instanceof checks whether an object was created by a constructor ("born from that parent"). It only works on reference values.

      js
      [] instanceof Array     // true
({}) instanceof Object  // true
let a = 12;
a instanceof Number     // false — primitives aren't instances
new Number(12) instanceof Number // true

      Other operators worth knowing

      • Spread / rest ... — covered in Functions and Arrays.
      • Comma a = (1, 2) → 2. Rare.
      • Bitwise & | ^ ~ << >> — rare in web work.
      • delete removes an object property; in checks if a key exists: "name" in user.
      • Logical assignment: a ||= b, a &&= b, a ??= b.

      Precedence (high → low, simplified): ( ) → ++ -- ! typeof → ** → * / % → + - → < > <= >= → == === → && → || ?? → ? : → = += …. When unsure, add brackets.

      Practice

      let x = 3; let y = x++; what are x and y?

      x = 4, y = 3 (post-increment returns the old value).

      let a = 10; let b = --a;

      Both are 9 (pre-decrement changes first).

      Temperature is 35. What does !(temp < 30) give?

      35 < 30 is false, NOT flips it → true.

      "10" + 5 - 3

      "10" + 5 = "105", then "105" - 3 = 102.

      Control flow: if, switch, early return

      if / else if / else

      js
      const marks = 72;
if (marks >= 90) {
  console.log("A");
} else if (marks >= 70) {
  console.log("B");
} else if (marks >= 50) {
  console.log("C");
} else {
  console.log("Fail");
}

      Order matters. Check the narrowest condition first. If you write if (n < 100) before if (n < 10), the second branch can never run, because every number below 10 is also below 100.

      switch

      Use when you compare one value against many exact matches. It uses strict equality (===).

      js
      const day = "sat";
switch (day) {
  case "sat":
  case "sun":              // grouped cases (fall-through on purpose)
    console.log("Weekend");
    break;                 // without break, execution falls into the next case
  case "mon":
    console.log("Ugh, Monday");
    break;
  default:
    console.log("Weekday");
}

      Trick: switch (true) lets you use ranges: case marks >= 90: ….

      Early return pattern

      Instead of deep nesting, exit the function as soon as a condition is handled. Code becomes flat and readable.

      js
      // ❌ nested
function check(age) {
  if (age >= 18) {
    if (age < 60) { return "working age"; } else { return "senior"; }
  } else { return "too young"; }
}
// ✅ early return
function check(age) {
  if (age < 18) return "too young";
  if (age >= 60) return "senior";
  return "working age";
}

      Mini project: Rock Paper Scissors logic

      js
      function rps(user, computer) {
  if (user === computer) return "draw";
  if (
    (user === "rock" && computer === "scissors") ||
    (user === "paper" && computer === "rock") ||
    (user === "scissors" && computer === "paper")
  ) return "user wins";
  return "computer wins";
}
const options = ["rock", "paper", "scissors"];
const comp = options[Math.floor(Math.random() * 3)];
console.log(rps("rock", comp));

      Practice

      Write a grade function using early return: 90+ A, 75+ B, 50+ C, else F.
      js
      function grade(m) {
  if (m >= 90) return "A";
  if (m >= 75) return "B";
  if (m >= 50) return "C";
  return "F";
}
      Given a number, print "Fizz" if divisible by 3, "Buzz" if by 5, "FizzBuzz" if both.
      js
      function fb(n) {
  if (n % 15 === 0) return "FizzBuzz";   // check the narrow case first
  if (n % 3 === 0) return "Fizz";
  if (n % 5 === 0) return "Buzz";
  return n;
}

      Loops

      Every loop = start + end + change. Use for when you know how many times. Use while when you don't know when it ends ("keep going until you find Harsh").

      for

      js
      //   start      end      change
for (let i = 1; i <= 10; i++) {
  console.log(i);    // 1 … 10
}
for (let i = 10; i >= 1; i--) console.log(i); // countdown: note >= not <=

      while

      js
      let i = 1;          // start
while (i < 33) {    // end condition
  console.log(i);
  i++;              // change — forget this and you get an infinite loop
}

      do…while

      Runs the body at least once, then checks the condition.

      js
      // Node (run with: node retry.js) — simulating attempts instead of a browser prompt
const attempts = ["1111", "4321", "1234"];
let i = 0, pin;
do {
  pin = attempts[i++];
  console.log("Trying", pin);
} while (pin !== "1234" && i < attempts.length);
console.log(pin === "1234" ? "Unlocked" : "Locked out");

      In a real app the value comes from an input field, not a dialog:

      js
      // browser: script.js loaded by index.html
form.addEventListener("submit", (e) => {
  e.preventDefault();
  if (pinInput.value !== "1234") { error.textContent = "Wrong PIN, try again"; return; }
  unlock();
});

      break and continue

      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 20; i++) {
  if (i % 3 === 0) continue;  // skip this round, go to next
  if (i > 15) break;          // stop the whole loop
  console.log(i);
}

      for…of (values) and for…in (keys)

      js
      for (const fruit of ["apple", "mango"]) console.log(fruit);  // arrays, strings, Maps, Sets
for (const ch of "hey") console.log(ch);                     // h e y

const user = { name: "Harsh", age: 26 };
for (const key in user) console.log(key, user[key]);          // objects: name Harsh, age 26

      Don't use for…in on arrays: it gives string indexes and can include inherited keys. Use for…of or forEach.

      Labels (rare)

      js
      outer: for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  for (let j = 0; j < 3; j++) {
    if (j === 1) continue outer;   // jumps to the next i
  }
}

      Infinite loop in the browser? The tab freezes. Close the tab (or copy the URL, open a new tab and paste it), fix the change/end condition, reload.

      Practice (from the lecture)

      1. Print numbers 1 to 10 using a for loop.
      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 10; i++) console.log(i);
      2. Print 10 down to 1 using a while loop.
      js
      let i = 10;
while (i >= 1) { console.log(i); i--; }
      3. Print even numbers from 1 to 20.
      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 20; i++) if (i % 2 === 0) console.log(i);
// or: for (let i = 2; i <= 20; i += 2) console.log(i);
      4. Sum of numbers from 1 to 100.
      js
      let sum = 0;
for (let i = 1; i <= 100; i++) sum += i;
console.log(sum); // 5050
      5. Print numbers 1–50 divisible by 3.
      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 50; i++) if (i % 3 === 0) console.log(i);
      6. Take a number and say whether it's even or odd.
      js
      // evenOdd.js — run with: node evenOdd.js 7
function evenOdd(n) {
  return n % 2 === 0 ? "even" : "odd";
}
console.log(evenOdd(7));                       // odd
console.log(evenOdd(Number(process.argv[2]))); // reads the number you pass in the terminal

      Writing it as a function is how interviews and real code do it: easy to test, no dialogs. In the browser you'd feed it input.value instead.

      7. Count how many numbers from 1–100 are divisible by both 3 and 5.
      js
      let count = 0;
for (let i = 1; i <= 100; i++) if (i % 3 === 0 && i % 5 === 0) count++;
console.log(count); // 6
      8. Loop 1–20 but skip multiples of 3 (use continue).
      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 20; i++) { if (i % 3 === 0) continue; console.log(i); }
      9. Keep reading input until the user types "stop".
      js
      // Node's built-in readline — run with: node ask.js
import readline from "node:readline/promises";
const rl = readline.createInterface({ input: process.stdin, output: process.stdout });

let answer;
while (answer !== "stop") {
  answer = (await rl.question("Type something (stop to quit): ")).trim();
  console.log("You typed:", answer);
}
rl.close();

      Save it as ask.mjs (or add "type": "module" to package.json) so import works. Same loop, real terminal input.

      10. Print the first number between 1–100 divisible by 7 and stop.
      js
      for (let i = 1; i <= 100; i++) if (i % 7 === 0) { console.log(i); break; }

      Functions

      Why functions? Code you write normally runs immediately. A function lets you save code now and run it later (when a button is clicked, when data arrives), and reuse it as many times as you want.

      Three ways to write one

      js
      // 1. Function declaration (hoisted)
function add(a, b) { return a + b; }

// 2. Function expression (stored in a variable, not hoisted)
const sub = function (a, b) { return a - b; };

// 3. Arrow function (ES6, shorter)
const mul = (a, b) => a * b;          // implicit return (no braces)
const square = n => n * n;            // one param: brackets optional
const hello = () => console.log("hi");
const makeUser = () => ({ name: "A" }); // return an object: wrap in ( )

      Parameters vs arguments

      js
      function dance(animal) {       // animal = parameter (placeholder)
  console.log(`${animal} is dancing`);
}
dance("horse");                // "horse" = argument (real value)
dance("deer");

      Missing arguments become undefined. Extra ones are ignored (but available in arguments in normal functions).

      Default parameters

      js
      function greet(name = "Guest") { return `Hello ${name}`; }
greet();        // "Hello Guest"

      Rest parameters (... in the parameter list)

      Collect any number of arguments into an array. Must be the last parameter.

      js
      function total(...scores) {
  let sum = 0;
  for (const s of scores) sum += s;
  return sum;
}
total(10, 20, 30);  // 60

      Same ... dots, two jobs. In a function's parameters → rest (gathers values into an array). Anywhere else (calling a function, inside [ ] or { }) → spread (expands an array/object into separate values).

      return

      return sends a value back and stops the function. No return → the function returns undefined.

      First-class functions

      In JS, functions are values. You can store them in variables, pass them as arguments, and return them from other functions.

      Callbacks

      A callback is a function you pass to another function so it can be called later.

      js
      function doMath(a, b, operation) { return operation(a, b); }
doMath(4, 5, (x, y) => x + y);   // 9
setTimeout(() => console.log("later"), 1000);  // callback runs after 1s

      Higher-order functions (HOF)

      A function that takes a function as an argument and/or returns a function. map, filter, forEach, setTimeout, addEventListener are all HOFs.

      js
      function multiplier(x) {
  return function (n) { return n * x; };  // returns a function
}
const double = multiplier(2);
double(5); // 10

      Pure vs impure functions

      • Pure: same input → always same output, and it doesn't change anything outside itself (no side effects).
      • Impure: depends on or changes outside state (global variables, DOM, random, time, network).
      js
      let total = 0;
function addImpure(n) { total += n; }            // ❌ changes outer variable
function addPure(t, n) { return t + n; }         // ✅ returns new value

// making the lecture example pure: copy instead of mutating
function addToTotal(total, n) { let newTotal = total; newTotal += n; return newTotal; }

      Arrow functions vs normal functions

      Normal functionArrow =>
      Own thisYes (depends on how it's called)No — uses this from the surrounding code
      arguments objectYesNo (use rest ...args)
      Can be used with newYesNo
      HoistedDeclarations yesNo (it's a variable)
      Good forObject methods, constructorsCallbacks, short helpers

      Recursion

      A function that calls itself. Always needs a base case to stop.

      js
      function factorial(n) {
  if (n <= 1) return 1;          // base case
  return n * factorial(n - 1);   // recursive case
}
factorial(5); // 120

      Practice (from the lecture)

      Convert function sq(n){ return n*n; } into an arrow function.

      Remove function, add =>: const sq = n => n * n;

      Use a rest parameter to accept any number of scores and return the total.
      js
      const getScore = (...scores) => scores.reduce((acc, s) => acc + s, 0);
      Fix using early return: if age < 18 return "too young", else "allowed".
      js
      function canEnter(age) {
  if (age < 18) return "too young";
  return "allowed";
}
      Write a BMI calculator function (weight kg, height m).
      js
      function bmi(weight, height) {
  const value = weight / (height * height);
  if (value < 18.5) return `${value.toFixed(1)} Underweight`;
  if (value < 25) return `${value.toFixed(1)} Normal`;
  if (value < 30) return `${value.toFixed(1)} Overweight`;
  return `${value.toFixed(1)} Obese`;
}
bmi(70, 1.75); // "22.9 Normal"
      Write a discount calculator HOF: discount(10) returns a function that applies 10% off.
      js
      const discount = pct => price => price - price * pct / 100;
const tenOff = discount(10);
tenOff(500); // 450

      Lexical scope, closures and IIFE

      Lexical scope

      "Lexical" means where you write it. A function can use variables from the place it was written, i.e. from the brackets it sits inside, not from where it is called.

      js
      function abcd() {
  let a = 1;
  function efgh() {
    let b = 2;
    function ghij() {
      let c = 3;
      console.log(a, b, c);  // ✅ can see a, b, c
    }
    ghij();
    // console.log(c);  ❌ c only lives inside ghij's brackets
  }
  efgh();
}

      Closures

      A closure is a function that is returned from another function and still remembers the variables of its parent, even after the parent has finished running.

      js
      function counter() {
  let count = 0;             // private — nothing outside can touch it
  return function () {
    count++;
    return count;
  };
}
const c = counter();
c(); // 1
c(); // 2
c(); // 3   ← count survives between calls: that's the closure
const d = counter();
d(); // 1   ← a separate, fresh count

      A closure is a function carrying a backpack. When it leaves home (the parent function), it packs the variables it needs and keeps them.

      Uses: private variables/data hiding, counters, function factories, memoisation, keeping state in event handlers, debounce/throttle.

      IIFE (Immediately Invoked Function Expression)

      A function that runs the moment it's defined. Used to create a private scope so variables don't leak into global.

      js
      (function () {
  const secret = 42;
  console.log("runs immediately");
})();

(() => console.log("arrow IIFE"))();

const bank = (function () {
  let balance = 0;          // private
  return {
    deposit(x) { balance += x; },
    getBalance() { return balance; }
  };
})();                        // "module pattern"

      Practice

      What is a closure? (interview)

      A function bundled with references to the variables of the scope it was created in. Even after the outer function returns, the inner function can still read and update those variables.

      Convert a normal function into an IIFE.

      Wrap it in brackets and call it: (function greet(){ console.log("hi") })();

      What will this log and why? for (var i=0;i<3;i++){ setTimeout(()=>console.log(i),0) }

      3 3 3. All three callbacks close over the same function-scoped i, which is 3 when they run. Use let to get 0 1 2.

      Arrays

      An array holds many values in one variable, in order. Your WhatsApp chats, Instagram feed and YouTube suggestions are all arrays rendered with loops.
      js
      const nums = [5, 12, 8, 130, 44];
nums[0]          // 5        (index starts at 0)
nums.length      // 5
nums[nums.length - 1] // 44  last item
nums.at(-1)      // 44       modern way to read from the end
nums[1] = 99;    // update
const mixed = [1, "hi", true, null, [1, 2], { a: 1 }]; // any types allowed
Array.isArray(nums)  // true

      Add and remove (these change the original array)

      MethodWhat it doesReturns
      push(x)Add to endnew length
      pop()Remove from endremoved item
      unshift(x)Add to startnew length
      shift()Remove from startremoved item
      splice(start, deleteCount, ...items)Remove / insert / replace anywherearray of removed items
      reverse()Reverse in placesame array
      sort(fn)Sort in placesame array
      fill(v, start, end)Fill with a valuesame array
      js
      const arr = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5];
arr.splice(1, 2);          // removes 2 and 3 → arr is [1, 4, 5]
arr.splice(1, 0, "a", "b");// inserts at index 1 → [1, "a", "b", 4, 5]
arr.splice(2, 1, "X");     // replaces index 2 → [1, "a", "X", 4, 5]

      Read-only methods (return something new, original untouched)

      js
      const a = [10, 20, 30, 40];
a.slice(1, 3)        // [20, 30]   (end index not included)
a.slice(-2)          // [30, 40]
a.concat([50, 60])   // [10, 20, 30, 40, 50, 60]
a.join(" - ")        // "10 - 20 - 30 - 40"
a.includes(20)       // true
a.indexOf(30)        // 2   (-1 if not found)
a.lastIndexOf(30)    // 2
[1, [2, [3]]].flat(Infinity)  // [1, 2, 3]
a.toReversed(); a.toSorted(); a.toSpliced(0, 1); a.with(0, 99); // ES2023 non-mutating copies

      slice vs splice: slice copies a part and never changes the original. splice cuts/inserts and changes the original.

      Iteration methods (the important ones)

      js
      const prices = [5, 12, 8, 130, 44];

prices.forEach((val, i) => console.log(i, val));      // just loop, returns undefined

const plus5 = prices.map(val => val + 5);             // new array, same length
// [10, 17, 13, 135, 49]

const big = prices.filter(val => val > 10);           // keep items where callback returns true
// [12, 130, 44]

const total = prices.reduce((acc, val) => acc + val, 0); // boil down to ONE value
// 199   (acc = accumulator, remembers its value each round; 0 = starting value)

prices.find(val => val < 10)       // 5    first match (or undefined)
prices.findIndex(val => val > 100) // 3
prices.findLast(v => v < 10)       // 8
prices.some(val => val > 100)      // true  at least one passes
prices.every(val => val > 0)       // true  all pass
prices.flatMap(v => [v, v * 2])    // map then flatten one level
      Use…when you want…
      forEachto do something for each item (no new array)
      mapa transformed copy, same length
      filtera smaller copy with only matching items
      reduceone value (sum, max, object, count)
      find / findIndexthe first matching item / its index
      some / everya yes/no answer

      You can't break out of forEach. Use a for…of loop, or some/find if you need to stop early.

      Sorting — the classic trap

      js
      [100, 20, 3].sort()                // [100, 20, 3]  ❌ sorted as STRINGS ("100" < "20" < "3")
[100, 20, 3].sort((a, b) => a - b) // [3, 20, 100]  ✅ ascending
[100, 20, 3].sort((a, b) => b - a) // [100, 20, 3]  ✅ descending
users.sort((a, b) => a.name.localeCompare(b.name)); // sort objects by text

      The comparator returns a negative number (a first), positive (b first) or 0 (keep).

      Spread, copy and destructuring

      js
      const a1 = [1, 2, 3];
const copy = [...a1];               // shallow copy
const merged = [...a1, ...[4, 5]];  // [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Math.max(...a1);                    // 3

const [first, second, ...rest] = [10, 20, 30, 40];
// first=10, second=20, rest=[30, 40]
const [x, , z] = [1, 2, 3];         // skip items
let p = 1, q = 2;
[p, q] = [q, p];                    // swap

      Creating arrays

      js
      Array.from("hello")               // ["h","e","l","l","o"]
Array.from({ length: 5 }, (_, i) => i + 1) // [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
Array.of(7)                       // [7]
new Array(3).fill(0)              // [0, 0, 0]

      Practice (from the lecture)

      Difference between push and unshift?

      push adds to the end, unshift adds to the start. Both change the array and return the new length.

      Use map to add 5 to every value.

      arr.map(v => v + 5)

      Use filter to keep numbers greater than 10.

      arr.filter(v => v > 10)

      Use find to get the first number less than 10.

      arr.find(v => v < 10)

      Use reduce to find the maximum.

      arr.reduce((max, v) => v > max ? v : max, arr[0])

      Why does [100, 20, 3].sort() give a wrong order?

      Default sort converts items to strings and compares character by character. Pass (a, b) => a - b.

      Remove duplicates from an array.

      [...new Set(arr)]

      Count how many times each fruit appears in ["a","b","a"].
      js
      const counts = fruits.reduce((acc, f) => { acc[f] = (acc[f] || 0) + 1; return acc; }, {});
// { a: 2, b: 1 }

      Objects

      An object stores related data as key: value pairs. Arrays are for ordered lists; objects are for describing one thing.
      js
      const user = {
  name: "Harsh",          // key: value
  age: 26,
  isAdmin: false,
  "fav food": "poha",     // keys with spaces need quotes
  address: { city: "Bhopal", coords: { lat: 23.2, lng: 77.4 } },
  greet() { return `Hi, I'm ${this.name}`; }  // method
};

      Read, add, update, delete

      js
      user.name             // dot notation
user["fav food"]      // bracket notation (spaces, or dynamic keys)
const key = "age";
user[key]             // 26  ← dynamic key must use brackets
user.email = "h@x.com";   // add
user.age = 27;            // update
delete user.isAdmin;      // delete
"email" in user           // true
user.hasOwnProperty("age")// true
user.address.coords.lat   // 23.2  nested
user.phone?.number        // undefined, no crash (optional chaining)

      Shorthand and computed keys

      js
      const name = "Aman", age = 22;
const u = { name, age };                 // same as { name: name, age: age }
const field = "score";
const obj = { [field]: 90, [`${field}Max`]: 100 }; // { score: 90, scoreMax: 100 }

      Looping over objects

      js
      for (const key in user) console.log(key, user[key]);
Object.keys(user)     // ["name", "age", ...]
Object.values(user)   // ["Harsh", 26, ...]
Object.entries(user)  // [["name","Harsh"], ["age",26], ...]
for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(user)) console.log(k, v);
Object.fromEntries([["a", 1]])  // { a: 1 }

      Destructuring

      js
      const { name, age } = user;                 // pull out into variables
const { name: firstName } = user;           // rename → variable firstName
const { phone = "N/A" } = user;             // default value
const { address: { city } } = user;         // nested
const { name: n, ...others } = user;        // rest of the keys
function show({ name, age }) { /* destructure params */ }

      Copying: shallow vs deep

      Assigning an object just copies the reference. To make a real copy:

      js
      const orig = { a: 1, inner: { b: 2 } };

const s1 = { ...orig };             // shallow copy
const s2 = Object.assign({}, orig); // shallow copy
s1.inner.b = 99;                    // ⚠️ also changes orig.inner.b (nested object is shared)

const d1 = JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(orig)); // deep copy (loses functions, Dates, undefined)
const d2 = structuredClone(orig);            // ✅ modern deep copy

      Shallow copy copies the top level only; nested objects are still shared. Deep copy copies every level. JSON.stringify turns the object into a string (breaking all references), and JSON.parse builds a brand-new object from it.

      Freezing and sealing

      js
      Object.freeze(obj);   // can't add, delete or change (shallow)
Object.seal(obj);     // can change existing, can't add/delete
Object.isFrozen(obj);

      Methods and this (intro)

      Inside a method, this is the object before the dot: user.greet() → this is user. Arrow functions don't have their own this, so don't use them as object methods. Full rules in the this topic.

      Arrays of objects (you'll use this constantly)

      js
      const users = [
  { name: "A", age: 17 }, { name: "B", age: 22 }, { name: "C", age: 30 }
];
users.filter(u => u.age >= 18).map(u => u.name); // ["B", "C"]
users.find(u => u.name === "C");                  // { name: "C", age: 30 }

      Practice (from the lecture)

      From { location: { coordinates: { lat, lng } } } print the latitude.

      obj.location.coordinates.lat or const { location: { coordinates: { lat } } } = obj;

      Destructure firstName from { name: { first: "Harsh" } }.

      const { name: { first: firstName } } = obj;

      Why did changing the copy also change the original?

      You copied the reference (=) or made a shallow copy, so nested objects are shared. Use structuredClone or JSON parse/stringify.

      Print every key and value of an object.

      Object.entries(obj).forEach(([k, v]) => console.log(k, v));

      Strings

      Strings are immutable: every method returns a new string.

      js
      const s = "  Hello World  ";
s.length                 // 15
s[1]  /  s.at(-1)        // " " / " "
s.trim()                 // "Hello World"   (also trimStart / trimEnd)
s.toUpperCase()  s.toLowerCase()
s.includes("World")      // true
s.startsWith("  He")     // true    endsWith(...)
s.indexOf("o")           // 6       (-1 if missing)
s.slice(2, 7)            // "Hello"  (negative indexes allowed)
s.substring(2, 7)        // "Hello"
s.replace("World", "JS") // first match      replaceAll("l", "L")
"a,b,c".split(",")       // ["a", "b", "c"]
"ha".repeat(3)           // "hahaha"
"5".padStart(3, "0")     // "005"    padEnd(...)
"abc".charCodeAt(0)      // 97       String.fromCharCode(97) → "a"
"b".localeCompare("a")   // 1

      Template literals

      js
      const name = "Harsh", age = 26;
const msg = `My name is ${name} and next year I'll be ${age + 1}`;
const multi = `line one
line two`;                                   // multi-line strings

      Common tricks

      js
      "hello".split("").reverse().join("")   // "olleh"  reverse a string
const isPal = w => w === w.split("").reverse().join("");
"hello world".split(" ").map(w => w[0].toUpperCase() + w.slice(1)).join(" ") // Title Case

      Numbers, Math and Date

      Numbers

      js
      (3.14159).toFixed(2)     // "3.14"   (returns a string)
Number.isInteger(5)      // true
Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER  // 9007199254740991
0.1 + 0.2                // 0.30000000000000004  ⚠️ floating point
Math.abs(0.1 + 0.2 - 0.3) < Number.EPSILON  // true — safe comparison
(1234567.89).toLocaleString("en-IN")        // "12,34,567.89"
1_000_000                // numeric separators for readability

      Math

      js
      Math.round(4.5)  // 5     Math.floor(4.9) // 4     Math.ceil(4.1) // 5
Math.trunc(-4.7) // -4    Math.abs(-3)    // 3
Math.max(1, 9, 3) // 9    Math.min(...arr)
Math.pow(2, 3)   // 8     Math.sqrt(16)   // 4     Math.cbrt(27) // 3
Math.PI          // 3.14159…
Math.random()    // 0 ≤ x < 1

// random integer between min and max (inclusive)
const rand = (min, max) => Math.floor(Math.random() * (max - min + 1)) + min;
rand(1, 6); // dice

      Date

      js
      const now = new Date();
now.getFullYear()  now.getMonth()  // ⚠️ month is 0-based (0 = January)
now.getDate()      now.getDay()    // day of month / day of week (0 = Sunday)
now.getHours()     now.getMinutes()
now.getTime()      // milliseconds since 1 Jan 1970 (timestamp)
Date.now()         // same, without creating an object
new Date(2026, 0, 26)            // 26 Jan 2026
new Date("2026-09-18")
now.toLocaleDateString("en-IN")  // "18/9/2026"
now.toISOString()
// difference in days
const days = (d2 - d1) / (1000 * 60 * 60 * 24);
      Part 3

      Browser & DOM

      Lecture Part 2: making the page react. Select elements, change them, listen to events, validate forms, run timers and save data in the browser.

      What the DOM is

      DOM = Document Object Model. When the browser reads your HTML, it builds a tree of JavaScript objects. JS changes that tree, and the page updates. Almost everything you do to a page falls into four jobs: select → change content/attributes → change style → listen to events.
      text
      window
 └─ document
     └─ html
         ├─ head
         │   └─ title → "My page"
         └─ body
             ├─ h1 → "Hello"
             └─ ul
                 ├─ li → "One"
                 └─ li → "Two"

      Node types

      • Element nodes — tags like <h1>, <div>.
      • Text nodes — the text inside elements (even whitespace/newlines).
      • Comment nodes — <!-- … -->.
      • Attribute nodes — href, src, class (accessed via methods).
      • The document node is the root of it all.

      window, document, BOM

      window is the global object (the browser tab). It holds the BOM (Browser Object Model: location, history, navigator, screen, timers, storage) and the document (the DOM). Global vars and functions become properties of window.

      Four kinds of DOM manipulation

      1. Text/content: change "Hey, how are you?" to "Hi".
      2. Attributes: change an image's src, a link's href.
      3. Styles/classes: change colour, show/hide.
      4. Structure: create, add, remove elements.

      Selecting elements

      js
      document.getElementById("title")          // one element, by id — no # needed
document.getElementsByClassName("card")   // HTMLCollection (live)
document.getElementsByTagName("li")       // HTMLCollection (live)
document.querySelector("#title")          // FIRST match of any CSS selector
document.querySelector(".card")
document.querySelector("ul li:nth-child(2)")
document.querySelectorAll(".card")        // NodeList (static) of ALL matches
      getElementByIdquerySelector
      AcceptsJust the id, no #Any CSS selector (#id, .class, tag, combinations)
      ReturnsElement or nullFirst matching element or null
      SpeedSlightly fasterPlenty fast; more flexible

      NodeList vs HTMLCollection

      NodeList (querySelectorAll)HTMLCollection (getElementsBy…)
      Live?Static snapshotLive — updates when the DOM changes
      forEach✅ Yes❌ No — convert with Array.from() or [...]
      ContainsAny nodesElements only
      js
      document.querySelectorAll("li").forEach(li => console.log(li.textContent));
[...document.getElementsByClassName("card")].map(c => c.id);

      If a selector returns null, the element doesn't exist yet (script ran before HTML) or the selector is wrong. Use defer, and check the #/..

      Changing content and attributes

      textContent vs innerText vs innerHTML

      js
      const h1 = document.querySelector("h1");
h1.textContent = "Welcome to Sheryians";   // sets plain text
h1.innerHTML = "Hello <i>world</i>";        // parses HTML tags
h1.innerText                                 // visible text only (respects CSS)
      textContentinnerTextinnerHTML
      ReadsAll text, including hiddenOnly visible textThe HTML markup
      SpeedFastSlower (needs layout)Parses HTML
      Safe with user input?✅ Yes✅ Yes❌ XSS risk

      Never put user-typed text into innerHTML. Someone could type <img src=x onerror="stealCookies()">. Use textContent for text, and build elements with createElement.

      Attributes

      An attribute is anything extra written inside a tag: href, src, alt, id, class, lang, name, type…

      js
      const link = document.querySelector("a");
link.getAttribute("href");                  // read
link.setAttribute("href", "https://sheryians.com"); // write
link.removeAttribute("target");
link.hasAttribute("target");               // true / false

// Most attributes are also properties:
const img = document.querySelector("img");
img.src = "cat.jpg";
img.alt = "A cat";
input.value; input.checked; input.disabled = true;

      data-* attributes and dataset

      html
      <button data-user-id="42" data-role="admin">Edit</button>
      js
      btn.dataset.userId   // "42"   (kebab-case → camelCase)
btn.dataset.role = "editor";

      Creating, adding and removing elements

      js
      const li = document.createElement("li");   // 1. create
li.textContent = "New item";               // 2. fill
li.classList.add("item");
const ul = document.querySelector("ul");
ul.appendChild(li);                        // 3. attach (end)

ul.append(li, "text too");   // end; accepts many nodes and strings
ul.prepend(li);              // start
ul.before(li); ul.after(li); // as sibling
ul.replaceChildren();        // remove all children
li.replaceWith(otherEl);

li.remove();                 // remove itself (modern)
ul.removeChild(li);          // older way, from the parent

const clone = li.cloneNode(true);  // true = deep (with children)
ul.insertAdjacentHTML("beforeend", "<li>Quick</li>");
// positions: beforebegin | afterbegin | beforeend | afterend
      appendChildappendprepend
      Adds one node at the end, returns itAdds many nodes or strings at the endAdds at the start

      Adding many elements efficiently

      js
      const frag = document.createDocumentFragment();   // off-screen container
for (let i = 1; i <= 100; i++) {
  const li = document.createElement("li");
  li.textContent = `Item ${i}`;
  frag.appendChild(li);
}
ul.appendChild(frag);    // one DOM update instead of 100

      Tasks (from the lecture)

      Select the page heading by id and change its text to "Welcome to Sheryians".

      document.getElementById("heading").textContent = "Welcome to Sheryians";

      Create a new image with a placeholder src and add it at the top of a div.
      js
      const img = document.createElement("img");
img.src = "https://placehold.co/200";
document.querySelector("div").prepend(img);
      Add a new li "Learn JS" to the end of a ul.
      js
      const li = document.createElement("li");
li.textContent = "Learn JS";
document.querySelector("ul").appendChild(li);
      Remove the last item of a list.

      document.querySelector("ul li:last-child").remove();

      Styles and classes

      js
      const box = document.querySelector(".box");
box.style.backgroundColor = "red";   // CSS property in camelCase
box.style.fontSize = "24px";         // include units
box.style.display = "none";          // hide
box.style.cssText = "color:white; padding:10px;";
getComputedStyle(box).width          // the final applied value

box.classList.add("active");
box.classList.remove("active");
box.classList.toggle("active");      // add if missing, remove if present
box.classList.contains("active");    // true / false
box.classList.replace("old", "new");
box.className = "a b";               // overwrites all classes

      Prefer toggling classes over setting many inline styles. Keep the look in CSS, and let JS only switch states (.active, .hidden, .dark).

      Tasks

      Change the background colour of an element.

      el.style.backgroundColor = "tomato";

      Toggle a class "active" every time a button is clicked.
      js
      btn.addEventListener("click", () => box.classList.toggle("active"));

      Moving around the DOM tree

      js
      el.parentElement
el.children             // HTMLCollection of child elements
el.firstElementChild    el.lastElementChild
el.nextElementSibling   el.previousElementSibling
el.closest(".card")     // nearest ancestor (or itself) matching the selector
el.matches(".active")   // does el match this selector?
el.contains(otherEl)
// Node versions include text nodes too:
el.childNodes  el.firstChild  el.nextSibling  el.parentNode

      Events and event handling

      An event is something that happens (click, key press, typing, scrolling). You attach a listener to an element: "when X happens here, run this function".
      js
      const btn = document.querySelector("button");
btn.addEventListener("click", function (event) {
  console.log("clicked!", event);
});

// Removing needs the SAME function reference
function handle() { console.log("once"); }
btn.addEventListener("click", handle);
btn.removeEventListener("click", handle);

btn.addEventListener("click", handle, { once: true });   // auto-removes after first run

      Avoid btn.onclick = fn for real projects: it allows only one handler (a second assignment overwrites the first). addEventListener allows many.

      Common events

      GroupEvents
      Mouseclick, dblclick, contextmenu, mousedown, mouseup, mousemove, mouseenter/mouseleave (don't bubble), mouseover/mouseout (bubble)
      Keyboardkeydown, keyup (keypress is deprecated)
      Form / inputinput (every keystroke), change (after value is committed: select, checkbox, file), submit, focus, blur, reset
      Window / documentload, DOMContentLoaded, resize, scroll, beforeunload
      Touch / pointertouchstart, touchmove, pointerdown, pointermove
      Othercopy, paste, dragstart, drop, animationend, transitionend

      The event object

      The browser passes an event object to your function with details about what happened.

      js
      el.addEventListener("click", (e) => {
  e.type            // "click"
  e.target          // the exact element that was clicked
  e.currentTarget   // the element the listener is attached to
  e.clientX, e.clientY  // mouse position in the viewport
  e.preventDefault();   // stop the browser's default action
  e.stopPropagation();  // stop bubbling further up
});
document.addEventListener("keydown", (e) => {
  e.key     // "a", "Enter", "ArrowUp", " "
  e.code    // "KeyA", "Space"
  e.shiftKey, e.ctrlKey, e.altKey, e.metaKey
});
input.addEventListener("input", (e) => console.log(e.target.value));
select.addEventListener("change", (e) => console.log(e.target.value)); // chosen option

      preventDefault

      Some elements have built-in behaviour: a link navigates, a form submit reloads the page, right-click opens a menu. e.preventDefault() stops that so your JS can handle it.

      Mini builds from the lecture

      Live character counter

      js
      const input = document.querySelector("textarea");
const counter = document.querySelector("#count");
input.addEventListener("input", () => {
  counter.textContent = `${input.value.length} / 200`;
});

      Show the key you press, on screen (no input box)

      js
      const h1 = document.querySelector("h1");
window.addEventListener("keydown", (e) => {
  h1.textContent = e.key === " " ? "SPACE" : e.key;
});

      Custom file-upload button

      js
      const fileInput = document.querySelector("input[type=file]"); // hidden with CSS
const btn = document.querySelector("#upload");
btn.addEventListener("click", () => fileInput.click());      // open picker
fileInput.addEventListener("change", (e) => {
  const file = e.target.files[0];
  if (file) btn.textContent = file.name;
});

      Box that follows the mouse

      js
      const box = document.querySelector("#box");
window.addEventListener("mousemove", (e) => {
  box.style.left = e.clientX + "px";   // box has position: fixed/absolute
  box.style.top = e.clientY + "px";
});

      Double-click to "like"

      js
      card.addEventListener("dblclick", () => {
  heart.style.opacity = 1;
  setTimeout(() => heart.style.opacity = 0, 800);
});

      Bubbling, capturing and delegation

      Event propagation: three phases

      1. Capturing phase — the event travels down from window → document → … → the parent of the target.
      2. Target phase — it reaches the element you actually clicked.
      3. Bubbling phase — it travels back up through every ancestor to window.

      Listeners run in the bubbling phase by default. Phase 1 always happens first, even though you usually don't listen to it.

      js
      // <div id="outer"><button id="btn">Click</button></div>
outer.addEventListener("click", () => console.log("outer"));
btn.addEventListener("click", () => console.log("button"));
// click button → "button", then "outer"  (bubbling: inside → out)

outer.addEventListener("click", () => console.log("outer capture"), true);
// with capture true → "outer capture" fires BEFORE "button"

      Think of a stone dropped into a well: it goes down (capture), hits the bottom (target), then bubbles rise up to the surface (bubble).

      Stop it with e.stopPropagation() (parents won't hear it). Use sparingly; it can break other code relying on bubbling.

      Event delegation

      Instead of adding a listener to every child, add one listener to the parent and use e.target to see which child was clicked. Works for elements added later too.

      js
      const ul = document.querySelector("ul");
ul.addEventListener("click", (e) => {
  if (e.target.tagName === "LI") {
    e.target.classList.toggle("done");   // toggle strike-through
  }
  const del = e.target.closest(".delete");
  if (del) del.closest("li").remove();   // handle buttons inside items
});

      Why delegation? Less memory (one listener instead of hundreds), and it automatically handles elements created dynamically after page load.

      Practice

      target vs currentTarget?

      target = the element where the event started (what you clicked). currentTarget = the element whose listener is running (e.g. the ul in delegation).

      Which events don't bubble?

      focus, blur, mouseenter, mouseleave, load, scroll (on elements). Use focusin/focusout if you need bubbling versions.

      Forms and form validation

      Two jobs: stop the page reload on submit, then check each value and show a clear message if it's wrong.
      html
      <form id="signup" novalidate>
  <input id="name" name="name" placeholder="Name">
  <small class="error" id="nameErr"></small>
  <input id="email" name="email" type="email" placeholder="Email">
  <small class="error" id="emailErr"></small>
  <input id="pass" name="pass" type="password" placeholder="Password">
  <small class="error" id="passErr"></small>
  <button type="submit">Create account</button>
</form>
      js
      const form = document.querySelector("#signup");
form.addEventListener("submit", (e) => {
  e.preventDefault();                       // stop the reload

  const name = form.name.value.trim();      // or document.querySelector("#name").value
  const email = form.email.value.trim();
  const pass = form.pass.value;
  let valid = true;

  const show = (id, msg) => { document.querySelector(id).textContent = msg; if (msg) valid = false; };

  show("#nameErr", name.length < 4 ? "Name must be at least 4 characters" : "");
  show("#emailErr", /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/.test(email) ? "" : "Enter a valid email");
  show("#passErr", /^(?=.*[A-Z])(?=.*\d).{8,}$/.test(pass) ? "" : "8+ chars, one capital, one number");

  if (valid) {
    console.log("Submit", { name, email });
    form.reset();                           // clear the form
  }
});

      Reading form values

      js
      input.value                     // text, email, password, number (always a string!)
Number(input.value)             // convert when you need a number
input.valueAsNumber             // for type=number/range
checkbox.checked                // true / false
select.value                    // selected option's value
document.querySelector("input[name=gender]:checked")?.value  // radio group
fileInput.files[0]              // chosen file
const data = new FormData(form);
Object.fromEntries(data);       // { name: "...", email: "..." } in one go

      Built-in HTML validation

      HTML can validate before JS runs: required, minlength, maxlength, min, max, pattern, type="email|url|number". JS can read it via input.checkValidity(), input.validity, and set custom messages with input.setCustomValidity("…"). Add novalidate on the form when you want to show your own messages.

      Front-end validation is for user experience only. Anyone can bypass it, so the server must validate again.

      Lecture project: profile card generator

      A form (name, image URL, role, bio) → on submit, build a card with createElement, append it to a container, then clear all inputs.

      js
      form.addEventListener("submit", (e) => {
  e.preventDefault();
  const inputs = form.querySelectorAll("input");
  const [name, img, role] = [...inputs].map(i => i.value.trim());
  if (!name || !img) { errorEl.textContent = "Name and image are required"; return; }  // inline error, not alert()

  const card = document.createElement("div");
  card.className = "card";
  const pic = document.createElement("img");
  pic.src = img; pic.alt = name;
  const h3 = document.createElement("h3"); h3.textContent = name;
  const p = document.createElement("p");  p.textContent = role;
  card.append(pic, h3, p);
  document.querySelector(".cards").appendChild(card);

  inputs.forEach(i => (i.value = ""));    // clear everything
});

      Regular expressions (regex)

      A regex is a pattern for matching text. Used for validation, search and replace.

      js
      const re = /^[a-z0-9_.]{3,16}$/i;      // literal syntax (i = ignore case)
const re2 = new RegExp("^\\d+$");      // constructor (escape backslashes)
re.test("harsh_01")                    // true / false  ← most used for validation
"a1b2c3".match(/\d/g)                  // ["1", "2", "3"]
"2026-09-18".replace(/-/g, "/")        // "2026/09/18"
"hello world".split(/\s+/)
[..."a1b22".matchAll(/\d+/g)].map(m => m[0])  // ["1", "22"]
      SymbolMeaningSymbolMeaning
      ^ / $start / end of string.any character except newline
      \d / \Ddigit / not digit\w / \Wword char [A-Za-z0-9_] / not
      \s / \Swhitespace / not[abc] / [^abc]one of / none of
      *0 or more+1 or more
      ?0 or 1 (optional){3,8}between 3 and 8 times
      ( )group / capturea|ba or b
      (?=…)lookahead ("must contain")\.a literal dot (escape)

      Flags: g global (all matches), i ignore case, m multiline, s dot matches newline, u unicode.

      Handy validation patterns

      js
      const patterns = {
  username: /^[a-zA-Z0-9_.]{3,16}$/,                 // no @ or spaces
  email:    /^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/,
  phoneIN:  /^[6-9]\d{9}$/,                          // Indian mobile
  password: /^(?=.*[a-z])(?=.*[A-Z])(?=.*\d)(?=.*[^\w\s]).{8,}$/,
  pincode:  /^\d{6}$/,
  hexColor: /^#([0-9a-f]{3}){1,2}$/i
};
patterns.username.test("a.a");   // true
patterns.username.test("a@a");   // false

      Timers and intervals

      js
      // run ONCE after a delay (ms)
const t = setTimeout(() => console.log("after 2s"), 2000);
clearTimeout(t);               // cancel before it runs

// run REPEATEDLY every N ms
const id = setInterval(() => console.log("tick"), 1000);
clearInterval(id);             // stop it

setTimeout((name) => console.log(`Hi ${name}`), 500, "Harsh"); // extra args are passed in

      The delay is a minimum, not a guarantee. setTimeout(fn, 0) still waits until the current code finishes (see the event loop). Always store the id if you'll need to stop it.

      Lecture project: fake download progress bar

      js
      const bar = document.querySelector(".bar-fill");
const label = document.querySelector(".percent");
let progress = 0;
const id = setInterval(() => {
  progress++;
  bar.style.width = progress + "%";
  label.textContent = progress + "%";
  if (progress >= 100) {
    clearInterval(id);                 // stop at 100
    label.textContent = "Downloaded ✓";
  }
