}, 50);                                // 100 steps × 50ms = 5 seconds

      Countdown timer

      js
      let seconds = 10;
const timer = setInterval(() => {
  display.textContent = seconds;
  if (seconds-- === 0) { clearInterval(timer); display.textContent = "Time's up"; }
}, 1000);

      Debounce and throttle (performance patterns)

      Debounce: wait until the user stops doing something (e.g. typing in a search box) before running. Throttle: run at most once every N ms (e.g. scroll, resize, mousemove).

      js
      function debounce(fn, delay) {
  let timer;
  return (...args) => {
    clearTimeout(timer);                       // reset on each call
    timer = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), delay);
  };
}
function throttle(fn, limit) {
  let waiting = false;
  return (...args) => {
    if (waiting) return;
    fn(...args);
    waiting = true;
    setTimeout(() => (waiting = false), limit);
  };
}
search.addEventListener("input", debounce(e => fetchResults(e.target.value), 400));
window.addEventListener("scroll", throttle(() => console.log("scroll"), 200));

      For smooth animation use requestAnimationFrame(fn) instead of setInterval: it syncs with the screen refresh.

      localStorage, sessionStorage and cookies

      All three store data in the browser. They differ in how long the data lives, how much fits, and whether it's sent to the server.
      localStoragesessionStorageCookies
      LifetimeUntil deleted (survives restarts)Until the tab is closed (survives reloads)Until the expiry date you set (or browser close)
      Size~5–10 MB~5 MB~4 KB per cookie
      Sent to server?NoNoYes, with every request
      Shared across tabs?Yes (same site)No, per tabYes
      Good forTheme, saved tasks, cartMulti-step form progress, one-time stateLogin sessions, server-read preferences

      Web Storage API (same for both)

      js
      localStorage.setItem("name", "Harsh");
localStorage.getItem("name");      // "Harsh"   (null if missing)
localStorage.removeItem("name");
localStorage.clear();              // wipe everything for this site
localStorage.length; localStorage.key(0);

sessionStorage.setItem("step", "2");   // identical API

      See your data in DevTools → Application tab → Storage.

      Storing arrays and objects: JSON

      Storage can only hold strings. Convert with JSON.

      js
      const tasks = [{ id: 1, text: "Learn DOM", done: false }];
localStorage.setItem("tasks", JSON.stringify(tasks));   // object → string

const saved = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("tasks")) || [];  // string → object
// "|| []" handles the first visit when nothing is saved (getItem returns null)

      Forgot JSON.stringify? You'll store "[object Object]". Forgot JSON.parse? You'll get a string, not an array. Wrap JSON.parse in try…catch if the data might be corrupted.

      Cookies

      js
      document.cookie = "username=harsh; max-age=3600; path=/";   // expires in 1 hour
document.cookie = "theme=dark; expires=Fri, 31 Dec 2027 23:59:59 GMT; path=/";
document.cookie;          // "username=harsh; theme=dark"  (one string for all)
document.cookie = "username=; max-age=0; path=/";            // delete

const getCookie = name =>
  document.cookie.split("; ").find(c => c.startsWith(name + "="))?.split("=")[1];

      Security flags (set by the server mostly): Secure (HTTPS only), HttpOnly (JS can't read it — protects login tokens), SameSite=Strict|Lax (CSRF protection).

      Lecture project: dark/light theme that remembers

      js
      const btn = document.querySelector("#toggleTheme");
const saved = localStorage.getItem("theme") || "light";
document.body.classList.toggle("dark", saved === "dark");   // apply on load (every page)

btn.addEventListener("click", () => {
  const isDark = document.body.classList.toggle("dark");
  localStorage.setItem("theme", isDark ? "dark" : "light");
});

      The storage event

      Other open tabs of the same site get window.addEventListener("storage", e => …) when localStorage changes. Useful to sync themes/carts across tabs.

      Template: task manager with localStorage

      js
      const form = document.querySelector("#taskForm");
const list = document.querySelector("#tasks");
let tasks = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("tasks")) || [];

const save = () => localStorage.setItem("tasks", JSON.stringify(tasks));

function render() {
  list.innerHTML = "";                              // clear, then rebuild from data
  tasks.forEach(t => {
    const li = document.createElement("li");
    li.dataset.id = t.id;
    li.className = t.done ? "done" : "";
    li.innerHTML = `<span></span><button class="del">Delete</button>`;
    li.querySelector("span").textContent = t.text; // safe text
    list.appendChild(li);
  });
}

form.addEventListener("submit", e => {
  e.preventDefault();
  const text = form.task.value.trim();
  if (!text) return;
  tasks.push({ id: Date.now(), text, done: false });   // Create
  save(); render(); form.reset();
});

list.addEventListener("click", e => {                  // delegation
  const id = Number(e.target.closest("li")?.dataset.id);
  if (!id) return;
  if (e.target.matches(".del")) tasks = tasks.filter(t => t.id !== id);            // Delete
  else tasks = tasks.map(t => t.id === id ? { ...t, done: !t.done } : t);         // Update
  save(); render();
});

render();                                               // Read on page load

      Pattern to remember: data is the source of truth. Change the array → save → re-render. Never treat the DOM itself as your data.

      Part 4

      Advanced JavaScript

      How code actually runs, this, prototypes and classes, asynchronous code and the event loop, errors, modules and the modern toolbox. This is the core of every JavaScript interview at 3 YOE.

      Execution context and the call stack

      How JS runs your file: it creates an execution context in two phases, memory first, then code. Function calls are stacked and unstacked on the call stack.

      Execution context

      1. Memory creation phase — JS scans the code: var variables get undefined, let/const are reserved but uninitialised (TDZ), function declarations are stored entirely. This is why hoisting happens.
      2. Code execution phase — runs line by line, assigning values and calling functions.

      There's one Global Execution Context, and every function call creates a new Function Execution Context with its own memory, scope chain and this.

      Call stack

      js
      function one() { two(); console.log("one done"); }
function two() { three(); console.log("two done"); }
function three() { console.log("three"); }
one();
// stack grows: global → one → two → three
// then shrinks: three done, two done, one done  (Last In, First Out)

      Infinite recursion keeps pushing until you get RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded (stack overflow). You can see the stack in DevTools → Sources → breakpoints.

      Debugging tools

      • debugger; statement pauses execution when DevTools is open.
      • Breakpoints (click a line number in Sources), step over / into / out, watch variables.
      • console.trace() prints the current call stack; console.time("x") / console.timeEnd("x") measure speed.

      The this keyword, call, apply, bind

      this is decided by how a function is called, not where it's written — except arrow functions, which borrow this from where they are written.
      How it's calledthis is…
      obj.method()obj (the thing before the dot)
      Plain fn()undefined in strict mode / modules; window in sloppy mode
      new Fn()the new object being created
      fn.call(x) / apply / bindx, whatever you pass
      Arrow functionthis of the surrounding scope (lexical)
      DOM listener with functionthe element (e.currentTarget)
      Global scopewindow (browser)
      js
      const user = {
  name: "Harsh",
  normal() { return this.name; },          // "Harsh"
  arrow: () => this.name,                  // undefined — arrow looks outside the object
  later() {
    setTimeout(function () { console.log(this.name); }, 0); // undefined (plain call)
    setTimeout(() => console.log(this.name), 0);            // "Harsh" ✅ arrow borrows
  }
};
const fn = user.normal;
fn();          // undefined — lost its "before the dot"

      call, apply, bind

      js
      function intro(city, country) { return `${this.name} from ${city}, ${country}`; }
const p = { name: "Aman" };

intro.call(p, "Bhopal", "India");     // call: args one by one
intro.apply(p, ["Bhopal", "India"]);  // apply: args as an array
const bound = intro.bind(p, "Bhopal"); // bind: returns a NEW function, doesn't run
bound("India");

button.addEventListener("click", user.normal.bind(user)); // fix lost this

      call vs apply vs bind: call and apply run immediately (call takes a comma list, apply takes an array). bind returns a new function with this fixed for later.

      OOP: constructors, prototypes and classes

      Constructor functions and new

      js
      function Person(name, age) {
  this.name = name;
  this.age = age;
}
Person.prototype.greet = function () { return `Hi, I'm ${this.name}`; };

const p1 = new Person("Harsh", 26);
p1.greet();

      new does four things: creates an empty object → links it to Person.prototype → runs the function with this = that object → returns it.

      Prototypes and the prototype chain

      Every object has a hidden link ([[Prototype]], visible as __proto__) to another object. If a property isn't found on the object, JS looks up this chain. That's how [1,2].map works: map lives on Array.prototype.

      text
      p1 ──▶ Person.prototype ──▶ Object.prototype ──▶ null
       (greet)              (toString, hasOwnProperty)
      js
      Object.getPrototypeOf(p1) === Person.prototype  // true
const child = Object.create(p1);                 // new object whose prototype is p1

      Classes (ES6 syntax over prototypes)

      js
      class Animal {
  #secret = "hidden";               // private field (only usable inside the class)
  static count = 0;                 // belongs to the class, not instances

  constructor(name) {
    this.name = name;
    Animal.count++;
  }
  speak() { return `${this.name} makes a sound`; }   // goes on the prototype
  get info() { return `Animal: ${this.name}`; }      // getter: use like a property
  set rename(v) { if (v) this.name = v; }             // setter
  static create(name) { return new Animal(name); }    // Animal.create("x")
  #privateMethod() {}
}

class Dog extends Animal {          // inheritance
  constructor(name, breed) {
    super(name);                    // must call parent constructor first
    this.breed = breed;
  }
  speak() {                         // override
    return super.speak() + " — woof";
  }
}
const d = new Dog("Tommy", "Lab");
d.speak(); d.info; d instanceof Animal; // true

      The four OOP pillars

      • Encapsulation — keep data and the methods that use it together; hide internals (#private, closures).
      • Abstraction — expose a simple interface, hide complexity.
      • Inheritance — extends to reuse behaviour.
      • Polymorphism — the same method name behaves differently per class (method overriding).

      Getters/setters and property descriptors on plain objects

      js
      const temp = {
  _c: 25,
  get f() { return this._c * 9 / 5 + 32; },
  set f(v) { this._c = (v - 32) * 5 / 9; }
};
Object.defineProperty(obj, "id", { value: 1, writable: false, enumerable: false });

      Asynchronous JS and the event loop

      JS does one thing at a time, yet it handles timers, clicks and network requests without freezing. The browser does the waiting; the event loop hands results back to JS when the stack is free.

      Synchronous vs asynchronous

      js
      console.log("1");
setTimeout(() => console.log("2"), 0);
console.log("3");
// 1, 3, 2  → the timer callback waits until the main code finishes

      The moving parts

      • Call stack — where your code runs, one frame at a time.
      • Web APIs — provided by the browser: timers, DOM events, fetch. They wait in the background.
      • Callback (task / macrotask) queue — finished timers and events wait here.
      • Microtask queue — promise callbacks (.then, await continuations), queueMicrotask. Higher priority.
      • Event loop — when the stack is empty: run all microtasks, then one macrotask, render if needed, repeat.
      js
      console.log("A");
setTimeout(() => console.log("B"), 0);          // macrotask
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log("C")); // microtask
console.log("D");
// A, D, C, B

      Classic question: order of sync code, setTimeout and Promise.then. Answer: sync first, then microtasks (promises), then macrotasks (timers).

      Callbacks and callback hell

      js
      getUser(1, (user) => {
  getPosts(user.id, (posts) => {
    getComments(posts[0].id, (comments) => {
      console.log(comments);       // "pyramid of doom": hard to read and handle errors
    });
  });
});

      Promises and async/await were created to flatten this.

      Promises

      A promise is an object for a value that will arrive later. It's either pending, then fulfilled (success, with a value) or rejected (failure, with a reason). Once settled, it never changes.
      js
      const order = new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
  const cooked = true;
  setTimeout(() => {
    cooked ? resolve("🍕 Pizza ready") : reject(new Error("Kitchen closed"));
  }, 1000);
});

order
  .then(food => { console.log(food); return "eat"; })  // runs on success; return passes on
  .then(next => console.log(next))                     // chaining
  .catch(err => console.error(err.message))            // any error above lands here
  .finally(() => console.log("done either way"));

      Turning a callback API into a promise

      js
      const wait = ms => new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve, ms));
wait(1000).then(() => console.log("1 second later"));

      Running many promises

      MethodResolves whenRejects when
      Promise.all([...])all succeed → array of valuesany one fails (fail-fast)
      Promise.allSettled([...])all finish → array of {status, value|reason}never
      Promise.race([...])first to settle succeedsfirst to settle fails
      Promise.any([...])first successall fail (AggregateError)
      js
      const [users, posts] = await Promise.all([fetchUsers(), fetchPosts()]); // run in parallel

      async/await and fetch

      async / await

      async makes a function always return a promise. await pauses that function (not the whole page) until a promise settles. It reads like normal top-to-bottom code.

      js
      async function loadUser() {
  try {
    const res = await fetch("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users/1");
    if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`);   // fetch doesn't reject on 404/500!
    const user = await res.json();
    console.log(user.name);
  } catch (err) {
    console.error("Failed:", err.message);
  } finally {
    spinner.hidden = true;
  }
}
loadUser();

      fetch only rejects on network failure. A 404 or 500 is still a "successful" response, so always check res.ok.

      Awaiting in a loop runs requests one after another. For independent requests, start them together: await Promise.all(ids.map(id => fetch(url + id))). forEach doesn't wait for await; use for…of.

      fetch in detail

      js
      // GET
const res = await fetch("/api/tasks?done=false");
const data = await res.json();       // also: res.text(), res.blob(), res.formData()

// POST JSON
await fetch("/api/tasks", {
  method: "POST",                    // PUT, PATCH, DELETE
  headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json", Authorization: "Bearer TOKEN" },
  body: JSON.stringify({ text: "Learn fetch" })
});

// Upload a file / form
await fetch("/upload", { method: "POST", body: new FormData(form) });

// Cancel / timeout
const ctrl = new AbortController();
setTimeout(() => ctrl.abort(), 5000);
await fetch(url, { signal: ctrl.signal });

      JSON

      JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) is the text format APIs use. Keys must be in double quotes; no functions, undefined or comments.

      js
      JSON.stringify({ a: 1, b: [1, 2] })           // '{"a":1,"b":[1,2]}'
JSON.stringify(obj, null, 2)                  // pretty-printed
JSON.parse('{"a":1}')                         // { a: 1 }

      Other ways you'll see

      XMLHttpRequest (old, callback-based), axios (popular library: auto JSON, rejects on HTTP errors), top-level await in modules.

      Error handling

      js
      try {
  const data = JSON.parse(badString);   // may throw
} catch (err) {
  console.error(err.name, err.message); // SyntaxError, "Unexpected token…"
} finally {
  console.log("always runs");
}

function withdraw(amount) {
  if (amount <= 0) throw new Error("Amount must be positive");  // throw your own
}

class ValidationError extends Error {       // custom error type
  constructor(field, msg) { super(msg); this.name = "ValidationError"; this.field = field; }
}
try { throw new ValidationError("email", "Invalid email"); }
catch (e) { if (e instanceof ValidationError) showError(e.field, e.message); else throw e; }
      ErrorWhen
      ReferenceErrorUsing a variable that doesn't exist or is in the TDZ
      TypeErrorWrong type: calling a non-function, reading a property of undefined/null, reassigning a const
      SyntaxErrorInvalid code or invalid JSON
      RangeErrorValue out of range (stack overflow, bad array length, toFixed(200))
      URIError, EvalError, AggregateErrorRare

      Global safety nets: window.addEventListener("error", …) and window.addEventListener("unhandledrejection", …).

      Modules (import / export)

      Modules split code into files. Each module has its own scope; nothing leaks unless exported.

      js
      // math.js
export const PI = 3.14;                      // named export
export function add(a, b) { return a + b; }
export default function multiply(a, b) { return a * b; }  // one default per file

// app.js
import multiply, { PI, add as sum } from "./math.js";
import * as MathUtils from "./math.js";
const { heavy } = await import("./heavy.js");  // dynamic import (lazy load)
      html
      <script type="module" src="app.js"></script>
      • Modules are strict mode automatically and deferred by default.
      • They need a server (Live Server), not a file:// path.
      • Node's older system is CommonJS: module.exports = … / require("…").
      • npm packages + a bundler (Vite, webpack) is how larger apps combine modules.

      Map, Set, WeakMap, WeakSet

      js
      // Set: unique values
const s = new Set([1, 2, 2, 3]);   // {1, 2, 3}
s.add(4); s.has(2); s.delete(1); s.size;
const unique = [...new Set(arr)];

// Map: key-value pairs where keys can be ANY type, order is kept
const m = new Map();
m.set("name", "Harsh");
m.set(domElement, { clicks: 0 });  // object as key
m.get("name"); m.has("name"); m.delete("name"); m.size;
for (const [k, v] of m) console.log(k, v);
new Map(Object.entries(obj));      // object → map
      ObjectMap
      Key typesStrings/symbols onlyAnything
      SizeObject.keys(o).lengthmap.size
      IterationNeeds Object.entriesDirectly iterable
      Best forFixed shapes, JSONFrequent add/remove, lookups

      WeakMap / WeakSet only hold objects and don't prevent them from being garbage-collected. Use them to attach private data to objects (e.g. DOM nodes) without memory leaks. They're not iterable.

      Iterators and generators

      An iterable is anything for…of and spread can loop over (arrays, strings, Maps, Sets, NodeLists). It has a [Symbol.iterator] method returning an iterator with next() → { value, done }.

      js
      function* idGenerator() {      // function* = generator
  let id = 1;
  while (true) yield id++;     // yield pauses and hands out a value
}
const gen = idGenerator();
gen.next().value; // 1
gen.next().value; // 2

function* range(start, end, step = 1) {
  for (let i = start; i <= end; i += step) yield i;
}
[...range(1, 10, 3)]  // [1, 4, 7, 10]

const custom = {
  *[Symbol.iterator]() { yield "a"; yield "b"; }
};
for (const x of custom) console.log(x);

      Async versions: async function* and for await (const chunk of stream).

      Modern features by version

      A map of what arrived when, so you recognise "new" syntax in code you read.

      VersionKey additions
      ES5 (2009)"use strict", forEach/map/filter/reduce, JSON, Object.keys, getters/setters, Array.isArray
      ES6 / ES2015let/const, arrow functions, classes, template literals, destructuring, default/rest/spread, promises, modules, Map/Set, symbols, iterators, generators, for…of
      ES2016**, Array.prototype.includes
      ES2017async/await, Object.values/entries, padStart/padEnd
      ES2018object rest/spread, Promise.finally, async iteration, regex named groups
      ES2019flat/flatMap, Object.fromEntries, trimStart/End, optional catch binding
      ES2020?., ??, BigInt, Promise.allSettled, globalThis, dynamic import()
      ES2021replaceAll, Promise.any, ||= &&= ??=, numeric separators 1_000
      ES2022class #private fields, static blocks, .at(), top-level await, Object.hasOwn, error cause
      ES2023findLast, toSorted/toReversed/toSpliced/with
      ES2024+Object.groupBy, Promise.withResolvers, new Set methods (union, intersection…)
      js
      const byAge = Object.groupBy(users, u => (u.age >= 18 ? "adult" : "minor"));

      Functional patterns worth knowing

      js
      // Currying: f(a, b, c) → f(a)(b)(c)
const add = a => b => c => a + b + c;
add(1)(2)(3); // 6

// Composition
const compose = (...fns) => x => fns.reduceRight((v, f) => f(v), x);

// Memoisation (cache results using a closure)
function memo(fn) {
  const cache = {};
  return n => cache[n] ?? (cache[n] = fn(n));
}

// Tagged template
const tag = (strings, ...vals) => strings.reduce((a, s, i) => a + s + (vals[i] ? `[${vals[i]}]` : ""), "");
tag`Hi ${"Harsh"}!`;  // "Hi [Harsh]!"

      Proxy and Reflect (metaprogramming)

      js
      const user = new Proxy({ age: 20 }, {
  set(target, key, value) {
    if (key === "age" && value < 0) throw new Error("Age can't be negative");
    return Reflect.set(target, key, value);
  }
});

      Browser APIs you'll reach for

      js
      // location (URL)
location.href; location.pathname; location.search; location.reload();
location.href = "/login";                         // navigate
new URLSearchParams(location.search).get("id");   // ?id=5 → "5"

// history (single-page apps)
history.back(); history.forward();
history.pushState({}, "", "/profile");            // change URL without reload

// navigator
navigator.userAgent; navigator.language; navigator.onLine;
navigator.clipboard.writeText("copied!");
navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(pos => console.log(pos.coords.latitude));

// window size & scroll
innerWidth; innerHeight; scrollY;
window.scrollTo({ top: 0, behavior: "smooth" });
el.scrollIntoView({ behavior: "smooth" });
el.getBoundingClientRect();                       // position & size on screen
matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches;

      Observers

      js
      // Lazy load / reveal on scroll
const io = new IntersectionObserver(entries => {
  entries.forEach(e => { if (e.isIntersecting) e.target.classList.add("show"); });
}, { threshold: 0.2 });
document.querySelectorAll(".reveal").forEach(el => io.observe(el));

new ResizeObserver(entries => {/* element size changed */}).observe(box);
new MutationObserver(changes => {/* DOM changed */}).observe(list, { childList: true });

      More APIs, one line each

      • Notification, Web Audio, Canvas (getContext("2d")) for drawing, Web Workers for heavy work off the main thread.
      • IndexedDB for large structured storage; Cache API + Service Workers for offline apps (PWAs).
      • WebSocket for real-time two-way communication (chats, live scores).
      • FileReader / URL.createObjectURL(file) to preview uploaded images.
      • Drag and drop: draggable="true" + dragstart, dragover (call preventDefault), drop.
      js
      // Preview an uploaded image
fileInput.addEventListener("change", e => {
  preview.src = URL.createObjectURL(e.target.files[0]);
});

      Strict mode, memory and good habits

      Strict mode

      "use strict"; at the top of a file or function turns silent mistakes into errors: undeclared variables throw, this in plain functions is undefined, duplicate parameter names are banned, writing to read-only properties throws. Modules and classes are strict automatically.

      Memory and garbage collection

      JS frees memory automatically when nothing references a value any more (mark-and-sweep). Leaks happen when you keep references by accident:

      • Intervals you never clear.
      • Event listeners on removed elements that are still referenced.
      • Growing global arrays/caches.
      • Closures holding large objects you no longer need.

      Habits that keep code clean

      • const by default, === always, meaningful names.
      • Small functions that do one thing; early returns over nesting.
      • Don't mutate data you received; return new arrays/objects (map, spread).
      • Keep data as the source of truth and render the DOM from it.
      • Handle errors and empty states (|| [], ?., try…catch).
      • Use a formatter (Prettier) and linter (ESLint).

      What comes after this

      TypeScript (types on top of JS), a framework (React / Vue / Svelte), Node.js + Express for back end, Git, testing (Vitest/Jest), and build tools (Vite).

      Part

      How the web works

      What happens between typing a URL and seeing pixels, HTTP, APIs, CORS, caching, authentication and real-time communication. Asked in almost every frontend interview.

      What happens when you type a URL

      1. URL parsing — the browser decides whether it's a URL or a search, and checks HSTS (force HTTPS).
      2. Cache check — service worker, then HTTP cache. A fresh cached response skips the network.
      3. DNS lookup — domain → IP address (browser cache → OS → router → ISP resolver → root → TLD → authoritative server).
      4. Connection — TCP handshake (SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK), then TLS handshake for HTTPS (certificate check, key exchange). HTTP/3 uses QUIC over UDP and combines these steps.
      5. HTTP request — GET / with headers (cookies, Accept, User-Agent).
      6. Server — often a CDN edge first; the origin server runs code and responds with HTML.
      7. Parsing — HTML → DOM; CSS → CSSOM. Scripts without defer/async block parsing.
      8. Render tree — DOM + CSSOM, only visible nodes.
      9. Layout (reflow) — calculate size and position of every box.
      10. Paint — fill pixels (text, colours, images, borders) into layers.
      11. Composite — the GPU combines layers into the final frame.
      12. Interactive — JS runs, event handlers attach (hydration in frameworks); more resources load.

      Critical rendering path — what blocks the first paint

      • CSS is render-blocking: the browser won't paint until it has the CSSOM. Keep critical CSS small.
      • Sync JS is parser-blocking: use defer / type="module".
      • Fonts can delay text: font-display: swap, preload key fonts.
      • Hints: <link rel="preconnect">, rel="preload", fetchpriority="high" on the hero image.

      Reflow vs repaint

      Reflow/layout recalculates geometry (changing width, font size, adding elements, reading offsetHeight after a write). Repaint redraws without geometry changes (colour, visibility). Composite-only changes (transform, opacity) are cheapest. Avoid layout thrashing: batch DOM reads, then writes.

      HTTP essentials

      Methods

      MethodMeaningSafe / idempotent
      GETReadSafe, idempotent
      POSTCreate / perform an actionNeither
      PUTReplace a resourceIdempotent
      PATCHPartially updateNot guaranteed
      DELETEDeleteIdempotent
      OPTIONSAsk what's allowed (CORS preflight)Safe

      Idempotent = doing it twice has the same effect as once (safe to retry).

      Status codes

      RangeCommon codes
      2xx success200 OK, 201 Created, 204 No Content
      3xx redirect301 Moved Permanently, 302/307 Temporary, 304 Not Modified (use your cache)
      4xx client error400 Bad Request, 401 Unauthorized (not logged in), 403 Forbidden (logged in, not allowed), 404 Not Found, 409 Conflict, 422 Unprocessable (validation), 429 Too Many Requests
      5xx server error500 Internal, 502 Bad Gateway, 503 Unavailable, 504 Timeout

      Headers you'll see

      • Request: Content-Type, Accept, Authorization: Bearer …, Cookie, Origin, If-None-Match.
      • Response: Content-Type, Set-Cookie, Cache-Control, ETag, Location, Access-Control-Allow-Origin, Content-Security-Policy.

      HTTP versions

      HTTP/1.1: one request at a time per connection (browsers open ~6). HTTP/2: many requests multiplexed on one connection, header compression. HTTP/3: over QUIC (UDP), faster connection setup, better on flaky mobile networks. HTTPS = HTTP over TLS: encryption, integrity, server identity.

      Inspect all of this in DevTools → Network tab: filter by Fetch/XHR, check headers, payload, timing, throttle to "Slow 4G".

      APIs: REST, GraphQL and friends

      REST

      text
      GET    /api/products            list (with ?page=2&limit=20&sort=price&q=shoe)
GET    /api/products/42         one
POST   /api/products            create   (JSON body)
PATCH  /api/products/42         update some fields
DELETE /api/products/42         delete
GET    /api/users/7/orders      nested resource

      GraphQL

      One endpoint; the client asks for exactly the fields it needs. Solves over-fetching and multiple round trips; adds complexity (schema, caching). Clients: Apollo, urql, Relay.

      graphql
      query {
  user(id: 7) { name avatar orders(last: 3) { id total } }
}

      Other styles

      tRPC (type-safe function calls between a TS client and server), gRPC (service-to-service), and in Next.js, Server Actions.

      Handling API data well on the frontend

      • Every request has four UI states: idle, loading, success, error — plus empty.
      • Pagination: offset (?page=3) or cursor (?after=abc, better for infinite scroll).
      • Cancel stale requests (AbortController), debounce search, retry with backoff on network errors.
      • Validate responses you don't control (Zod) and handle 401 globally (redirect to login / refresh token).

      Same-origin policy and CORS

      An origin = protocol + host + port. https://app.com and https://api.app.com are different origins. The browser's same-origin policy stops a page from reading responses from another origin, unless that server opts in with CORS headers.

      text
      Browser (https://app.com) ──GET https://api.shop.com/products──▶ server
                                     Origin: https://app.com
server responds with:  Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.com   ← browser lets JS read it
      • Simple requests (GET/POST with basic headers) go straight through; the browser checks the response header.
      • Preflight: for PUT/DELETE, JSON Content-Type, or custom headers, the browser first sends OPTIONS asking permission (Access-Control-Allow-Methods, -Headers).
      • Sending cookies cross-origin needs credentials: "include" on fetch and Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true plus an exact origin (not *).

      "How do you fix a CORS error?" CORS is enforced by the browser and fixed on the server (allow your origin). On the frontend you can proxy requests through your own server/dev server (Vite server.proxy, Next.js route handler or rewrites) so the browser sees same-origin. Postman works because it isn't a browser.

      Browser caching and CDNs

      text
      Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable   → hashed assets (app.3f9a1.js): cache for a year
Cache-Control: no-cache                              → store, but revalidate every time (HTML)
Cache-Control: no-store                              → never store (sensitive data)
Cache-Control: private, max-age=60                   → only the browser, not shared caches
ETag: "abc123"  →  next request sends If-None-Match: "abc123"  →  304 Not Modified (no body)
stale-while-revalidate=60                            → serve stale while fetching fresh in background

      Build tools put a content hash in file names, so you can cache JS/CSS forever and still ship updates (new hash = new URL). HTML should revalidate.

      A CDN (Cloudflare, Vercel Edge, CloudFront) stores copies of your assets on servers worldwide, so users download from a nearby location.

      Storage recap (browser side)

      Cookies (4 KB, sent to server), localStorage (persistent), sessionStorage (per tab), IndexedDB (large structured data), Cache Storage (service worker offline cache). See the Storage topic in Browser & DOM.

      Authentication on the web

      Session cookieToken (JWT) in header
      HowServer stores session; browser holds an id in an HttpOnly cookie, sent automaticallyServer signs a token; client sends Authorization: Bearer <jwt>
      XSS riskLow (JS can't read HttpOnly)High if stored in localStorage
      CSRF riskNeeds SameSite / CSRF tokensLow (not sent automatically)
      Logout / revokeEasy: delete sessionHard until expiry (use short-lived access + refresh tokens)
      Good forWeb apps on your own domainMobile apps, third-party APIs, microservices

      A JWT has three base64 parts: header.payload.signature. The payload is readable by anyone (not encrypted); the signature proves it wasn't changed. Never put secrets in it.

      OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect ("Sign in with Google")

      1. Your app redirects to Google with your client id and a redirect URL.
      2. User logs in and consents on Google.
      3. Google redirects back with a short-lived code.
      4. Your server exchanges the code (+ client secret, + PKCE verifier) for tokens.
      5. You create your own session for the user.

      Use a library (Auth.js, Better Auth, Clerk, Firebase/Supabase Auth) rather than building this yourself.

      Real-time communication

      TechniqueHowUse for
      Short pollingsetInterval(fetch, 5000)Simple, low-frequency updates
      Long pollingServer holds the request open until there's dataFallback when others aren't available
      Server-Sent Eventsnew EventSource(url): server → client stream over HTTPNotifications, live feeds, AI token streaming
      WebSocketnew WebSocket("wss://…"): full two-way connectionChat, multiplayer, collaborative editing, trading
      WebRTCPeer-to-peer media/dataVideo calls, screen sharing
      js
      const ws = new WebSocket("wss://chat.example.com");
ws.addEventListener("open", () => ws.send(JSON.stringify({ type: "join", room: "general" })));
ws.addEventListener("message", e => addMessage(JSON.parse(e.data)));
ws.addEventListener("close", () => setTimeout(reconnect, 1000));   // reconnect with backoff

const es = new EventSource("/api/notifications");
es.onmessage = e => showToast(JSON.parse(e.data));

      Libraries: Socket.IO, Pusher, Ably, Supabase Realtime, Liveblocks (collaboration).

      Part

      TypeScript

      JavaScript with types. Most frontend job posts list it; React, Next.js and Vue projects default to it.

      Why TypeScript, and the basic types

      TypeScript checks your code before it runs. Typos, wrong arguments, missing fields and undefined access become red squiggles in the editor instead of bugs in production. Types are removed at build time; the browser runs plain JS.
      bash
      npm install -D typescript
npx tsc --init         # creates tsconfig.json
npx tsc --noEmit       # type-check the project
# Vite/Next templates come with TS already set up (react-ts, create-next-app)
      ts
      let username: string = "Harsh";
let age = 26;                        // inferred as number — don't annotate what TS can infer
let isAdmin: boolean = false;
let tags: string[] = ["js", "ts"];   // or Array<string>
let point: [number, number] = [10, 20];  // tuple
let anything: any;                   // turns checking OFF — avoid
let input: unknown;                  // safe "any": must narrow before use
let nothing: null = null;
let maybe: string | undefined;

function greet(name: string, greeting = "Hi"): string {
  return `${greeting}, ${name}`;
}
function log(msg: string): void { console.log(msg); }
function fail(msg: string): never { throw new Error(msg); }

const user: { name: string; email?: string; readonly id: number } = { name: "A", id: 1 };
//                              ?  optional        readonly can't be reassigned

      Turn on "strict": true in tsconfig.json. It enables strictNullChecks, which catches the most common real bug: using something that may be undefined.

      Types, interfaces, unions and narrowing

      ts
      // type alias
type User = { id: number; name: string; role: "admin" | "user" };   // literal union
// interface
interface Product { id: number; title: string; price: number }
interface DigitalProduct extends Product { downloadUrl: string }     // extend
type WithTimestamps<T> = T & { createdAt: Date };                   // intersection

type Status = "idle" | "loading" | "success" | "error";
type Id = string | number;
      typeinterface
      Can describe anything: unions, tuples, primitives, mapped typesOnly object shapes (and classes)
      Combine with &Combine with extends; can be re-opened (declaration merging)
      For object shapes either works. Pick one convention per codebase (many use type for everything in React).

      Narrowing: proving the type to TypeScript

      ts
      function format(id: string | number) {
  if (typeof id === "string") return id.toUpperCase();   // here id: string
  return id.toFixed(0);                                   // here id: number
}
if (Array.isArray(x)) {}           if (err instanceof Error) {}
if ("downloadUrl" in product) {}   if (user?.email) {}

// Discriminated unions — the best pattern for UI state
type State =
  | { status: "loading" }
  | { status: "error"; message: string }
  | { status: "success"; data: Product[] };

function render(s: State) {
  switch (s.status) {
    case "loading": return "Loading…";
    case "error":   return s.message;          // TS knows message exists here
    case "success": return s.data.length;
    default: const _exhaustive: never = s;     // compile error if a case is missing
  }
}

// custom type guard
function isProduct(x: unknown): x is Product {
  return typeof x === "object" && x !== null && "price" in x;
}

      Escape hatches (use rarely): value as Product (type assertion), value! (non-null assertion). They silence the checker; they don't make code safe.

      Generics and utility types

      Generics = type parameters

      ts
      function first<T>(arr: T[]): T | undefined { return arr[0]; }
first([1, 2]);        // T = number
first(["a"]);         // T = string

type ApiResponse<T> = { data: T; error: string | null };
async function getJSON<T>(url: string): Promise<T> {
  const res = await fetch(url);
  return res.json() as Promise<T>;
}
const users = await getJSON<User[]>("/api/users");

function pluck<T, K extends keyof T>(obj: T, key: K): T[K] { return obj[key]; }  // constraints

      Built-in utility types

      UtilityResult
      Partial<User>All fields optional (update forms, PATCH)
      Required<T>All required
      Readonly<T>All readonly
      Pick<User, "id" | "name">Only those keys
      Omit<User, "password">Everything except
      Record<string, number>Object map type
      ReturnType<typeof fn>, Parameters<typeof fn>Types from a function
      Awaited<Promise<T>>T
      NonNullable<T>, Exclude, ExtractFilter unions

      Types from values

      ts
      const config = { theme: "dark", pageSize: 20 } as const;   // literal, readonly
type Config = typeof config;
type ConfigKey = keyof Config;                             // "theme" | "pageSize"
const ROLES = ["admin", "editor", "viewer"] as const;
type Role = (typeof ROLES)[number];                        // "admin" | "editor" | "viewer"

// Zod: one schema → runtime validation AND a type
import { z } from "zod";
const UserSchema = z.object({ name: z.string().min(2), email: z.email() });
type UserInput = z.infer<typeof UserSchema>;

      Advanced (recognise them)

      ts
      type Optional<T> = { [K in keyof T]?: T[K] };               // mapped type
type IsString<T> = T extends string ? true : false;          // conditional type
type EventName = `on${Capitalize<"click" | "hover">}`;       // template literal type → "onClick" | "onHover"

      TypeScript in real projects

      • Type the edges: component props, function parameters, API responses, store state. Let inference handle the inside.
      • Prefer unknown over any for data you don't control, then validate/narrow.
      • Types for libraries: most ship their own; otherwise npm i -D @types/lodash. Write declare module "x"; in a .d.ts as a last resort.
      • Path aliases: "paths": { "@/*": ["./src/*"] } in tsconfig.
      • Enums: prefer union literal types or as const objects; TS enums generate runtime code.
      • Type-check in CI with tsc --noEmit; Vite and Next strip types without checking during dev.
      • Framework specifics: React props/events in the React TypeScript topic; Vue uses defineProps<{ … }>() (see the Vue part).

      Practice

      Type a function that takes an array of any type and a predicate, and returns filtered items.
      ts
      function filterBy<T>(items: T[], predicate: (item: T) => boolean): T[] {
  return items.filter(predicate);
}
      any vs unknown?

      any disables checking entirely. unknown accepts anything but forces you to narrow before using it, so it's type-safe.

      Make a type for a user update form where every field is optional except id.

      type UserUpdate = Partial<Omit<User, "id">> & Pick<User, "id">;

      Part

      Vue.js

      The progressive framework: HTML-based templates, fine-grained reactivity and an official router, store and meta-framework (Nuxt). Written for Vue 3 with the Composition API and <script setup>.

      What Vue is and how to start

      Vue is a framework for building UIs from components, like React, but with HTML templates, directives (v-if, v-for) and automatic dependency tracking: when reactive data changes, only what depends on it updates. No need for manual memoisation.
      bash
      npm create vue@latest my-vue-app     # choose TypeScript, Router, Pinia, Vitest, Playwright, ESLint
cd my-vue-app && npm install && npm run dev

      Single-File Components (.vue)

      vue
      <!-- src/components/Counter.vue -->
<script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, computed } from "vue";

const count = ref(0);                         // reactive state
const double = computed(() => count.value * 2);
function increment() { count.value++; }       // .value in script, not in template
</script>

<template>
  <button @click="increment">Clicked {{ count }} times (double: {{ double }})</button>
</template>

<style scoped>
button { padding: 8px 16px; }   /* scoped: applies only to this component */
</style>
      ts
      // src/main.ts
import { createApp } from "vue";
import App from "./App.vue";
createApp(App).mount("#app");

      Install the Vue - Official VS Code extension (Volar) and Vue DevTools.

      Options API vs Composition API

      Older Vue (and many existing apps) use the Options API (data(), methods, computed, mounted as object keys, this.count). New code uses the Composition API with <script setup>: logic grouped by feature and reusable via composables, better TypeScript support. Recognise both.

      js
      // Options API (for reading old code)
export default {
  data() { return { count: 0 }; },
  computed: { double() { return this.count * 2; } },
  methods: { increment() { this.count++; } },
  mounted() { console.log("ready"); },
};

      Template syntax and directives

      vue
      <template>
  <h1>{{ title.toUpperCase() }}</h1>                    <!-- text interpolation (escaped) -->
  <img :src="avatarUrl" :alt="user.name" />              <!-- v-bind shorthand : -->
  <div :class="{ active: isActive, 'text-red': hasError }" :style="{ width: progress + '%' }"></div>
  <button @click="save" :disabled="saving">Save</button> <!-- v-on shorthand @ -->
  <form @submit.prevent="submit">                        <!-- event modifier = preventDefault -->
  <input @keyup.enter="search" />                         <!-- key modifier -->

  <p v-if="loading">Loading…</p>
  <p v-else-if="error">{{ error }}</p>
  <ul v-else>
    <li v-for="item in items" :key="item.id">{{ item.name }}</li>
  </ul>
  <p v-show="hasHint">Toggled with CSS display, stays in the DOM</p>

  <input v-model="query" />                              <!-- two-way binding -->
  <input v-model.trim="name" />  <input v-model.number="age" type="number" />
  <input type="checkbox" v-model="agree" />
  <select v-model="plan"><option value="free">Free</option></select>

  <div v-html="trustedHtml"></div>                       <!-- ⚠️ XSS risk, like innerHTML -->
  <template v-if="ok"><h2>Group</h2><p>without a wrapper element</p></template>
</template>
      DirectivePurposeReact equivalent
      {{ }}Output text{value}
      :attr / v-bindBind an attribute or propattr={value}
      @event / v-onListen to an eventonEvent={fn}
      v-if / v-elseConditionally renderternary / &&
      v-showToggle visibility (CSS)style={{display}}
      v-for + :keyRender a listmap + key
      v-modelTwo-way bindingvalue + onChange
      v-slot / #namePass template contentchildren / render props

      v-if vs v-show: v-if creates/destroys (cheaper when rarely shown), v-show just toggles CSS (cheaper when toggled often). Don't use v-if and v-for on the same element; filter with a computed first.

      Reactivity: ref, reactive, computed, watch

      ts
      import { ref, reactive, computed, watch, watchEffect } from "vue";

const count = ref(0);                        // any value; access with .value in script
count.value++;                               // mutate directly — Vue tracks it
const todos = ref<Todo[]>([]);
todos.value.push({ id: 1, text: "Learn Vue", done: false });   // mutation is fine in Vue!

const form = reactive({ email: "", password: "" });   // objects only, no .value
form.email = "a@b.com";
// ⚠️ destructuring a reactive object loses reactivity: use toRefs(form) or stick to ref()

const remaining = computed(() => todos.value.filter(t => !t.done).length);  // cached, recalculates when deps change
const fullName = computed({                  // writable computed
  get: () => `${first.value} ${last.value}`,
  set: v => ([first.value, last.value] = v.split(" ")),
});

watch(query, async (newQ, oldQ) => {         // run side effects when a source changes
  results.value = await search(newQ);
}, { immediate: false });
watch(() => form.email, v => validate(v));   // watch a getter
watch(todos, save, { deep: true });          // deep watch nested changes
watchEffect(() => console.log(count.value)); // runs now and re-runs when anything it reads changes
      VueReact
      Update modelMutate reactive data; Vue tracks dependencies via proxiesCall setState with new immutable values; component re-runs
      Derived datacomputed (auto-cached)Calculate in render / useMemo
      Side effectswatch / watchEffectuseEffect
      Setup code runsOnce per component instanceEvery render

      DOM updates are batched: after changing state, await nextTick() if you need the updated DOM.

      Components: props, emits, v-model, slots

      vue
      <!-- ProductCard.vue -->
<script setup lang="ts">
const props = withDefaults(defineProps<{
  title: string;
  price: number;
  inStock?: boolean;
}>(), { inStock: true });

const emit = defineEmits<{
  (e: "add", id: string): void;           // child → parent communication
}>();
</script>

<template>
  <article class="card">
    <h3>{{ title }}</h3>
    <p>₹{{ price }}</p>
    <slot name="badge" />                    <!-- named slot -->
    <slot>Default content if parent passes nothing</slot>
    <button :disabled="!inStock" @click="emit('add', title)">Add to cart</button>
  </article>
</template>
      vue
      <!-- Parent.vue -->
<script setup lang="ts">
import ProductCard from "./ProductCard.vue";   // auto-registered in <script setup>
function addToCart(id: string) { /* … */ }
</script>
<template>
  <ProductCard title="Keyboard" :price="1999" @add="addToCart">
    <template #badge><span>New</span></template>
    <p>Mechanical, hot-swappable</p>
  </ProductCard>
</template>
      • Props down, events up — the same one-way data flow as React. Never mutate a prop.
      • Scoped slots pass data back to the parent's template: <slot :item="item" /> → <template #default="{ item }">.
      • Attributes not declared as props (class, id, listeners) fall through to the root element automatically.

      v-model on your own components

      vue
      <!-- SearchInput.vue -->
<script setup lang="ts">
const model = defineModel<string>();     // Vue 3.4+: creates prop + update event for you
</script>
<template><input v-model="model" placeholder="Search" /></template>

<!-- usage -->
<SearchInput v-model="query" />

      Built-in components

      <Transition> / <TransitionGroup> (enter/leave animations), <KeepAlive> (cache component state when switching), <Teleport to="body"> (portals for modals), <Suspense> (async setup), dynamic <component :is="current" />.

      Lifecycle, template refs, composables, provide/inject

      vue
      <script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, onMounted, onUnmounted, onUpdated, useTemplateRef } from "vue";

const input = useTemplateRef<HTMLInputElement>("search");  // Vue 3.5+ (older: const search = ref(null))
let timer: number;
onMounted(() => {                       // DOM is ready: focus, measure, start timers, fetch
  input.value?.focus();
  timer = window.setInterval(tick, 1000);
});
onUnmounted(() => clearInterval(timer)); // cleanup
</script>
<template><input ref="search" /></template>

      Order: setup → onBeforeMount → onMounted → (onBeforeUpdate → onUpdated)* → onBeforeUnmount → onUnmounted. Also onErrorCaptured (error boundary) and onActivated/onDeactivated for KeepAlive.

      Composables (Vue's custom hooks)

      ts
      // composables/useFetch.ts
import { ref, watchEffect, toValue, type MaybeRefOrGetter } from "vue";
export function useFetch<T>(url: MaybeRefOrGetter<string>) {
  const data = ref<T | null>(null);
  const error = ref<string | null>(null);
  const loading = ref(false);
  watchEffect(async (onCleanup) => {
    const ctrl = new AbortController();
    onCleanup(() => ctrl.abort());          // cancel the previous request
    loading.value = true; error.value = null;
    try {
      const res = await fetch(toValue(url), { signal: ctrl.signal });
      if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`);
      data.value = await res.json();
    } catch (e: any) { if (e.name !== "AbortError") error.value = e.message; }
    finally { loading.value = false; }
  });
  return { data, error, loading };
}
// usage: const { data, loading } = useFetch<User[]>(() => `/api/users?q=${query.value}`);

      VueUse is a large collection of ready-made composables (useLocalStorage, useDebounceFn, useIntersectionObserver, useDark…).

      provide / inject (Vue's context)

      ts
      // ancestor
provide("theme", theme);                      // theme is a ref → stays reactive
// any descendant
const theme = inject("theme", ref("light"));   // second arg = default

      Vue Router and Pinia

      Vue Router

      ts
      // router/index.ts
import { createRouter, createWebHistory } from "vue-router";
export default createRouter({
  history: createWebHistory(),
  routes: [
    { path: "/", component: () => import("@/views/Home.vue") },          // lazy-loaded
    { path: "/products/:id", name: "product", component: () => import("@/views/Product.vue"), props: true },
    { path: "/admin", component: Admin, meta: { requiresAuth: true },
      children: [{ path: "users", component: AdminUsers }] },           // nested → <RouterView> inside Admin
    { path: "/:pathMatch(.*)*", component: NotFound },
  ],
});
// guard
router.beforeEach((to) => {
  if (to.meta.requiresAuth && !auth.isLoggedIn) return { path: "/login", query: { redirect: to.fullPath } };
});
      vue
      <RouterLink to="/">Home</RouterLink>
<RouterLink :to="{ name: 'product', params: { id: 42 } }">Product</RouterLink>
<RouterView />
<script setup>
import { useRoute, useRouter } from "vue-router";
const route = useRoute();     // route.params.id, route.query.q
const router = useRouter();   // router.push("/cart"), router.back()
</script>

      Pinia (the official store)

      ts
      // stores/cart.ts
import { defineStore } from "pinia";
import { ref, computed } from "vue";
export const useCartStore = defineStore("cart", () => {
  const items = ref<CartItem[]>([]);                              // state
  const total = computed(() => items.value.reduce((s, i) => s + i.price * i.qty, 0)); // getter
  function add(item: CartItem) { items.value.push(item); }        // action
  return { items, total, add };
});

// any component
import { storeToRefs } from "pinia";
const cart = useCartStore();
const { items, total } = storeToRefs(cart);   // keep reactivity when destructuring
cart.add(product);

      Pinia replaced Vuex. For server data, TanStack Query has a Vue adapter (@tanstack/vue-query).

      React vs Vue (and where Angular and Svelte fit)

      ReactVue
      TemplatesJSX (JavaScript)HTML templates with directives (JSX optional)
      State updatesImmutable + setState, component re-rendersMutable reactive data, fine-grained tracking
      Performance tuningmemo / compilerMostly automatic
      Official ecosystemCommunity (Next.js, TanStack, Zustand)Official router (Vue Router), store (Pinia), tooling
      StylingYour choice<style scoped> built in
      Meta-frameworkNext.js, React Router, TanStack StartNuxt
      Job marketLargest by farStrong in Europe/Asia, agencies, Laravel shops
      • Angular: full framework by Google with TypeScript, dependency injection, RxJS and signals; common in large enterprises and banks.
      • Svelte / SvelteKit: compiler-based, very little runtime, runes for reactivity.
      • Solid, Qwik, Astro: signals, resumability, content-first islands. Astro is popular for content sites and can host React/Vue components.

      The concepts transfer: components, props, one-way data flow, reactive state, derived state, effects, routing, stores, SSR. Learn one deeply, then the second takes weeks, not months.

      Vue interview questions

      ref vs reactive?

      ref wraps any value (access via .value in script) and can be reassigned; reactive makes an object deeply reactive but loses reactivity if destructured or replaced. Most teams default to ref.

      computed vs watch?

      computed derives a cached value from reactive state (no side effects). watch runs side effects (API calls, logging) when a source changes.

      How does Vue's reactivity work?

      Vue 3 wraps state in JavaScript Proxies. Reading a property during render/computed tracks it as a dependency; writing triggers the effects that depend on it.

      How do parent and child communicate?

      Props down, emitted events up; v-model combines both. For distant components use provide/inject or Pinia.

      Why is a key needed with v-for?

      Same reason as React: to identify items so Vue can reuse and reorder DOM elements and component state correctly.

      Part

      Vue in depth

      For people who already ship Vue: how reactivity and rendering work under the hood, performance, advanced component patterns, Vue 2 → 3 differences, Nuxt SSR, large-app architecture, and the questions 3-YOE Vue interviews ask.

      How Vue reactivity works internally

      Vue 3 reactivity = Proxies + dependency tracking. Reading reactive data inside an "effect" (a component render, a computed, a watch) records a dependency (track). Writing that data re-runs every effect that depends on it (trigger).
      js
      // A 25-line mental model of Vue's reactivity
let activeEffect = null;
const targetMap = new WeakMap();                  // target → key → Set(effects)

function track(target, key) {
  if (!activeEffect) return;
  let deps = targetMap.get(target) ?? targetMap.set(target, new Map()).get(target);
  let set = deps.get(key) ?? deps.set(key, new Set()).get(key);
  set.add(activeEffect);
}
function trigger(target, key) {
  targetMap.get(target)?.get(key)?.forEach(effect => effect());
}
function reactive(obj) {
  return new Proxy(obj, {
    get(t, k, r) { track(t, k); const v = Reflect.get(t, k, r); return typeof v === "object" && v ? reactive(v) : v; },
    set(t, k, v, r) { const ok = Reflect.set(t, k, v, r); trigger(t, k); return ok; },
  });
}
function effect(fn) { const run = () => { activeEffect = run; fn(); activeEffect = null; }; run(); }
function ref(value) {                              // refs use getters/setters on .value
  const box = { get value() { track(box, "value"); return value; }, set value(v) { value = v; trigger(box, "value"); } };
  return box;
}

const state = reactive({ count: 0 });
effect(() => console.log("count is", state.count));  // logs 0
state.count++;                                       // logs 1 — re-run automatically

      Consequences you must understand

      • Destructuring breaks reactivity: const { count } = reactive(state) copies a plain value. Use toRefs, storeToRefs (Pinia) or keep refs. Since Vue 3.5, destructured defineProps is reactive (the compiler rewrites it), but pass it to watch as a getter: watch(() => count, …).
      • Replacing a reactive object (state = {…}) loses the proxy; that's why many teams default to ref (you replace .value).
      • Identity: reactive(obj) !== obj. Compare with toRaw() if needed.
      • Only what's read during the effect is tracked. A value read only inside an if branch that didn't run isn't a dependency (yet).
      • Updates are batched: many writes in one tick → one re-render in the next microtask. await nextTick() to read the updated DOM.
      • computed is lazy and cached: it recomputes only when a dependency changed and someone reads it. Since Vue 3.4, a computed whose new value is equal doesn't trigger its dependents.

      Escape hatches for performance and integration

      APIUse when
      shallowRef / shallowReactiveLarge immutable data (API responses, big lists, chart data): only .value replacement is tracked, not deep mutations
      triggerRef(r)Force effects after mutating inside a shallowRef
      markRaw(obj)Never make it reactive: map/chart/editor instances, class instances from libraries
      toRaw(proxy)Get the original object (passing to third-party code, comparisons)
      readonly / shallowReadonlyExpose state that consumers can't mutate
      customRefCustom track/trigger timing, e.g. a debounced ref
      effectScopeGroup effects created outside components (in composables/stores) and stop them together
      onWatcherCleanup (3.5)Register cleanup inside a watcher (abort stale requests)

      Vue 3.6 reworked the reactivity core for speed (a signals-style implementation) and introduces Vapor Mode. The public API (ref, computed, watch) stays the same, so this mental model still holds.

      How Vue renders: compiler, virtual DOM, Vapor Mode

      1. Compile: the SFC compiler turns <template> into a render function (at build time with Vite).
      2. Render: the render function runs inside a reactive effect and returns a virtual DOM tree (VNodes). Reads during render are tracked, so each component re-renders only when its own dependencies change — no parent-cascade like React.
      3. Patch: Vue diffs old vs new VNodes and applies minimal DOM changes.

      Compiler-informed virtual DOM (why Vue's diff is cheap)

      • Static hoisting: static elements are created once and reused.
      • Patch flags: each dynamic node is marked with what can change (text only, class only, props…), so the patch skips everything else.
      • Block tree: a block tracks its dynamic descendants in a flat array; the diff walks only those, not the whole tree.
      • Cached event handlers: inline handlers aren't recreated, so children don't see "new" props.

      Paste a template into the Vue SFC Playground (play.vuejs.org) and open the JS output tab to see this for real.

      Keyed diffing

      For v-for, Vue matches old and new children by key, then uses the longest increasing subsequence to move the fewest DOM nodes. Index keys break state and transitions when items are inserted/reordered.

      Vapor Mode

      An opt-in compilation strategy (<script setup vapor>) introduced with Vue 3.6: components compile to direct DOM operations bound to fine-grained reactive effects, with no virtual DOM — the approach of Solid and Svelte 5. It gives smaller bundles and faster updates and can be adopted per component. Check the Vue blog for its current status and limitations before adopting it in production.

      SSR and hydration in Vue

      On the server, renderToString (or streams) produces HTML; on the client, createSSRApp hydrates it by walking existing DOM and attaching listeners. Mismatches (different text, random ids, dates, window checks during render) cause warnings and client re-rendering. Vue 3.5 added lazy hydration for async components (hydrate: hydrateOnVisible(), hydrateOnIdle, hydrateOnInteraction) and useId() for SSR-stable ids.

      Vue performance

      Rendering performance

      • Keep props stable: a child re-renders when any prop it receives changes. Pass activeId and compute isActive inside a parent's computed/per-item comparison instead of passing a changing value to every row.
      • Prefer computed over methods in templates for anything expensive (methods run every render).
      • v-once: render once, never update. v-memo="[item.id === selected]": skip re-rendering a subtree unless the listed values change (big lists).
      • shallowRef for large data: avoid deep proxies over thousands of objects; replace the array instead of mutating.
      • Virtualise long lists (vue-virtual-scroller, @tanstack/vue-virtual).
      • v-show for frequently toggled UI, v-if for rarely shown.
      • <KeepAlive :max="5"> to cache expensive tab views instead of re-creating them.
      • Avoid deep watchers on big objects; watch specific getters.

      Loading performance

      • Route-level code splitting: component: () => import("./views/Reports.vue").
      • defineAsyncComponent for heavy widgets (editors, charts, maps) + lazy hydration in SSR.
      • Import only what you use from UI libraries (auto-import/resolver plugins for Element Plus, Vuetify, PrimeVue).
      • Analyse the bundle with rollup-plugin-visualizer; replace heavy dependencies.
      • Nuxt: routeRules to prerender/cache pages, <NuxtImg>, <LazyMyComponent> prefix for lazy components.

      Measuring

      Vue DevTools → Performance/Timeline shows component render times and events; Chrome Performance panel for long tasks; app.config.performance = true in dev adds marks to the timeline. Fix what you measure.

      "A list of 5,000 rows is slow to filter. What do you do?" Measure; make the data shallowRef; filter in a computed, debounced on input; virtualise the list; use stable keys; move per-row derived state out of the template; v-memo on rows; consider server-side filtering/pagination.

      Advanced component patterns

      Typed provide/inject

      ts
      // keys.ts
import type { InjectionKey, Ref } from "vue";
export const TabsKey: InjectionKey<{ active: Ref<string>; select: (id: string) => void }> = Symbol("Tabs");
// Tabs.vue:  provide(TabsKey, { active, select })
// Tab.vue:   const tabs = inject(TabsKey)!;   // fully typed

      Compound / headless components

      A parent (Tabs) provides state; children (TabList, Tab, TabPanel) inject it. Headless libraries (Reka UI, formerly Radix Vue; Headless UI) provide behaviour and accessibility with no styles — the basis of most modern design systems.

      Renderless components with scoped slots

      vue
      <!-- MouseTracker.vue: logic only, UI decided by the parent -->
<script setup>
import { useMouse } from "@vueuse/core";
const { x, y } = useMouse();
</script>
<template><slot :x="x" :y="y" /></template>

<!-- usage -->
<MouseTracker v-slot="{ x, y }">Cursor at {{ x }}, {{ y }}</MouseTracker>

      Today, composables usually replace renderless components; scoped slots remain ideal when a component owns both markup structure and lets the parent customise pieces (tables with custom cells).

      Generic components

      vue
      <script setup lang="ts" generic="T extends { id: string | number }">
defineProps<{ items: T[]; selected?: T }>();
defineEmits<{ select: [item: T] }>();
defineSlots<{ row(props: { item: T }): any }>();
</script>

      Exposing methods to the parent

      vue
      <script setup>
const input = useTemplateRef("input");
function focus() { input.value?.focus(); }
defineExpose({ focus });           // <script setup> components are closed by default
</script>
<!-- parent: const field = useTemplateRef("field"); field.value.focus() -->

      Custom directives

      ts
      // v-click-outside
export const vClickOutside = {
  mounted(el: HTMLElement, binding: { value: (e: Event) => void }) {
    (el as any)._handler = (e: Event) => { if (!el.contains(e.target as Node)) binding.value(e); };
    document.addEventListener("click", (el as any)._handler);
  },
  unmounted(el: HTMLElement) { document.removeEventListener("click", (el as any)._handler); },
};
// <div v-click-outside="close"> — in <script setup>, any vCamelCase variable is usable as a directive

      Use directives only for low-level DOM behaviour (focus, click-outside, intersection, tooltips); prefer components/composables otherwise.

      Render functions and JSX

      ts
      import { h, defineComponent } from "vue";
export default defineComponent({
  props: { level: { type: Number, default: 1 } },
  setup(props, { slots }) {
    return () => h(`h${props.level}`, { class: "heading" }, slots.default?.());
  },
});

      Useful when markup is highly dynamic (dynamic heading levels, table renderers, libraries).

      Plugins

      ts
      export default {
  install(app, options) {
    app.provide("toast", createToast(options));
    app.component("AppButton", AppButton);
    app.directive("click-outside", vClickOutside);
    app.config.globalProperties.$format = format;   // Options API access
  },
};
// app.use(myPlugin, { position: "top-right" })

      Other things 3-YOE Vue devs should know

      • defineOptions({ inheritAttrs: false }) + v-bind="$attrs" to forward attributes to an inner element (wrapper inputs).
      • Multiple v-models: v-model:title, v-model:open with defineModel("title"); modifiers via defineModel's set.
      • Dynamic and async components with <component :is>, defineAsyncComponent({ loader, loadingComponent, errorComponent, delay, timeout }).
      • <Suspense> with async setup (still marked experimental in Vue core; Nuxt relies on it).
      • Global error handling: app.config.errorHandler, onErrorCaptured for boundaries.

      Vue 2 → Vue 3: differences and migration

      Vue 2 reached end of life on 31 December 2023. Many companies still maintain Vue 2 apps, and "how would you migrate?" is a common interview question.

      Vue 2Vue 3
      Object.defineProperty reactivity: can't detect added/deleted properties or array index writes (Vue.set, this.$set)Proxy reactivity: additions, deletions, index writes, Map/Set all tracked
      Options API only (Composition API via plugin in 2.7)Composition API + <script setup>; Options API still supported
      new Vue({}), global Vue.use/Vue.componentcreateApp(), app-scoped plugins/components
      Single root element requiredFragments (multiple roots)
      v-model = value + input; .sync modifierv-model = modelValue + update:modelValue; multiple v-model:arg; .sync removed
      Filters {{ price | currency }}Removed: use methods/computed
      Event bus with $on/$offRemoved: use mitt, provide/inject, or Pinia
      beforeDestroy/destroyedbeforeUnmount/unmounted
      v-if vs v-for precedence: v-for winsv-if wins (don't combine them)
      VuexPinia (official)
      Vue CLI + webpackVite
      Vue Router 3, Nuxt 2Vue Router 4, Nuxt 3/4
      $listeners, $scopedSlotsMerged into $attrs and $slots
      <portal-vue>Built-in <Teleport>

      A migration strategy that works

      1. Upgrade to Vue 2.7 first (Composition API, <script setup> available) and move to Vite.
      2. Remove deprecated patterns in 2.x: filters, event bus, .sync, $listeners; replace Vuex modules with Pinia (works with 2.7).
      3. Audit dependencies: UI libraries often need a major upgrade (Vuetify 2 → 3, Element UI → Element Plus).
      4. Switch to Vue 3 with the migration build (@vue/compat), which warns about each incompatibility at runtime; fix warnings until compat mode can be removed.
      5. Protect with E2E tests on critical flows before and after. Migrate incrementally, route by route if possible.

      Nuxt in depth

      Rendering modes and route rules

      ts
      // nuxt.config.ts
export default defineNuxtConfig({
  routeRules: {
    "/": { prerender: true },                 // static at build (SSG)
    "/blog/**": { isr: 3600 },                // regenerate at most hourly (ISR, platform-dependent)
    "/products/**": { swr: 600 },             // cache + stale-while-revalidate
    "/dashboard/**": { ssr: false },          // client-only SPA section
    "/api/**": { cors: true, headers: { "cache-control": "no-store" } },
    "/old": { redirect: "/new" },
  },
});

      Hybrid rendering per route is Nuxt's big strength: marketing pages static, product pages cached, dashboards client-only.

      Data fetching rules

      • useFetch(url) = useAsyncData(key, () => $fetch(url)). It runs on the server during SSR, serialises the result into the payload, and doesn't refetch on hydration.
      • Plain $fetch in setup would run twice (server and client). Use it in event handlers and inside useAsyncData.
      • Keys deduplicate and share data: same key → same request/cache. Use refreshNuxtData(key) / clearNuxtData to invalidate.
      • Options: lazy: true (don't block navigation), server: false (client only), pick/transform (shrink payload), watch (refetch on change), default.
      • Shared state: useState("key", () => init) is SSR-safe. Never use a module-level ref for per-user state on the server: it's shared across all requests (a data leak).

      Server side (Nitro)

      ts
      // server/api/orders/[id].get.ts
export default defineEventHandler(async (event) => {
  const id = getRouterParam(event, "id");
  const user = await requireUserSession(event);           // e.g. nuxt-auth-utils
  const order = await db.order.findFirst({ where: { id, userId: user.id } });
  if (!order) throw createError({ statusCode: 404, statusMessage: "Order not found" });
  return order;
});
// server/middleware/log.ts runs on every request; server/utils/* auto-imported

      Other essentials

      • Route middleware (middleware/auth.ts with defineNuxtRouteMiddleware) for client/server navigation guards; plugins (plugins/*.ts) for app-wide setup; .client/.server suffixes to limit where they run.
      • <ClientOnly> for browser-only widgets; onMounted for window access; import.meta.client/import.meta.server checks.
      • runtimeConfig (server secrets) vs runtimeConfig.public (exposed), overridable with NUXT_* env vars.
      • Layers to share config/components between apps; modules ecosystem (Nuxt UI, Image, Content, i18n, Pinia, Test Utils).
      • Nuxt 4 moved app code into an app/ directory by default and tightened data-fetching defaults (shared refs across same-key calls, undefined default data). Read the upgrade guide when migrating from Nuxt 3.

      Structuring a large Vue app

      text
      src/
├─ app/                 # app shell: main.ts, router, providers, global styles
├─ features/            # one folder per business feature
│  ├─ checkout/
│  │  ├─ components/    # CheckoutForm.vue, OrderSummary.vue
│  │  ├─ composables/   # useCheckout.ts
│  │  ├─ api.ts         # typed API calls for this feature
│  │  ├─ store.ts       # Pinia store (only if state is shared)
│  │  ├─ types.ts
│  │  └─ index.ts       # public exports of the feature
│  └─ products/ …
├─ shared/
│  ├─ ui/               # design-system components (BaseButton, BaseModal)
│  ├─ composables/      # useDebounce, useMediaQuery
│  ├─ lib/              # http client, formatters, validation schemas
│  └─ types/
└─ pages/ or views/     # thin route components that compose features
      • Feature-based folders scale better than type-based (components/, stores/ with hundreds of files). Features import from shared, not from each other's internals.
      • One HTTP client (ofetch/axios instance) with base URL, auth header, error normalisation, 401 handling and retries. Components never call fetch directly.
      • Server state vs client state: use TanStack Vue Query or Pinia Colada for API data (caching, refetching, deduping); keep Pinia for true client state (auth session, cart draft, UI preferences).
      • Pinia store patterns: small stores per domain, setup-store syntax, no component-specific state in stores, storeToRefs when destructuring, reset functions for logout.
      • Forms: VeeValidate or FormKit + Zod schemas shared with the backend.
      • Linting guardrails: eslint-plugin-vue recommended rules, import boundaries between features, TS strict mode, vue-tsc --noEmit in CI.

      Vue interview questions for 3 YOE

      Explain Vue 3's reactivity system.

      State is wrapped in Proxies (reactive) or getter/setter objects (ref). When an effect (render, computed, watcher) runs, property reads call track to register the effect as a dependency; writes call trigger, which schedules dependent effects. Scheduler batches them into the next microtask.

      Why can destructuring lose reactivity, and how do you fix it?

      Destructuring reads the current values into plain variables, cutting the link to the proxy. Use toRefs/toRef, storeToRefs for Pinia, or keep accessing through the object. Props destructured in <script setup> (3.5+) are compiled to stay reactive.

      computed vs watch vs watchEffect?

      computed: derived value, cached, no side effects. watch: explicit sources, gives old/new values, lazy by default, for side effects. watchEffect: runs immediately and tracks whatever it reads. Prefer computed for derivation.

      What is nextTick and when do you need it?

      DOM updates are batched and applied asynchronously. await nextTick() waits until the DOM reflects the latest state changes, e.g. before measuring an element or focusing a newly rendered input.

      How is Vue's rendering more efficient than a naive virtual DOM diff?

      The compiler hoists static content, adds patch flags describing what can change on each node, and builds blocks tracking only dynamic descendants, so patches skip static parts. Component-level dependency tracking means only components whose data changed re-render.

      How do Vue and React differ in re-rendering?

      Vue tracks dependencies per component, so a change re-renders only components that read that data. React re-renders a component and, by default, all its children when its state changes, relying on memoisation (or the React Compiler) to skip work.

      When would you use shallowRef or markRaw?

      shallowRef for large data you replace rather than mutate (API lists, chart data) to avoid deep proxy costs. markRaw for objects that must never be proxied: third-party instances like maps, editors, charts.

      How does v-model work on a component in Vue 3?

      It expands to :modelValue + @update:modelValue (or named: :title + @update:title). defineModel() generates both and returns a ref you can read and write.

      Explain slots, scoped slots and when you'd use each.

      Slots let parents pass template content; named slots place content in specific spots; scoped slots let the child pass data back to the parent's slot template (e.g. a table passing each row to a custom cell template).

      provide/inject vs Pinia?

      provide/inject scopes data to a component subtree (compound components, per-form context, theming). Pinia is global app state with DevTools, plugins and SSR support. Use provide/inject for local component families, Pinia for app-wide state.

      What is the difference between v-if and v-show, and v-if with v-for?

      v-if mounts/unmounts, v-show toggles display. In Vue 3, v-if has higher priority than v-for on the same element, so the loop variable isn't available to it; filter with a computed or wrap with <template v-for>.

      How does KeepAlive work and what are the lifecycle hooks?

      It caches inactive component instances instead of destroying them (include/exclude/max control it). Cached components get onActivated/onDeactivated instead of mount/unmount on toggle.

      What causes hydration mismatches in Nuxt and how do you fix them?

      Server HTML differs from the first client render: dates/random values, browser-only checks during render, locale differences, invalid HTML nesting. Fix with useId, <ClientOnly>, moving browser code to onMounted, SSR-safe useState, consistent formatting.

      Why must you not use a module-level ref for user state in Nuxt?

      On the server, modules are loaded once and shared by all requests, so one user's data could leak into another's response. Use useState or Pinia, which are created per request.

      useFetch vs $fetch?

      useFetch is SSR-aware: fetches on the server, transfers the result in the payload, doesn't refetch on hydration, and is reactive/cached by key. $fetch is a plain fetch helper; use it in event handlers or inside useAsyncData.

      How would you migrate a large Vue 2 app to Vue 3?

      Upgrade to 2.7 and Vite, remove deprecated APIs, move Vuex → Pinia, upgrade UI libraries, switch to the @vue/compat build and fix warnings, with E2E tests guarding critical flows, then remove compat.

      How do you share logic between components?

      Composables (functions using reactivity and lifecycle hooks, returning refs/functions). Mixins are discouraged in Vue 3: unclear origin of properties and name clashes.

      How do you write and test a composable?

      Return refs and functions, accept refs/getters (MaybeRefOrGetter + toValue), clean up with onScopeDispose. Test pure ones directly; for ones using lifecycle hooks or inject, mount a tiny host component or use withSetup helpers.

      What's Vapor Mode?

      An opt-in compile mode (Vue 3.6) that generates direct DOM updates driven by fine-grained reactivity instead of virtual DOM diffing, for smaller bundles and faster updates, adoptable per component.

      Machine coding: build a debounced search with cancellation in Vue.
      vue
      <script setup lang="ts">
import { ref, watch, onWatcherCleanup } from "vue";
const q = ref(""); const results = ref<string[]>([]); const status = ref<"idle"|"loading"|"error">("idle");
watch(q, (value) => {
  if (!value.trim()) { results.value = []; return; }
  const ctrl = new AbortController();
  const t = setTimeout(async () => {
    status.value = "loading";
    try {
      const res = await fetch(`/api/search?q=${encodeURIComponent(value)}`, { signal: ctrl.signal });
