      results.value = await res.json(); status.value = "idle";
    } catch (e: any) { if (e.name !== "AbortError") status.value = "error"; }
  }, 300);
  onWatcherCleanup(() => { clearTimeout(t); ctrl.abort(); });   // runs before the next change
});
</script>
<template>
  <input v-model="q" placeholder="Search" aria-label="Search" />
  <p v-if="status === 'loading'">Searching…</p>
  <p v-else-if="status === 'error'">Search failed. Try again.</p>
  <ul><li v-for="r in results" :key="r">{{ r }}</li></ul>
</template>
      Part 1

      React foundations

      What React is, how to set up a project, JSX, components, props, state, lists, events and forms. Everything in Part 2 and Next.js sits on this.

      What React is and why it exists

      React is a JavaScript library for building user interfaces out of components. You describe what the UI should look like for the current data; React figures out how to update the DOM.

      The problem it solves

      In the vanilla JS task manager (Browser & DOM part), every change meant: update the array → save → clear the list → rebuild the DOM by hand. That's fine for one list. For a whole app (feeds, carts, chats, notifications all changing at once) manual DOM work becomes buggy and slow to write. React automates the "rebuild the DOM from data" step.

      Vanilla JS (imperative)React (declarative)
      "Find the list, create an li, set its text, append it, update the counter…""The list shows these tasks. The counter shows tasks.length." Change the data and the UI follows.

      Core ideas

      • Components — reusable pieces of UI written as functions (Navbar, Card, Button). An app is a tree of components.
      • Declarative UI — UI = f(state). Same data in, same UI out.
      • One-way data flow — data flows down from parent to child through props.
      • Virtual DOM and reconciliation — when state changes, React re-runs your component to get a new description of the UI, compares (diffs) it with the previous one, and applies only the minimal real DOM changes.
      • It's "just JavaScript" — loops are map, conditions are ternaries and &&, modules are ES modules. The JavaScript parts of this handbook are the prerequisite.

      Library vs framework

      React only handles the UI. Routing, data fetching, server rendering, bundling and SEO are up to you. Next.js is a framework built on React that provides those pieces. That's why you learn React first, then Next.js.

      SPA, SSR, SSG — the rendering vocabulary

      TermWhere HTML is madeTypical tool
      CSR / SPA (client-side rendering)In the browser, by JS, after an empty HTML page loadsReact + Vite
      SSR (server-side rendering)On the server, per requestNext.js dynamic routes
      SSG (static site generation)At build time, served as filesNext.js static routes
      ISR / revalidationStatic, but regenerated after a time or on demandNext.js
      RSC (React Server Components)Components that run only on the server and send rendered outputNext.js App Router

      Setting up a React project

      You need Node.js (LTS) installed; it gives you npm. Check with node -v.

      bash
      # Plain React (single-page app) with Vite
npm create vite@latest my-app -- --template react   # or react-ts for TypeScript
cd my-app
npm install
npm run dev          # opens http://localhost:5173

# Full-stack React with Next.js (Part 3)
npx create-next-app@latest my-next-app

      Create React App (create-react-app) is deprecated. Old tutorials use it; use Vite for plain React or a framework like Next.js.

      Vite project structure

      text
      my-app/
├─ index.html          ← the one HTML page, has <div id="root">
├─ package.json        ← dependencies + scripts (dev, build, preview)
├─ vite.config.js
├─ public/             ← static files served as-is (favicon, images)
└─ src/
   ├─ main.jsx         ← entry: mounts <App /> into #root
   ├─ App.jsx          ← root component
   ├─ index.css
   └─ components/      ← your components (convention)
      jsx
      // src/main.jsx
import { StrictMode } from "react";
import { createRoot } from "react-dom/client";
import App from "./App.jsx";
import "./index.css";

createRoot(document.getElementById("root")).render(
  <StrictMode>
    <App />
  </StrictMode>
);

      npm essentials

      bash
      npm install axios          # add a dependency (saved in package.json)
npm install -D prettier    # dev-only dependency
npm uninstall axios
npm run build              # production build → dist/

      node_modules/ is never committed to Git; package-lock.json is. Install the React Developer Tools browser extension to inspect components, props and state.

      JavaScript you'll use constantly in React

      If any of these feel shaky, revisit them in the JavaScript parts of this handbook first. React code is 80% this.

      jsx
      // destructuring (props, useState)
const { title, price } = product;
const [count, setCount] = useState(0);

// spread for immutable updates
const updatedUser = { ...user, name: "Aman" };
const withNew = [...todos, newTodo];

// array methods for rendering and updating
todos.map(t => <li key={t.id}>{t.text}</li>);
todos.filter(t => t.id !== id);
todos.map(t => (t.id === id ? { ...t, done: !t.done } : t));

// conditional expressions (no if/else inside JSX)
isLoggedIn ? <Dashboard /> : <Login />;
error && <p>{error}</p>;
user?.profile?.avatar ?? "/default.png";

// arrow functions and callbacks
<button onClick={() => setCount(c => c + 1)}>+</button>

// modules
import Button from "./Button";          // default
import { formatPrice } from "./utils";  // named

// template literals, async/await, fetch
const res = await fetch(`/api/products/${id}`);

      JSX

      JSX is HTML-like syntax inside JavaScript. It compiles to function calls that create React elements. It's not HTML, so a few rules differ.
      jsx
      function Welcome() {
  const name = "Harsh";
  const isAdmin = true;
  return (
    <div className="card">                 {/* className, not class */}
      <h1>Hello, {name.toUpperCase()}!</h1>   {/* {} = any JS expression */}
      <p>2 + 2 = {2 + 2}</p>
      {isAdmin && <span>Admin</span>}
      <img src="/me.png" alt="Me" />       {/* every tag must close */}
      <label htmlFor="email">Email</label>  {/* htmlFor, not for */}
      <input id="email" style={{ padding: 8, marginTop: "4px" }} />
    </div>
  );
}

      The rules

      1. Return one root element. Wrap siblings in a <div> or a Fragment <>…</> (adds nothing to the DOM).
      2. Close every tag: <br />, <img />, <input />.
      3. camelCase attributes: className, htmlFor, onClick, tabIndex, strokeWidth. Exceptions: aria-* and data-* stay kebab-case.
      4. Curly braces { } hold expressions (values), not statements. No if, for or let inside; use ternaries, && and map.
      5. Inline styles are objects with camelCase keys: style={{ backgroundColor: "red" }} (outer braces = JS, inner = object).
      6. Comments: {/* like this */}.
      7. Components must start with a capital letter: <Card /> is a component, <card /> is treated as an HTML tag.

      JSX escapes values automatically, so {userInput} is safe from XSS (unlike innerHTML). The escape hatch dangerouslySetInnerHTML is named that way on purpose.

      What JSX compiles to

      jsx
      <h1 className="t">Hi</h1>
// becomes roughly:
jsx("h1", { className: "t", children: "Hi" })
// → a plain object: { type: "h1", props: { className: "t", children: "Hi" } }

      What renders: strings, numbers, elements, arrays of those. true, false, null and undefined render nothing. Objects can't be rendered directly (error: "Objects are not valid as a React child").

      Components and props

      A component is a function that returns JSX

      jsx
      // ProductCard.jsx
export default function ProductCard({ title, price, inStock = true }) {  // destructured props + default
  return (
    <div className="card">
      <h3>{title}</h3>
      <p>₹{price}</p>
      {!inStock && <small>Out of stock</small>}
    </div>
  );
}

// App.jsx
import ProductCard from "./ProductCard";
export default function App() {
  return (
    <>
      <ProductCard title="Keyboard" price={1999} />
      <ProductCard title="Mouse" price={799} inStock={false} />
    </>
  );
}

      Props

      • Props are the inputs to a component, passed like HTML attributes. Strings in quotes, everything else in braces: price={1999}, tags={["a","b"]}, onSelect={handleSelect}.
      • Props are read-only. A component must never modify its props. To change data, the owner changes its state and passes new props down.
      • A prop with no value is true: <Button disabled />.
      • Spread an object: <Card {...product} />.
      • Functions as props let children talk to parents: <Child onAdd={addItem} /> → child calls onAdd(item).

      children

      Whatever you put between a component's tags arrives as props.children. This is how you build wrappers and layouts.

      jsx
      function Card({ title, children }) {
  return (
    <section className="card">
      <h2>{title}</h2>
      {children}
    </section>
  );
}
<Card title="Profile">
  <img src="/me.png" alt="" />
  <p>Frontend developer</p>
</Card>

      Composition and file organisation

      • Build big components from small ones. If a piece of JSX repeats or a component passes ~150 lines, extract a component.
      • One component per file, file named like the component (ProductCard.jsx).
      • Never define a component inside another component: it gets recreated on every render and loses its state.
      • Components must be pure during rendering: same props/state → same JSX, no side effects (no fetch, no DOM edits, no changing outside variables) in the function body.

      Rendering lists and conditions

      Lists with map

      jsx
      const users = [
  { id: 1, name: "Harsh", role: "admin" },
  { id: 2, name: "Aman", role: "user" },
];
function UserList() {
  return (
    <ul>
      {users.map(user => (
        <li key={user.id}>{user.name}</li>
      ))}
    </ul>
  );
}
// filter then map
{users.filter(u => u.role === "admin").map(u => <Admin key={u.id} {...u} />)}

      Keys

      A key tells React which item is which between renders, so it can reorder, insert or delete efficiently and keep each item's state attached to the right item.

      • Keys must be unique among siblings and stable (same item → same key every render). Use database ids.
      • Avoid array index as key when the list can be reordered, filtered or have items inserted: state and input values get attached to the wrong rows.
      • Never generate keys with Math.random() or Date.now() during render: every render looks like a brand-new list.
      • The key goes on the outermost element returned from map. For fragments use <Fragment key={id}>.

      Conditional rendering

      jsx
      // ternary: either / or
{isLoggedIn ? <Profile /> : <LoginButton />}

// && : show or nothing
{cart.length > 0 && <CartBadge count={cart.length} />}

// ⚠️ numbers leak: {count && <p>…</p>} renders "0" when count is 0
{count > 0 && <p>{count} items</p>}     // ✅ make it a boolean

// early return for whole-component states
function Orders({ orders, loading, error }) {
  if (loading) return <Spinner />;
  if (error) return <p>Could not load orders: {error}</p>;
  if (orders.length === 0) return <p>No orders yet. Your first order will show here.</p>;
  return <OrderTable orders={orders} />;
}

// map of variants
const icons = { success: "✅", error: "❌", info: "ℹ️" };
<span>{icons[status]}</span>

      Handling events

      jsx
      function Buttons() {
  function handleClick(e) {
    console.log("clicked", e.target);
  }
  return (
    <>
      <button onClick={handleClick}>Pass the function</button>
      <button onClick={() => setOpen(true)}>Inline arrow</button>
      <button onClick={() => deleteItem(42)}>Pass arguments via arrow</button>
      {/* ❌ onClick={handleClick()} calls it during render */}
    </>
  );
}
      • Event props are camelCase: onClick, onChange, onSubmit, onKeyDown, onMouseEnter, onFocus, onBlur, onScroll.
      • Pass a function, don't call it. Use an arrow when you need arguments.
      • React gives you a SyntheticEvent (a cross-browser wrapper) with the familiar e.target, e.preventDefault(), e.stopPropagation(), e.key.
      • onChange on inputs fires on every keystroke (like the DOM input event).
      • Events bubble in React too; onClickCapture listens in the capture phase.
      • Name handlers handleX and the props that receive them onX: <Modal onClose={handleClose} />.

      State with useState

      State is data a component remembers between renders. Changing it with the setter re-renders the component with the new value. Regular variables reset on every render and don't trigger updates.
      jsx
      import { useState } from "react";

function Counter() {
  const [count, setCount] = useState(0);   // [current value, setter], initial value 0
  return (
    <>
      <p>Count: {count}</p>
      <button onClick={() => setCount(count + 1)}>+1</button>
      <button onClick={() => setCount(0)}>Reset</button>
    </>
  );
}

      How a state update works

      1. You call setCount(5).
      2. React schedules a re-render (it doesn't change count right away).
      3. Your component function runs again; useState returns 5 this time.
      4. React diffs the new JSX and updates the DOM.

      State is a snapshot. Within one render, count is fixed. setCount(count + 1) three times in a row adds only 1. Use the updater function when the new value depends on the old one:

      jsx
      setCount(count + 1); setCount(count + 1);          // → +1 (both read the same snapshot)
setCount(c => c + 1); setCount(c => c + 1);        // → +2 ✅ each gets the latest value
console.log(count);   // still the OLD value here; the update shows on the next render

      React batches multiple setState calls in the same event into one re-render.

      Updating objects and arrays immutably

      Never mutate state (user.name = "x", todos.push(t)): React compares references, sees the same object and may not re-render. Always create a new object/array.

      jsx
      const [user, setUser] = useState({ name: "Harsh", address: { city: "Bhopal" } });
setUser({ ...user, name: "Aman" });                                     // update field
setUser(u => ({ ...u, address: { ...u.address, city: "Indore" } }));    // nested

const [todos, setTodos] = useState([]);
setTodos([...todos, { id: crypto.randomUUID(), text, done: false }]);   // add
setTodos(todos.filter(t => t.id !== id));                                // remove
setTodos(todos.map(t => t.id === id ? { ...t, done: !t.done } : t));     // update one
setTodos([...todos].sort((a, b) => a.text.localeCompare(b.text)));      // sort a copy
      Avoid (mutates)Use (returns new)
      push, unshift[...arr, x], [x, ...arr]
      pop, shift, splicefilter, slice, toSpliced
      arr[i] = xmap, with(i, x)
      sort, reversetoSorted, toReversed, or copy first

      Libraries like Immer (useImmer) let you write mutating-style code that produces immutable updates.

      Designing state well

      • Don't store what you can calculate. If you have todos, compute remaining = todos.filter(t => !t.done).length during render instead of keeping it in state.
      • Don't copy props into state (it won't update when the prop changes) unless you mean "initial value".
      • Group related values ({ x, y }), avoid contradictory flags (isSending + isSent → one status).
      • Store ids, not duplicated objects (selectedId, not a copy of the selected item).
      • Expensive initial value? Pass a function: useState(() => JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("todos")) ?? []) runs once.
      • State is local and private to each component instance. Two <Counter />s have independent counts.

      Lifting state up

      When two components need the same data, move the state to their closest common parent and pass it down as props (plus setter functions as callbacks).

      jsx
      function Parent() {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  return (
    <>
      <SearchBox value={query} onChange={setQuery} />
      <Results query={query} />
    </>
  );
}

      Forms

      Controlled inputs

      The input's value lives in state; React is the "single source of truth". You get live validation, formatting and easy resetting.

      jsx
      function SignupForm() {
  const [form, setForm] = useState({ name: "", email: "", plan: "free", agree: false });
  const [errors, setErrors] = useState({});

  function handleChange(e) {
    const { name, value, type, checked } = e.target;
    setForm(f => ({ ...f, [name]: type === "checkbox" ? checked : value })); // one handler for all
  }

  function handleSubmit(e) {
    e.preventDefault();
    const errs = {};
    if (form.name.trim().length < 3) errs.name = "Name needs at least 3 characters";
    if (!/^\S+@\S+\.\S+$/.test(form.email)) errs.email = "Enter a valid email";
    if (!form.agree) errs.agree = "Accept the terms to continue";
    setErrors(errs);
    if (Object.keys(errs).length) return;
    console.log("submit", form);
    setForm({ name: "", email: "", plan: "free", agree: false });
  }

  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit} noValidate>
      <input name="name" value={form.name} onChange={handleChange} />
      {errors.name && <small>{errors.name}</small>}
      <input name="email" type="email" value={form.email} onChange={handleChange} />
      {errors.email && <small>{errors.email}</small>}
      <select name="plan" value={form.plan} onChange={handleChange}>
        <option value="free">Free</option>
        <option value="pro">Pro</option>
      </select>
      <textarea name="bio" />   {/* textarea uses value too, not children */}
      <label>
        <input name="agree" type="checkbox" checked={form.agree} onChange={handleChange} /> I agree
      </label>
      <button type="submit">Create account</button>
    </form>
  );
}

      A value without onChange makes a read-only input. Starting with undefined then switching to a string triggers the "uncontrolled to controlled" warning: always initialise with "".

      Uncontrolled inputs

      The DOM keeps the value; you read it when needed with a ref or FormData. Less code, good for simple forms and file inputs.

      jsx
      function QuickForm() {
  function handleSubmit(e) {
    e.preventDefault();
    const data = Object.fromEntries(new FormData(e.currentTarget));
    console.log(data);  // { name: "...", email: "..." }
  }
  return (
    <form onSubmit={handleSubmit}>
      <input name="name" defaultValue="Harsh" />
      <input name="email" />
      <button>Send</button>
    </form>
  );
}

      React 19 form actions

      You can pass a function to a form's action. React calls it with the FormData, handles pending state and resets uncontrolled fields after success. Pairs with useActionState and useFormStatus (see React 19 hooks) and with Next.js Server Actions.

      jsx
      function Newsletter() {
  async function subscribe(formData) {
    await saveEmail(formData.get("email"));
  }
  return (
    <form action={subscribe}>
      <input name="email" type="email" required />
      <button>Subscribe</button>
    </form>
  );
}

      Real-world forms

      For bigger forms use React Hook Form (performance, validation) with Zod (schema validation shared between client and server).

      Styling React apps

      ApproachHowGood for
      Global CSSimport "./index.css"Resets, base styles
      CSS Modulesimport s from "./Card.module.css" → className={s.card}Scoped styles with plain CSS; class names made unique automatically
      Tailwind CSSUtility classes: className="p-4 rounded-lg bg-white"Fast building, very common with Next.js
      Inline stylesstyle={{ width: progress + "%" }}Truly dynamic values only
      CSS-in-JSstyled-components, EmotionOlder codebases; fits client components only
      Component librariesshadcn/ui, MUI, Chakra, Mantine, RadixAccessible prebuilt components
      jsx
      // conditional classes
<button className={`btn ${isActive ? "btn-active" : ""}`}>Save</button>
// with the tiny clsx helper
import clsx from "clsx";
<button className={clsx("btn", { "btn-active": isActive, "btn-disabled": disabled })} />
      Part 2

      Hooks, patterns and the React ecosystem

      Effects, refs, context, reducers, custom hooks, the React 19 additions, how rendering really works, and the libraries almost every React app uses.

      What hooks are, and their rules

      Hooks are functions starting with use that let components "hook into" React features: state, effects, context, refs. (Before hooks, you needed class components for this.)

      The two rules

      1. Only call hooks at the top level of a component or custom hook. Not inside if, loops, nested functions or after an early return. React identifies hooks by their call order, which must be the same every render.
      2. Only call hooks from React functions: components or custom hooks, not regular JS functions or event handlers.
      jsx
      function Profile({ userId }) {
  if (!userId) return null;          // ❌ early return BEFORE a hook
  const [data, setData] = useState(); // hook order now changes between renders
}
// ✅ call hooks first, then return conditionally

      (The new use() API is the exception: it can be called conditionally.) The ESLint plugin eslint-plugin-react-hooks catches these mistakes; keep it on.

      HookOne-line purpose
      useStateRemember a value; changing it re-renders
      useEffectSync with something outside React after render
      useRefHold a DOM node or a value that doesn't cause re-renders
      useContextRead shared data without prop drilling
      useReducerState with many actions, logic in one reducer
      useMemo / useCallbackCache a computed value / a function between renders
      useLayoutEffectEffect that runs before paint (measure layout)
      useIdStable unique ids for accessibility attributes
      useTransition / useDeferredValueMark updates as non-urgent to keep UI responsive
      use, useActionState, useFormStatus, useOptimistic, useEffectEventReact 19 additions for async data, forms and effects
      useSyncExternalStore, useImperativeHandle, useDebugValue, useInsertionEffectRare, library-level hooks

      useEffect

      Effects synchronise your component with something outside React: a network request, a timer, a browser API, a subscription, a third-party widget. They run after the render is painted.
      jsx
      useEffect(() => {
  // setup: runs after render
  return () => {
    // cleanup: runs before the next effect run and when the component unmounts
  };
}, [dependencies]);
      Dependency arrayEffect runs
      none: useEffect(fn)After every render
      empty: useEffect(fn, [])Once after the first render (mount)
      [a, b]On mount and whenever a or b changed

      Common effects

      jsx
      // 1. Document title
useEffect(() => { document.title = `${count} new messages`; }, [count]);

// 2. Timer with cleanup
useEffect(() => {
  const id = setInterval(() => setSeconds(s => s + 1), 1000);
  return () => clearInterval(id);          // without this, intervals pile up
}, []);

// 3. Event listener with cleanup
useEffect(() => {
  const onResize = () => setWidth(window.innerWidth);
  window.addEventListener("resize", onResize);
  return () => window.removeEventListener("resize", onResize);
}, []);

// 4. Fetching data (plain React; in Next.js prefer server components)
useEffect(() => {
  let ignore = false;                      // or AbortController
  setLoading(true);
  fetch(`/api/users/${userId}`)
    .then(r => r.json())
    .then(data => { if (!ignore) setUser(data); })
    .catch(err => { if (!ignore) setError(err.message); })
    .finally(() => { if (!ignore) setLoading(false); });
  return () => { ignore = true; };         // ignore stale responses (race conditions)
}, [userId]);

// 5. Sync to localStorage
useEffect(() => { localStorage.setItem("todos", JSON.stringify(todos)); }, [todos]);

      Include every reactive value you use (props, state, values derived from them) in the dependency array. Lying about dependencies causes stale values. If the linter asks for something you don't want, change the code, not the array.

      Strict Mode runs effects twice in development (setup → cleanup → setup) to expose missing cleanups. It doesn't happen in production. If double-running breaks your app, your cleanup is missing.

      You might not need an effect

      • Deriving data? Calculate during render: const fullName = first + " " + last; — not an effect that sets state.
      • Responding to a user action? Put the code in the event handler (submit → send request), not in an effect watching state.
      • Resetting state when a prop changes? Give the component a key: <Profile key={userId} />.
      • Expensive calculation? useMemo, not effect + state.
      • Fetching data? In real apps use a framework (Next.js server components) or TanStack Query, which handle caching, races and loading states for you.

      An effect that immediately calls setState with something computable from props/state is a red flag: an extra render and possible loops.

      Infinite loop checklist

      • Effect sets state that's in its own dependency list, with no condition.
      • Object/array/function created during render used as a dependency (new reference every render). Move it inside the effect, or memoise it.
      • No dependency array plus setState inside.

      useLayoutEffect

      Same API but runs synchronously after DOM changes and before the browser paints. Use only to measure layout (tooltip positions) and avoid a flicker. Prefer useEffect otherwise.

      useRef

      useRef(initial) returns an object { current: initial } that stays the same across renders. Changing .current does not re-render.

      Use 1: access a DOM element

      jsx
      function SearchBar() {
  const inputRef = useRef(null);
  return (
    <>
      <input ref={inputRef} />
      <button onClick={() => inputRef.current.focus()}>Focus search</button>
    </>
  );
}
// scrollIntoView, play/pause video, measure size, integrate a chart/map library

      Use 2: remember a value without re-rendering

      jsx
      function Stopwatch() {
  const [time, setTime] = useState(0);
  const intervalRef = useRef(null);         // interval id doesn't affect the UI
  const start = () => {
    clearInterval(intervalRef.current);
    intervalRef.current = setInterval(() => setTime(t => t + 10), 10);
  };
  const stop = () => clearInterval(intervalRef.current);
  return <p onClick={start} onDoubleClick={stop}>{(time / 1000).toFixed(2)}s</p>;
}
      stateref
      Change triggers re-renderYesNo
      Value updatesOn the next render (snapshot)Immediately
      Read during renderYesAvoid (except initialisation)
      Use forAnything shown on screenDOM nodes, timer ids, previous values, instances

      Passing refs to your own components

      In React 19, ref is a normal prop for function components: function Input({ ref, ...props }) { return <input ref={ref} {...props} />; }. Older code used forwardRef. useImperativeHandle lets you expose only chosen methods (focus, reset) through a ref.

      Context and prop drilling

      Prop drilling is passing props through many components that don't use them, just to reach a deep child. Context lets a parent make a value available to its whole subtree.

      jsx
      // ThemeContext.jsx
import { createContext, useContext, useState } from "react";

const ThemeContext = createContext(null);          // 1. create

export function ThemeProvider({ children }) {       // 2. provide
  const [theme, setTheme] = useState("light");
  const toggle = () => setTheme(t => (t === "light" ? "dark" : "light"));
  return (
    <ThemeContext value={{ theme, toggle }}>        {/* React 19; older: ThemeContext.Provider */}
      {children}
    </ThemeContext>
  );
}

export function useTheme() {                         // 3. consume via a custom hook
  const ctx = useContext(ThemeContext);
  if (!ctx) throw new Error("useTheme must be used inside ThemeProvider");
  return ctx;
}

// main.jsx:  <ThemeProvider><App /></ThemeProvider>
// anywhere:  const { theme, toggle } = useTheme();
      • Good for: theme, logged-in user, language, feature flags — data many components read and that changes rarely.
      • Every component using a context re-renders when its value changes. For fast-changing, widely shared state, use a store (Zustand, Redux) or split contexts.
      • Before reaching for context, try passing children (composition) — often it removes the drilling.

      useReducer

      When state has many update types (add, edit, delete, toggle, clear), put all the logic in one reducer function: (state, action) => newState. Components just dispatch actions describing what happened.

      jsx
      function todosReducer(state, action) {
  switch (action.type) {
    case "added":
      return [...state, { id: action.id, text: action.text, done: false }];
    case "toggled":
      return state.map(t => (t.id === action.id ? { ...t, done: !t.done } : t));
    case "deleted":
      return state.filter(t => t.id !== action.id);
    case "cleared":
      return [];
    default:
      throw new Error("Unknown action: " + action.type);
  }
}

function TodoApp() {
  const [todos, dispatch] = useReducer(todosReducer, []);
  return (
    <>
      <button onClick={() => dispatch({ type: "added", id: Date.now(), text: "Learn reducers" })}>Add</button>
      {todos.map(t => (
        <li key={t.id} onClick={() => dispatch({ type: "toggled", id: t.id })}>{t.text}</li>
      ))}
    </>
  );
}

      Reducers must be pure (no fetch, no random ids inside). Combine useReducer + context to build a small app-wide store. This is the same pattern Redux uses.

      Performance: memo, useMemo, useCallback and the React Compiler

      Why components re-render

      1. Their own state changed.
      2. Their parent re-rendered (children re-render by default, even with the same props).
      3. A context they use changed.

      Re-rendering is usually cheap; React only touches the DOM where output changed. Optimise only when something is actually slow (measure with the React DevTools Profiler).

      jsx
      // memo: skip re-rendering a child if its props are the same (shallow compare)
const Row = memo(function Row({ item, onSelect }) { /* ... */ });

// useMemo: cache an expensive calculation until dependencies change
const visible = useMemo(() => filterAndSort(products, query), [products, query]);

// useCallback: cache a function so memoised children don't see a "new" prop each render
const handleSelect = useCallback(id => setSelected(id), []);

      useMemo vs useCallback: useMemo(() => fn, deps) caches the result of calling a function; useCallback(fn, deps) caches the function itself. useCallback(fn, d) equals useMemo(() => fn, d).

      The React Compiler

      The React Compiler (stable since late 2025) automatically memoises components and values at build time, so most manual memo/useMemo/useCallback becomes unnecessary. It relies on your code following the rules (pure components, no mutation). Next.js supports it with reactCompiler: true in next.config. Still learn the manual hooks: you'll read them in every existing codebase.

      Other performance tools

      • Code splitting: const Chart = lazy(() => import("./Chart")) + <Suspense fallback={…}> loads code only when needed.
      • Virtualisation for very long lists: render only visible rows (TanStack Virtual, react-window).
      • Move state down: keep state in the smallest component that needs it so fewer components re-render.
      • Transitions / deferred values for heavy updates (next topic).

      Custom hooks

      A custom hook is a function whose name starts with use and that calls other hooks. It lets you reuse stateful logic (not state itself: each component calling it gets its own copy).

      jsx
      // useLocalStorage.js
export function useLocalStorage(key, initial) {
  const [value, setValue] = useState(() => {
    try { return JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem(key)) ?? initial; }
    catch { return initial; }
  });
  useEffect(() => { localStorage.setItem(key, JSON.stringify(value)); }, [key, value]);
  return [value, setValue];
}
const [todos, setTodos] = useLocalStorage("todos", []);

// useDebounce.js
export function useDebounce(value, delay = 400) {
  const [debounced, setDebounced] = useState(value);
  useEffect(() => {
    const t = setTimeout(() => setDebounced(value), delay);
    return () => clearTimeout(t);
  }, [value, delay]);
  return debounced;
}

// useToggle.js
export const useToggle = (init = false) => {
  const [on, setOn] = useState(init);
  const toggle = useCallback(() => setOn(o => !o), []);
  return [on, toggle];
};

      Other classic ones: useFetch, useWindowSize, useOnClickOutside, useMediaQuery, usePrevious, useInterval. Collections like usehooks-ts have tested versions.

      React 19 hooks and APIs

      useTransition and useDeferredValue — keep the UI responsive

      jsx
      const [isPending, startTransition] = useTransition();
function selectTab(tab) {
  startTransition(() => setTab(tab));   // non-urgent: React can interrupt it for typing/clicks
}
{isPending && <Spinner />}

const deferredQuery = useDeferredValue(query);  // lags behind while React is busy
<SlowResults query={deferredQuery} />           // input stays snappy

      In React 19, startTransition accepts async functions ("Actions"): isPending stays true until the await finishes.

      useActionState — form state with an action

      jsx
      async function signup(prevState, formData) {
  const email = formData.get("email");
  if (!email.includes("@")) return { error: "Enter a valid email" };
  await createUser(email);
  return { success: true };
}
function SignupForm() {
  const [state, formAction, isPending] = useActionState(signup, { error: null });
  return (
    <form action={formAction}>
      <input name="email" />
      <button disabled={isPending}>{isPending ? "Creating…" : "Create account"}</button>
      {state.error && <p>{state.error}</p>}
    </form>
  );
}

      useFormStatus — pending state of the parent form

      jsx
      import { useFormStatus } from "react-dom";
function SubmitButton() {
  const { pending } = useFormStatus();    // must be rendered INSIDE the <form>
  return <button disabled={pending}>{pending ? "Saving…" : "Save"}</button>;
}

      useOptimistic — show the result before the server confirms

      jsx
      const [optimisticLikes, addLike] = useOptimistic(likes, (current, n) => current + n);
async function like() {
  addLike(1);                 // UI shows +1 instantly
  await saveLike(postId);     // if it fails, React reverts to the real value
}

      use — read a promise or context

      jsx
      import { use, Suspense } from "react";
function Comments({ commentsPromise }) {
  const comments = use(commentsPromise);  // suspends until resolved
  return comments.map(c => <p key={c.id}>{c.text}</p>);
}
<Suspense fallback={<p>Loading comments…</p>}>
  <Comments commentsPromise={fetchComments()} />   {/* promise created by a server component / cached */}
</Suspense>
const theme = use(ThemeContext);   // like useContext, but allowed inside if-statements

      useEffectEvent (React 19.2)

      Extracts "event-like" logic from an effect so it always reads the latest props/state without making the effect re-run.

      jsx
      const onConnected = useEffectEvent(() => showToast(`Connected to ${roomId}`, theme));
useEffect(() => {
  const conn = connect(roomId);
  conn.on("connected", onConnected);   // theme changes won't reconnect
  return () => conn.disconnect();
}, [roomId]);

      Other React 19 changes worth knowing

      • ref as a regular prop (no forwardRef), and ref callbacks can return a cleanup function.
      • <Context value> works as the provider.
      • Render <title>, <meta> and <link> anywhere; React hoists them to <head>.
      • <Activity mode="hidden"> (19.2) hides part of the UI while keeping its state, and can pre-render it in the background.
      • useId() for matching id/htmlFor/aria-* without clashes: const id = useId();

      How rendering works (and the component lifecycle)

      Trigger → render → commit

      1. Trigger: first mount (createRoot().render) or a state update.
      2. Render: React calls your component functions to get the new JSX. Pure calculation, nothing on screen yet. May run more than once or be interrupted.
      3. Commit: React applies the minimal DOM changes, then the browser paints, then effects run.

      Lifecycle in hook terms

      MomentFunction componentOld class equivalent
      MountuseEffect(fn, [])componentDidMount
      UpdateuseEffect(fn, [deps])componentDidUpdate
      Unmountcleanup returned from the effectcomponentWillUnmount
      Error catchingError boundary (still a class, or a library)componentDidCatch

      State and position in the tree

      • React ties state to a component's position in the tree. Same component at the same position → state kept. Different type at that position → state destroyed.
      • Changing a component's key forces React to treat it as new and reset its state: <ChatBox key={contactId} />.

      Strict Mode

      <StrictMode> in development double-invokes component bodies, initialisers and effects to surface impure code and missing cleanups. Seeing logs twice is expected in dev only.

      Class components (for reading old code)

      jsx
      class Counter extends React.Component {
  state = { count: 0 };
  componentDidMount() { /* ... */ }
  render() {
    return <button onClick={() => this.setState({ count: this.state.count + 1 })}>{this.state.count}</button>;
  }
}

      New code uses function components and hooks.

      Component patterns

      Container / presentational

      One component fetches and manages data, another just displays props. Makes UI pieces reusable and testable. In Next.js this maps neatly to a server component (data) rendering a client component (interaction).

      Composition with children and "slots"

      jsx
      function Layout({ sidebar, children }) {
  return <div className="grid"><aside>{sidebar}</aside><main>{children}</main></div>;
}
<Layout sidebar={<Filters />}><ProductGrid /></Layout>

      Compound components

      jsx
      <Tabs defaultValue="profile">
  <Tabs.List>
    <Tabs.Trigger value="profile">Profile</Tabs.Trigger>
    <Tabs.Trigger value="billing">Billing</Tabs.Trigger>
  </Tabs.List>
  <Tabs.Panel value="profile">…</Tabs.Panel>
</Tabs>
// parent shares state with its parts through context

      Controlled vs uncontrolled components

      A reusable component is controlled when the parent passes value + onChange, uncontrolled when it keeps its own state (defaultValue). Good libraries support both.

      Render props and higher-order components (older patterns)

      jsx
      <MouseTracker render={({ x, y }) => <Dot x={x} y={y} />} />   // render prop
const ProtectedPage = withAuth(Page);                          // HOC wraps a component

      Custom hooks replaced most uses of both; recognise them in older code.

      Portals

      Render children into a different DOM node (e.g. document.body) while keeping them in the React tree. Used for modals, toasts and tooltips so parent overflow: hidden or z-index doesn't clip them.

      jsx
      import { createPortal } from "react-dom";
function Modal({ open, onClose, children }) {
  if (!open) return null;
  return createPortal(
    <div className="backdrop" onClick={onClose}>
      <div className="modal" role="dialog" aria-modal="true" onClick={e => e.stopPropagation()}>{children}</div>
    </div>,
    document.body
  );
}

      Suspense, lazy loading and error boundaries

      Suspense

      <Suspense fallback={…}> shows a fallback while something inside is "not ready" (lazy code, data read with use, a streaming server component). Nest several boundaries so parts of the page load independently.

      jsx
      const Dashboard = lazy(() => import("./Dashboard"));   // separate JS chunk
<Suspense fallback={<PageSkeleton />}>
  <Dashboard />
</Suspense>

      Error boundaries

      An error boundary catches rendering errors in its subtree and shows a fallback instead of a blank screen. They don't catch errors in event handlers or async code (use try/catch there).

      jsx
      // the simplest route: the react-error-boundary package
import { ErrorBoundary } from "react-error-boundary";
<ErrorBoundary fallbackRender={({ error, resetErrorBoundary }) => (
  <div role="alert">
    <p>This widget failed to load: {error.message}</p>
    <button onClick={resetErrorBoundary}>Try again</button>
  </div>
)}>
  <Widget />
</ErrorBoundary>

      Next.js creates error boundaries for you from error.tsx files (Part 3).

      Routing in plain React (React Router)

      A Vite React app is one page; React Router maps URLs to components without full page reloads. (Next.js has its own file-based router, so you won't use this there.)

      jsx
      // npm install react-router
import { BrowserRouter, Routes, Route, Link, NavLink, useParams, useNavigate, Outlet } from "react-router";

function App() {
  return (
    <BrowserRouter>
      <nav>
        <NavLink to="/">Home</NavLink>
        <NavLink to="/products">Products</NavLink>
      </nav>
      <Routes>
        <Route path="/" element={<Home />} />
        <Route path="/products" element={<ProductsLayout />}>
          <Route index element={<ProductList />} />
          <Route path=":id" element={<ProductDetail />} />   {/* dynamic segment */}
        </Route>
        <Route path="*" element={<NotFound />} />
      </Routes>
    </BrowserRouter>
  );
}
function ProductsLayout() { return <><h1>Products</h1><Outlet /></>; } // nested routes render here
function ProductDetail() {
  const { id } = useParams();
  const navigate = useNavigate();
  return <button onClick={() => navigate("/products")}>Back from {id}</button>;
}

      React Router v7 also has a "framework mode" (formerly Remix) with loaders and actions. TanStack Router is a typed alternative.

      Server state and client state libraries

      Two kinds of state. Server state (data from an API: products, users) needs caching, refetching and loading/error handling. Client state (UI: open modals, cart before checkout, theme) lives only in the browser. Use the right tool for each.

      TanStack Query (server state)

      jsx
      // npm install @tanstack/react-query ; wrap app in <QueryClientProvider client={new QueryClient()}>
const { data, isPending, error } = useQuery({
  queryKey: ["products", category],               // cache key; refetches when it changes
  queryFn: () => fetch(`/api/products?c=${category}`).then(r => r.json()),
  staleTime: 60_000,
});

const qc = useQueryClient();
const addProduct = useMutation({
  mutationFn: newP => fetch("/api/products", { method: "POST", body: JSON.stringify(newP) }),
  onSuccess: () => qc.invalidateQueries({ queryKey: ["products"] }),  // refresh the list
});

      Gives caching, deduping, background refetch, retries, pagination and infinite scroll. SWR is a lighter alternative. In Next.js, server components often replace this for initial data.

      Zustand (client state, simple)

      jsx
      import { create } from "zustand";
export const useCart = create(set => ({
  items: [],
  add: item => set(s => ({ items: [...s.items, item] })),
  clear: () => set({ items: [] }),
}));
const count = useCart(s => s.items.length);   // subscribes only to what you select

      Redux Toolkit (client state, large teams)

      A single store, slices with reducers (createSlice, Immer built-in), useSelector / useDispatch, and RTK Query for data fetching. Common in big existing codebases.

      NeedReach for
      State used by one componentuseState
      Shared by a few nearby componentsLift state up
      Read in many places, changes rarelyContext
      Complex app-wide client stateZustand / Redux Toolkit / Jotai
      Data from a serverServer components (Next.js), TanStack Query, SWR
      Should survive a refresh / be shareableThe URL (search params), localStorage, a database

      TypeScript with React

      TypeScript adds types to JavaScript and catches mistakes (wrong prop, missing field, typo) as you type. Next.js projects default to it; files become .tsx.

      tsx
      type ButtonProps = {
  label: string;
  variant?: "primary" | "ghost";           // optional, union of literals
  onClick: () => void;
  children?: React.ReactNode;               // anything renderable
};
export function Button({ label, variant = "primary", onClick }: ButtonProps) {
  return <button className={variant} onClick={onClick}>{label}</button>;
}

type User = { id: number; name: string; email?: string };
const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);
const [items, setItems] = useState<string[]>([]);
const inputRef = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);

function handleChange(e: React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>) { setName(e.target.value); }
function handleSubmit(e: React.FormEvent<HTMLFormElement>) { e.preventDefault(); }

// extend native element props
type InputProps = React.ComponentProps<"input"> & { label: string };

      Start by typing props and API responses; let TypeScript infer the rest.

      Testing and accessibility

      Testing

      • Vitest (or Jest) runs tests; React Testing Library renders components and queries them like a user would.
      • Playwright or Cypress for end-to-end tests in a real browser.
      • MSW (Mock Service Worker) to fake API responses.
      jsx
      import { render, screen } from "@testing-library/react";
import userEvent from "@testing-library/user-event";
test("increments the counter", async () => {
  render(<Counter />);
  await userEvent.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: "+1" }));
  expect(screen.getByText("Count: 1")).toBeInTheDocument();
});

      Accessibility checklist

      • Use real elements: <button> for actions, <a>/<Link> for navigation, not clickable <div>s.
      • Every input has a <label> (htmlFor + id, via useId).
      • Images have meaningful alt (or alt="" if decorative).
      • Visible focus styles; everything works with the keyboard; modals trap focus and close on Escape.
      • Announce async changes with aria-live; use headings in order.
      Part 3

      Next.js

      The React framework for full-stack apps: file-based routing, server components, data fetching, caching, server actions, SEO and deployment. Written for Next.js 16 and the App Router.

      What Next.js adds to React

      React builds UI. Next.js builds the whole app around it: routing, server rendering, data fetching, caching, API endpoints, image/font optimisation, SEO and production builds, all configured for you.
      Problem in a plain React SPAWhat Next.js does
      Empty HTML until JS loads: slow first paint, weaker SEORenders HTML on the server or at build time
      Need a router libraryFolders in app/ are routes
      Need a separate backend for APIs and secretsServer Components, Server Actions and Route Handlers run on the server
      Fetching in effects causes waterfalls and spinnersFetch directly in async server components, stream with Suspense
      Big JS bundlesServer Components send zero JS; automatic code splitting per route
      Manual image/font optimisationnext/image, next/font

      App Router vs Pages Router

      Next.js has two routers. The App Router (app/ folder, since v13) supports Server Components, layouts, streaming and Server Actions; it's the one to learn. The Pages Router (pages/) is the older system still found in many codebases (see the last Next.js topic).

      Where Next.js is today

      As of September 2026 the current line is Next.js 16 (16.3 released August 2026). Version 16 made Turbopack the default bundler, introduced Cache Components ("use cache"), renamed middleware.ts to proxy.ts, made params, searchParams, cookies() and headers() async, and ships with React 19.2. Older tutorials (Next 13/14) show patterns that have changed, so check the version when you read one.

      Project setup and special files

      bash
      npx create-next-app@latest my-app     # recommended defaults: TypeScript, Tailwind, ESLint, App Router, Turbopack
cd my-app
npm run dev        # http://localhost:3000
npm run build      # production build
npm run start      # serve the production build
      text
      my-app/
├─ app/
│  ├─ layout.tsx        ← root layout (required): <html> and <body>
│  ├─ page.tsx          ← the "/" route
│  ├─ globals.css
│  ├─ favicon.ico
│  ├─ about/page.tsx    ← "/about"
│  └─ api/hello/route.ts← "/api/hello" endpoint
├─ components/          ← shared components (not routes)
├─ lib/                 ← helpers, db client
├─ public/              ← static files served from "/" (public/logo.png → /logo.png)
├─ proxy.ts             ← runs before requests (optional)
├─ next.config.ts
└─ package.json

      Special file names inside app/

      FilePurpose
      page.tsxThe UI of a route. A folder only becomes a URL when it has a page (or route).
      layout.tsxShared UI wrapping a segment and its children; keeps state across navigation
      template.tsxLike a layout but remounts on every navigation
      loading.tsxInstant loading UI (automatic Suspense boundary)
      error.tsxError boundary for the segment (must be a client component)
      global-error.tsxError boundary for the root layout
      not-found.tsxUI for notFound() and unknown URLs
      route.tsAPI endpoint (Route Handler) instead of a page
      default.tsxFallback for parallel routes
      opengraph-image.tsx, icon.tsx, sitemap.ts, robots.ts, manifest.tsGenerated metadata files

      Nesting order for one segment: layout → template → error boundary → loading (Suspense) → not-found → page.

      Any other file (components, utils, styles) can sit inside app/ safely; only special files become public. The @/ import alias points to the project root (or src/): import Button from "@/components/Button".

      File-based routing

      FolderURLKind
      app/page.tsx/Root
      app/blog/page.tsx/blogStatic segment
      app/blog/[slug]/page.tsx/blog/hello-worldDynamic segment
      app/shop/[...parts]/page.tsx/shop/a/b/cCatch-all (1+ segments)
      app/docs/[[...parts]]/page.tsx/docs and /docs/a/bOptional catch-all
      app/(marketing)/pricing/page.tsx/pricingRoute group: organises without affecting the URL; can have its own layout
      app/_components/—Private folder: never routable
      app/@modal/…—Parallel route slot (render two pages in one layout)
      app/feed/(..)photo/[id]—Intercepting route (open a photo in a modal over the feed)

      Reading params and search params (server component)

      tsx
      // app/blog/[slug]/page.tsx  →  /blog/react-hooks?tab=comments
export default async function BlogPost({
  params,
  searchParams,
}: {
  params: Promise<{ slug: string }>;
  searchParams: Promise<{ [key: string]: string | string[] | undefined }>;
}) {
  const { slug } = await params;            // Next 15+: params are a Promise
  const { tab = "content" } = await searchParams;
  const post = await getPost(slug);
  return <article><h1>{post.title}</h1>{tab === "comments" && <Comments id={post.id} />}</article>;
}
// Shortcut type helper (Next 15.5+): export default async function Page(props: PageProps<"/blog/[slug]">)

      In client components, read them with hooks: useParams(), useSearchParams(), usePathname() from next/navigation.

      Pre-building dynamic pages

      tsx
      // Build /blog/a, /blog/b … as static HTML at build time
export async function generateStaticParams() {
  const posts = await getAllPosts();
  return posts.map(p => ({ slug: p.slug }));
}

      Layouts and navigation

      tsx
      // app/layout.tsx — root layout
import "./globals.css";
import type { Metadata } from "next";

export const metadata: Metadata = { title: "My Store", description: "Shop the best stuff" };

export default function RootLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return (
    <html lang="en">
      <body>
        <Navbar />
        {children}          {/* the current page (or nested layout) */}
        <Footer />
      </body>
    </html>
  );
}

// app/dashboard/layout.tsx — wraps every /dashboard/* page
export default function DashboardLayout({ children }: { children: React.ReactNode }) {
  return <div className="flex"><Sidebar /><main>{children}</main></div>;
}
      • Layouts nest: /dashboard/settings renders root layout → dashboard layout → settings page.
      • Layouts don't re-render or lose state when you navigate between their child pages (partial rendering).
      • Layouts can't read searchParams or the current pathname directly (use a client component with hooks).

      Link and programmatic navigation

      tsx
      import Link from "next/link";
<Link href="/about">About</Link>
<Link href={`/blog/${post.slug}`}>{post.title}</Link>
<Link href={{ pathname: "/search", query: { q: "shoes" } }}>Shoes</Link>
<Link href="/big-page" prefetch={false}>…</Link>   // prefetching is automatic for visible links
      tsx
      "use client";
import { useRouter, usePathname, useSearchParams } from "next/navigation";  // NOT next/router
export function Controls() {
  const router = useRouter();
  const pathname = usePathname();          // "/dashboard/settings"
  const searchParams = useSearchParams();  // read-only URLSearchParams
  return (
    <>
      <button onClick={() => router.push("/dashboard")}>Go</button>
      <button onClick={() => router.back()}>Back</button>
      <button onClick={() => router.refresh()}>Refetch server data</button>
      <Link href="/settings" className={pathname === "/settings" ? "active" : ""}>Settings</Link>
    </>
  );
}

      Server-side navigation helpers

      tsx
      import { redirect, permanentRedirect, notFound } from "next/navigation";
if (!session) redirect("/login");           // in server components, actions, route handlers
if (!product) notFound();                   // renders the nearest not-found.tsx

      Store UI state in the URL when it should be shareable or survive refresh: filters, tabs, search, pagination. Update with router.replace("?" + params.toString()) and read with searchParams on the server.

      Server Components vs Client Components

      In the App Router every component is a Server Component by default. It runs only on the server and sends rendered output, with zero JavaScript to the browser. Add "use client" at the top of a file when a component needs interactivity.
      Need to…Server ComponentClient Component
      Fetch data, query a database, read files✅ directly with awaitvia API / server action / props
      Use secrets (API keys, DB passwords)✅ safe❌ everything here is sent to the browser
      Keep big libraries off the client (markdown, date libs)✅❌ adds to bundle
      useState, useEffect, useReducer, context❌✅
      onClick, onChange, other event handlers❌✅
      Browser APIs (window, localStorage, geolocation)❌✅ (inside effects/handlers)
      Be an async function✅❌ (use use() or a library)
      tsx
      // app/products/page.tsx — server component (default)
import AddToCart from "./AddToCart";
export default async function Products() {
  const products = await db.product.findMany();        // runs on the server
  return products.map(p => (
    <div key={p.id}>
      <h3>{p.name}</h3>
      <AddToCart productId={p.id} />                     {/* small interactive island */}
    </div>
  ));
}

// app/products/AddToCart.tsx
"use client";
import { useState } from "react";
export default function AddToCart({ productId }: { productId: string }) {
  const [added, setAdded] = useState(false);
  return <button onClick={() => setAdded(true)}>{added ? "Added ✓" : "Add to cart"}</button>;
}

      The rules of the boundary

      • "use client" marks a boundary: that file and everything it imports becomes client code. Put it as low in the tree as possible (on the button, not the page).
      • Server components can render client components and pass them props. Props must be serialisable: strings, numbers, plain objects/arrays, Dates, promises, JSX — not functions (except Server Actions) or class instances.
      • A client component can't import a server component, but it can receive one as children or another prop: <ClientModal><ServerDetails /></ClientModal>.
      • Context providers are client components; wrap them in a Providers.tsx with "use client" and use it in the root layout.
      • Client components still render to HTML on the server for the first load, then hydrate in the browser (attach event handlers). Code that touches window must be in effects or handlers.
      • Protect server-only modules with import "server-only"; so an accidental client import fails the build.

      Hydration mismatch errors happen when server HTML differs from the first client render: using Date.now(), Math.random(), window or localStorage during render, or invalid HTML nesting (a <div> inside a <p>). Move such code into useEffect.

      Data fetching and streaming

      Fetch in async server components

      tsx
      // app/users/page.tsx
export default async function UsersPage() {
  const res = await fetch("https://api.example.com/users");
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error("Failed to load users");   // caught by error.tsx
  const users: User[] = await res.json();
  return <ul>{users.map(u => <li key={u.id}>{u.name}</li>)}</ul>;
}

// or go straight to the database — no API route needed
import { db } from "@/lib/db";
const orders = await db.order.findMany({ where: { userId } });

      Avoid waterfalls: fetch in parallel

      tsx
      // ❌ sequential: the second waits for the first
const user = await getUser(id);
const posts = await getPosts(id);

// ✅ parallel
const [user, posts] = await Promise.all([getUser(id), getPosts(id)]);

      Streaming with loading.tsx and Suspense

      Instead of waiting for all data before sending anything, Next.js sends the page shell immediately and streams in slow parts as they finish.

      tsx
      // app/dashboard/loading.tsx — shown instantly while page.tsx loads
export default function Loading() { return <DashboardSkeleton />; }

// finer control inside a page
import { Suspense } from "react";
export default function Dashboard() {
  return (
    <>
      <h1>Dashboard</h1>                                  {/* sent immediately */}
      <Suspense fallback={<p>Loading revenue…</p>}>
        <Revenue />                                        {/* async component, streams in */}
      </Suspense>
      <Suspense fallback={<p>Loading orders…</p>}>
        <LatestOrders />
      </Suspense>
    </>
  );
}

      Passing a promise to a client component

      tsx
      // server: start the fetch, don't await it
const reviewsPromise = getReviews(id);
<Suspense fallback={<p>Loading reviews…</p>}><Reviews reviewsPromise={reviewsPromise} /></Suspense>

// client
"use client";
import { use } from "react";
export function Reviews({ reviewsPromise }) { const reviews = use(reviewsPromise); /* … */ }

      Deduplicating requests

      Identical fetch calls during one render are deduplicated automatically. For database calls, wrap the function in React's cache() so a layout and page calling getUser(id) hit the DB once per request.

      tsx
      import { cache } from "react";
export const getUser = cache(async (id: string) => db.user.findUnique({ where: { id } }));

      Client-side fetching still has a place

      For data that changes with user interaction after load (infinite scroll, live search, polling), fetch from a client component with TanStack Query or SWR, calling a Route Handler or Server Action.

      Rendering and caching

      The question for every piece of data: can it be computed once and reused (static/cached), or must it be fresh for each request (dynamic)? Next.js 16's answer is Cache Components: dynamic by default, and you mark what to cache with "use cache".

      Static vs dynamic rendering

      • Static: HTML generated at build time (or first request), served from cache/CDN. Fastest. Good for marketing pages, blogs, docs.
      • Dynamic: generated per request. Needed when output depends on the request: await cookies(), await headers(), searchParams, logged-in user, real-time data.
      • Partial Prerendering: one page can have a static shell (sent instantly) with dynamic holes that stream in inside <Suspense>. With Cache Components this is how pages render by default.

      Cache Components (Next.js 16)

      ts
      // next.config.ts
import type { NextConfig } from "next";
const nextConfig: NextConfig = { cacheComponents: true };
export default nextConfig;
      tsx
      import { cacheLife, cacheTag } from "next/cache";

// cache a data function
export async function getProducts() {
  "use cache";
  cacheLife("hours");            // presets: seconds, minutes, hours, days, weeks, max (or custom)
  cacheTag("products");          // label it so you can invalidate it later
  return db.product.findMany();
}

// or cache a whole component / page
export default async function BlogIndex() {
  "use cache";
  const posts = await getPosts();
  return <PostList posts={posts} />;
}

      Anything not cached and depending on request data must be wrapped in <Suspense> so the rest of the page can still prerender. The build tells you when a dynamic access is missing a boundary.

      Invalidating cached data

      API (from next/cache)Use
      updateTag("products")In a Server Action: expire the tag and show fresh data immediately (read-your-own-writes)
      revalidateTag("products", "max")Mark stale; serve old data while refetching in the background (stale-while-revalidate)
      revalidatePath("/products")Refresh everything for a route
      refresh()In a Server Action: refresh uncached data on the current page
      cacheLife(...)Time-based expiry (ISR-style)

      Without Cache Components (older/default model)

      • Routes are prerendered statically unless they use request-time APIs; fetch isn't cached by default since Next 15.
      • Opt in per fetch: fetch(url, { next: { revalidate: 3600, tags: ["products"] } }) or { cache: "force-cache" }.
      • Route segment config: export const revalidate = 60; (ISR), export const dynamic = "force-dynamic";.
      • unstable_cache wrapped non-fetch functions (now replaced by "use cache").

      You'll meet both models in tutorials. Recognise them, but for new Next.js 16 projects prefer Cache Components. On the client, the Router Cache keeps visited routes so back/forward is instant; router.refresh() refetches the current route.

      Server Actions (mutations)

      A Server Action is an async function marked "use server" that runs on the server but can be called from a form or a client component. No API route or fetch code needed: Next.js creates the endpoint for you.
      ts
      // app/actions.ts
"use server";
import { z } from "zod";
import { updateTag } from "next/cache";
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";
import { auth } from "@/lib/auth";

const TodoSchema = z.object({ text: z.string().min(1, "Write something first").max(200) });

export async function addTodo(prevState: { error?: string }, formData: FormData) {
  const session = await auth();                       // 1. authenticate EVERY time
  if (!session) return { error: "Log in to add todos" };

  const parsed = TodoSchema.safeParse({ text: formData.get("text") });  // 2. validate
  if (!parsed.success) return { error: parsed.error.issues[0].message };

  await db.todo.create({ data: { text: parsed.data.text, userId: session.user.id } }); // 3. mutate
  updateTag("todos");                                 // 4. refresh cached data
  return {};
}

export async function deleteTodo(id: string) {
  "use server";
  // ...check the todo belongs to the user, then delete
  redirect("/todos");                                 // optional: navigate after
}
      tsx
      // app/todos/AddTodoForm.tsx
"use client";
import { useActionState } from "react";
import { addTodo } from "../actions";

export function AddTodoForm() {
  const [state, action, pending] = useActionState(addTodo, {});
  return (
    <form action={action}>
      <input name="text" placeholder="New todo" />
      <button disabled={pending}>{pending ? "Adding…" : "Add todo"}</button>
      {state.error && <p role="alert">{state.error}</p>}
    </form>
  );
}
      • Works without JavaScript when used as a form action in a server component (progressive enhancement).
      • Call from event handlers too: onClick={() => startTransition(() => deleteTodo(id))}.
      • Pass extra arguments with bind: <form action={deleteTodo.bind(null, todo.id)}>.
      • Combine with useOptimistic for instant UI.

      Server Actions are public HTTP endpoints. Anyone can call them with any data. Always check authentication and authorisation, and validate input, inside the action itself, never only in the UI. Keep them for mutations; use server components for reading data.

      Route Handlers (API endpoints)

      A route.ts file exports functions named after HTTP methods. Use them for webhooks, public APIs, mobile apps, file downloads, or anything an external client calls.

      ts
      // app/api/products/route.ts  →  GET/POST /api/products
import { NextRequest } from "next/server";

export async function GET(request: NextRequest) {
  const q = request.nextUrl.searchParams.get("q") ?? "";
  const products = await db.product.findMany({ where: { name: { contains: q } } });
  return Response.json(products);
}

export async function POST(request: Request) {
  const body = await request.json();
  // validate + auth here
  const product = await db.product.create({ data: body });
  return Response.json(product, { status: 201 });
}

// app/api/products/[id]/route.ts
export async function DELETE(_req: Request, { params }: { params: Promise<{ id: string }> }) {
  const { id } = await params;
  await db.product.delete({ where: { id } });
  return new Response(null, { status: 204 });
}
      • Supported: GET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE, HEAD, OPTIONS.
      • Read cookies/headers with await cookies() / await headers() from next/headers.
      • A folder can't have both page.tsx and route.ts.
      • Don't call your own Route Handlers from server components; call the underlying function directly.
      Use a…When
      Server ComponentReading data for a page
      Server ActionMutations from your own UI (forms, buttons)
      Route HandlerEndpoints called by others (webhooks, mobile apps, third parties) or non-HTML responses

      proxy.ts (formerly middleware)

      proxy.ts at the project root runs before a request is completed. Use it for redirects, rewrites, setting headers/cookies, and quick checks such as sending logged-out users to /login. In Next.js 16 the file and function were renamed from middleware to proxy and it runs on the Node.js runtime.

      ts
      // proxy.ts
import { NextResponse, type NextRequest } from "next/server";

export function proxy(request: NextRequest) {
  const token = request.cookies.get("session")?.value;
  if (!token && request.nextUrl.pathname.startsWith("/dashboard")) {
    return NextResponse.redirect(new URL("/login", request.url));
  }
  const res = NextResponse.next();
  res.headers.set("x-app-version", "1.0");
  return res;
}

export const config = {
  matcher: ["/dashboard/:path*", "/account/:path*"],   // only run where needed
};

      Treat proxy as a fast first filter, not your only security check. Verify the session again in pages, Server Actions and Route Handlers that read or change private data.

      Metadata and SEO

      tsx
      // static metadata (layout.tsx or page.tsx)
export const metadata: Metadata = {
  title: { default: "My Store", template: "%s | My Store" },   // child pages: "Shoes | My Store"
  description: "Handmade goods, shipped across India",
  openGraph: { images: ["/og.png"] },
  metadataBase: new URL("https://mystore.com"),
};

// dynamic metadata
export async function generateMetadata({ params }: { params: Promise<{ slug: string }> }): Promise<Metadata> {
  const { slug } = await params;
  const product = await getProduct(slug);
  return { title: product.name, description: product.summary, openGraph: { images: [product.image] } };
}

      File-based metadata

      ts
      // app/sitemap.ts
export default async function sitemap() {
  const posts = await getPosts();
  return [{ url: "https://mysite.com", lastModified: new Date() },
          ...posts.map(p => ({ url: `https://mysite.com/blog/${p.slug}`, lastModified: p.updatedAt }))];
}
// app/robots.ts
export default function robots() {
  return { rules: { userAgent: "*", allow: "/", disallow: "/admin" }, sitemap: "https://mysite.com/sitemap.xml" };
}

      Also: favicon.ico/icon.png, opengraph-image.tsx (generate social images with ImageResponse from next/og), manifest.ts. Metadata streams in without blocking the page.

      Images, fonts, scripts and styling

      next/image

      tsx
      import Image from "next/image";
import hero from "@/public/hero.jpg";          // local import: width/height/blur known automatically

<Image src={hero} alt="Team at work" placeholder="blur" priority />   // priority for above-the-fold
<Image src="https://cdn.shop.com/p/1.jpg" alt="Blue sneaker" width={400} height={400} />
<div className="relative h-64"><Image src={url} alt="" fill sizes="(max-width:768px) 100vw, 33vw" className="object-cover" /></div>
      • Resizes, converts to WebP/AVIF, lazy-loads and prevents layout shift automatically.
      • Remote images need their host allowed in next.config: images: { remotePatterns: [{ protocol: "https", hostname: "cdn.shop.com" }] }.
      • Use fill + sizes when the image should fill a sized parent.

      next/font

      tsx
      // app/layout.tsx
import { Inter, JetBrains_Mono } from "next/font/google";
const inter = Inter({ subsets: ["latin"], variable: "--font-sans" });
const mono = JetBrains_Mono({ subsets: ["latin"], variable: "--font-mono" });
<html lang="en" className={`${inter.variable} ${mono.variable}`}>
// local files: import localFont from "next/font/local"

      Fonts are downloaded at build time and self-hosted: no request to Google from the browser, no layout shift.

      next/script

      tsx
      import Script from "next/script";
<Script src="https://example.com/analytics.js" strategy="afterInteractive" />  // or lazyOnload, beforeInteractive

      Styling

      Global CSS goes in the root layout; CSS Modules (*.module.css), Tailwind CSS (set up by create-next-app) and Sass work out of the box. CSS-in-JS libraries that rely on runtime JS only work in client components. shadcn/ui + Tailwind is a very common Next.js combination.

      Environment variables and configuration

      bash
      # .env.local  (never commit; add to .gitignore)
DATABASE_URL="postgres://..."
STRIPE_SECRET_KEY="sk_live_..."
NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL="https://mysite.com"
      • Plain variables are server-only: process.env.DATABASE_URL works in server components, actions and route handlers; it's undefined in the browser.
      • Prefix with NEXT_PUBLIC_ to expose to the browser. These are inlined at build time, so never put secrets there.
      • Files: .env (all), .env.local (local overrides), .env.development, .env.production. Set real values in your host's dashboard for production.
      ts
      // next.config.ts
import type { NextConfig } from "next";
const nextConfig: NextConfig = {
  cacheComponents: true,
  reactCompiler: true,
  images: { remotePatterns: [{ protocol: "https", hostname: "images.unsplash.com" }] },
  async redirects() { return [{ source: "/old-blog/:slug", destination: "/blog/:slug", permanent: true }]; },
  // output: "standalone",   // for Docker/self-hosting
};
export default nextConfig;

      Turbopack is the default bundler for next dev and next build in v16. next lint was removed; run ESLint (or Biome) directly.

      Authentication

      Authentication = who you are (login). Authorisation = what you're allowed to do. Common choices with Next.js:

      OptionNotes
      Auth.js (NextAuth)Open source; OAuth (Google, GitHub), email, credentials; sessions in cookies or DB
      Better AuthOpen source, TypeScript-first, plugins for 2FA, organisations
      Clerk, Kinde, WorkOSHosted services with prebuilt UI
      Supabase Auth, Firebase AuthCome with their database platforms

      The pattern, whatever library you use

      tsx
      // lib/dal.ts — a small "data access layer"
import "server-only";
import { cache } from "react";
import { cookies } from "next/headers";
import { redirect } from "next/navigation";

export const getSession = cache(async () => {
  const token = (await cookies()).get("session")?.value;
  return token ? await verifySession(token) : null;   // decrypt / look up in DB
});

export async function requireUser() {
  const session = await getSession();
  if (!session) redirect("/login");
  return session.user;
}

// app/dashboard/page.tsx
export default async function Dashboard() {
  const user = await requireUser();          // check where the data is read
  const invoices = await db.invoice.findMany({ where: { userId: user.id } });
  return <InvoiceList invoices={invoices} />;
}
      • Store sessions in HttpOnly, Secure, SameSite cookies (JS can't read them).
      • Check auth close to the data: in pages, Server Actions and Route Handlers. proxy.ts is only an early redirect.
      • Hash passwords (bcrypt/argon2) if you store them yourself; better, use a library.

      Databases and ORMs

      Server components and Server Actions can talk to a database directly. An ORM gives you typed queries instead of raw SQL.

      ts
      // Prisma: schema.prisma defines models → npx prisma migrate dev
const user = await prisma.user.create({ data: { email, name } });
const posts = await prisma.post.findMany({ where: { published: true }, include: { author: true }, orderBy: { createdAt: "desc" } });

// Drizzle: schema in TypeScript, SQL-like API
const rows = await db.select().from(posts).where(eq(posts.published, true));
      PiecePopular choices
      DatabasePostgreSQL (Neon, Supabase), MySQL (PlanetScale), SQLite (Turso), MongoDB (with Mongoose)
      ORM / query builderPrisma, Drizzle
      ValidationZod (share schemas between forms and actions)
      File uploadsS3 / R2 / Vercel Blob / UploadThing
      PaymentsStripe, Razorpay (webhooks → Route Handler)
      EmailResend + React Email

      In development, hot reload can create many DB clients. Create one client in lib/db.ts and reuse it (store it on globalThis in dev).

      Error handling and not-found pages

      tsx
      // app/dashboard/error.tsx — catches errors in this segment
"use client";
export default function Error({ error, reset }: { error: Error & { digest?: string }; reset: () => void }) {
  return (
    <div role="alert">
      <h2>The dashboard couldn't load.</h2>
      <p>Check your connection and try again.</p>
      <button onClick={() => reset()}>Try again</button>
    </div>
  );
}

// app/not-found.tsx
import Link from "next/link";
export default function NotFound() {
  return <><h2>This page doesn't exist</h2><Link href="/">Go to the homepage</Link></>;
}
      • error.tsx doesn't catch errors in the layout.tsx of the same segment; the parent's error file does. global-error.tsx covers the root layout (it must render its own <html> and <body>).
      • In production, server error messages are hidden from the client (only a digest id) so secrets don't leak.
      • Expected errors (validation, "email taken") should be returned as values from actions and shown in the form, not thrown.
      • Log/monitor with Sentry or similar; instrumentation.ts is the hook for observability setup.

      Building and deploying

      bash
      npm run build     # compiles, prerenders static pages, prints a route summary
npm run start     # runs the production server locally
      TargetHow
      VercelImport the Git repo; every push deploys, every PR gets a preview URL. Zero config.
      Other platformsNetlify, Cloudflare (OpenNext), AWS Amplify, Google Cloud: supported through the Adapter API (stable in 16.2)
      Node server / VPSnext build then next start behind a reverse proxy (pm2, nginx)
      Dockeroutput: "standalone" produces a minimal server folder to copy into an image
      Static hosting onlyoutput: "export" → plain HTML/CSS/JS in out/ (no server features: no actions, no dynamic rendering)

      Before you ship

      • Set environment variables on the host; never commit .env* files with secrets.
      • Run npm run build locally: type errors and missing Suspense boundaries show up here.
      • Check Lighthouse / Core Web Vitals (LCP, CLS, INP); use priority on the hero image.
      • Keep Next.js patched: security releases now arrive on a regular monthly schedule.

      The Pages Router (for older codebases)

      Many existing apps still use pages/. You'll recognise it by these patterns:

      tsx
      // pages/index.tsx → "/"     pages/blog/[slug].tsx → "/blog/:slug"     pages/api/hello.ts → API
// pages/_app.tsx wraps every page; pages/_document.tsx customises <html>

// SSR: runs on every request
export async function getServerSideProps(context) {
  const data = await fetchData(context.params.slug);
  return { props: { data } };
}
// SSG: runs at build time (+ ISR with revalidate)
export async function getStaticProps() {
  return { props: { posts: await getPosts() }, revalidate: 60 };
}
export async function getStaticPaths() {
  return { paths: [{ params: { slug: "hello" } }], fallback: "blocking" };
}
export default function Page({ data }) { return <div>{data.title}</div>; }

import { useRouter } from "next/router";   // Pages Router hook (App Router uses next/navigation)
      Pages RouterApp Router equivalent
      getServerSidePropsasync server component reading request data (dynamic)
      getStaticProps + revalidate"use cache" + cacheLife (or fetch revalidate)
      getStaticPathsgenerateStaticParams
      _app.tsx / _document.tsxapp/layout.tsx
      pages/api/*.tsapp/**/route.ts
      next/headmetadata / generateMetadata
      next/routernext/navigation

      Both routers can live in the same project, which is how teams migrate route by route.

      Part

      Testing

      How professional teams know their UI still works: unit tests, component tests, mocked APIs, end-to-end tests in real browsers, accessibility and visual checks, all running in CI.

      Why test, and what kinds of tests exist

      Tests are code that checks your code. They let you refactor and ship without fear, catch regressions before users do, and document how things should behave. "I write tests" is a strong hiring signal for juniors because few do.
      TypeTestsSpeedTools
      StaticTypos, types, lint rulesInstantTypeScript, ESLint
      UnitOne function/hook in isolationMillisecondsVitest, Jest
      Component / integrationA component (or a few together) rendered in a fake DOM, used like a user wouldFastTesting Library, Vue Test Utils, MSW
      End-to-end (E2E)The real app in a real browser: login, checkoutSeconds eachPlaywright, Cypress
      Visual regressionScreenshots compared to a baselineMediumPlaywright screenshots, Chromatic, Percy
      AccessibilityAutomated a11y rule checksFastaxe-core, Lighthouse
