      PerformanceBudgets, Web VitalsMediumLighthouse CI

      The testing trophy (how much of each)

      For frontend, the common advice (Kent C. Dodds' "testing trophy") is: a base of static checks, some unit tests for pure logic, the most effort on integration/component tests, and a few E2E tests for critical user journeys. The classic "test pyramid" puts unit tests at the base; either way, the principle is the same:

      "The more your tests resemble the way your software is used, the more confidence they can give you." Test behaviour (what the user sees and does), not implementation details (state variable names, internal functions).

      Anatomy of a test: Arrange, Act, Assert

      js
      test("adds an item to the cart", () => {
  const cart = createCart();              // Arrange
  cart.add({ id: 1, price: 100 });        // Act
  expect(cart.total()).toBe(100);         // Assert
});

      Unit tests with Vitest

      Vitest is the standard test runner for Vite, React, Vue and many Next.js projects. Its API is Jest-compatible, so the syntax transfers.

      bash
      npm install -D vitest
# package.json: "test": "vitest"      → watch mode;  "vitest run" → once (CI);  "vitest --coverage"
      js
      // utils/price.js
export function formatPrice(paise) {
  if (!Number.isFinite(paise)) throw new Error("Invalid price");
  return "₹" + (paise / 100).toLocaleString("en-IN", { minimumFractionDigits: 2 });
}

// utils/price.test.js
import { describe, it, expect } from "vitest";
import { formatPrice } from "./price";

describe("formatPrice", () => {
  it("formats paise as rupees", () => {
    expect(formatPrice(199900)).toBe("₹1,999.00");
  });
  it("handles zero", () => {
    expect(formatPrice(0)).toBe("₹0.00");
  });
  it("throws on invalid input", () => {
    expect(() => formatPrice(NaN)).toThrow("Invalid price");
  });
  it.each([[100, "₹1.00"], [5050, "₹50.50"]])("formatPrice(%i) → %s", (input, out) => {
    expect(formatPrice(input)).toBe(out);
  });
});

      Matchers you'll use most

      js
      expect(x).toBe(3);                    // ===  (primitives)
expect(obj).toEqual({ a: 1 });        // deep equality (objects/arrays)
expect(obj).toStrictEqual(other);     // also checks undefined props & types
expect(arr).toContain("js");          expect(arr).toHaveLength(3);
expect(str).toMatch(/hello/i);
expect(val).toBeTruthy(); toBeFalsy(); toBeNull(); toBeUndefined(); toBeDefined();
expect(n).toBeGreaterThan(2); toBeCloseTo(0.3);
expect(obj).toHaveProperty("user.name", "Harsh");
expect(fn).toThrow(); expect(x).not.toBe(4);
await expect(promise).resolves.toEqual(data);
await expect(promise).rejects.toThrow("404");
expect(tree).toMatchSnapshot();       // snapshot: use sparingly

      Mocks, spies and fake timers

      js
      import { vi, beforeEach, afterEach } from "vitest";

const onSave = vi.fn();                          // a fake function that records calls
onSave("draft");
expect(onSave).toHaveBeenCalledWith("draft");
expect(onSave).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1);

const fetchUser = vi.fn().mockResolvedValue({ name: "Harsh" });   // fake async result
vi.spyOn(console, "error").mockImplementation(() => {});          // watch/replace a real method
vi.mock("./api", () => ({ getUser: vi.fn(() => Promise.resolve({ id: 1 })) }));  // mock a module

beforeEach(() => vi.useFakeTimers());
afterEach(() => { vi.useRealTimers(); vi.restoreAllMocks(); });
it("debounces", () => {
  const fn = vi.fn();
  const d = debounce(fn, 300);
  d(); d(); d();
  vi.advanceTimersByTime(300);                   // jump forward in time
  expect(fn).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(1);
});

      Setup/teardown hooks: beforeAll, beforeEach, afterEach, afterAll. Focus/skip: it.only, it.skip, it.todo.

      Component tests (Testing Library, Vue Test Utils)

      bash
      npm install -D @testing-library/react @testing-library/user-event @testing-library/jest-dom jsdom
# vitest.config: test: { environment: "jsdom", setupFiles: "./vitest.setup.js" }
# vitest.setup.js: import "@testing-library/jest-dom/vitest";   // adds toBeInTheDocument etc.
      jsx
      import { render, screen } from "@testing-library/react";
import userEvent from "@testing-library/user-event";
import LoginForm from "./LoginForm";

test("shows an error when the email is invalid", async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  const onSubmit = vi.fn();
  render(<LoginForm onSubmit={onSubmit} />);

  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText(/email/i), "not-an-email");
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText(/password/i), "secret123");
  await user.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: /log in/i }));

  expect(await screen.findByText(/enter a valid email/i)).toBeInTheDocument();
  expect(onSubmit).not.toHaveBeenCalled();
});

test("submits valid credentials", async () => {
  const user = userEvent.setup();
  const onSubmit = vi.fn();
  render(<LoginForm onSubmit={onSubmit} />);
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText(/email/i), "a@b.com");
  await user.type(screen.getByLabelText(/password/i), "secret123");
  await user.click(screen.getByRole("button", { name: /log in/i }));
  expect(onSubmit).toHaveBeenCalledWith({ email: "a@b.com", password: "secret123" });
});

      Query priority (use the first that works)

      1. getByRole (with { name }) — how assistive tech sees the page. If you can't find it by role, the UI may be inaccessible.
      2. getByLabelText, getByPlaceholderText, getByText, getByDisplayValue
      3. getByAltText, getByTitle
      4. getByTestId (data-testid) — last resort
      PrefixNot foundAsync?Use for
      getBy…ThrowsNoElements that should be there now
      queryBy…Returns nullNoAsserting something is absent
      findBy…Rejects after timeoutYes (await)Elements that appear later (after fetch)
      …AllBy…Multiple matches (arrays)

      jest-dom matchers: toBeInTheDocument, toBeVisible, toBeDisabled, toHaveValue, toHaveTextContent, toHaveAttribute, toHaveClass, toHaveFocus. Use waitFor(() => expect(…)) for other async assertions. Test hooks with renderHook. Wrap components needing providers (router, query client, store) in a custom renderWithProviders helper.

      Vue Test Utils (or @testing-library/vue)

      ts
      import { mount } from "@vue/test-utils";
import Counter from "./Counter.vue";
test("increments", async () => {
  const wrapper = mount(Counter, { props: { start: 1 } });
  await wrapper.get("button").trigger("click");
  expect(wrapper.text()).toContain("Clicked 2 times");
  expect(wrapper.emitted("change")?.[0]).toEqual([2]);
});

      Next.js async Server Components aren't well supported by unit-test renderers yet. Unit-test their data functions, test client components normally, and cover server-rendered pages with E2E tests.

      Mocking APIs with MSW

      Mock Service Worker intercepts network requests at the network level, so your components use their real fetch code while receiving fake responses. The same handlers work in tests, in the browser during development, and in Storybook.

      js
      // mocks/handlers.js
import { http, HttpResponse, delay } from "msw";
export const handlers = [
  http.get("/api/products", async ({ request }) => {
    const q = new URL(request.url).searchParams.get("q");
    await delay(100);
    return HttpResponse.json([{ id: 1, name: `Result for ${q}` }]);
  }),
  http.post("/api/login", async ({ request }) => {
    const { email } = await request.json();
    return email === "a@b.com"
      ? HttpResponse.json({ token: "abc" })
      : HttpResponse.json({ message: "Invalid credentials" }, { status: 401 });
  }),
];

// vitest.setup.js
import { setupServer } from "msw/node";
export const server = setupServer(...handlers);
beforeAll(() => server.listen({ onUnhandledRequest: "error" }));
afterEach(() => server.resetHandlers());
afterAll(() => server.close());

// in one test: force an error state
server.use(http.get("/api/products", () => HttpResponse.json(null, { status: 500 })));
expect(await screen.findByText(/couldn't load products/i)).toBeInTheDocument();

      Always test the loading, empty, error and success states of a data-driven component.

      End-to-end tests with Playwright

      Playwright drives real Chromium, Firefox and WebKit browsers. It auto-waits for elements, records traces, and runs tests in parallel. (Cypress is the main alternative.)

      bash
      npm init playwright@latest
npx playwright test               # run all
npx playwright test --ui          # interactive UI mode
npx playwright codegen localhost:3000   # record actions into test code
npx playwright show-report
      ts
      // e2e/checkout.spec.ts
import { test, expect } from "@playwright/test";

test.describe("checkout", () => {
  test.beforeEach(async ({ page }) => {
    await page.goto("/");
  });

  test("user can add a product and check out", async ({ page }) => {
    await page.getByRole("link", { name: "Keyboards" }).click();
    await page.getByRole("button", { name: "Add to cart" }).first().click();
    await expect(page.getByTestId("cart-count")).toHaveText("1");

    await page.getByRole("link", { name: "Cart" }).click();
    await page.getByLabel("Email").fill("a@b.com");
    await page.getByRole("button", { name: "Place order" }).click();

    await expect(page).toHaveURL(/\/order\/\w+/);
    await expect(page.getByRole("heading", { name: "Thank you" })).toBeVisible();
  });

  test("shows an error when payment fails", async ({ page }) => {
    await page.route("**/api/pay", route => route.fulfill({ status: 402, json: { message: "Card declined" } }));
    // …
  });
});
      • Locators: getByRole, getByLabel, getByText, getByPlaceholder, getByTestId, chain with .filter({ hasText }).
      • Web-first assertions retry until timeout: toBeVisible, toHaveText, toHaveURL, toHaveCount, toHaveScreenshot (visual regression).
      • Auth once: log in in a setup project, save storageState, reuse it in all tests.
      • Page Object Model: wrap pages in classes (new LoginPage(page).login(…)) to keep tests readable.
      • Test only critical journeys E2E (signup, login, search, checkout); they're slower and more brittle.
      • Never use fixed waits (waitForTimeout(3000)); rely on auto-waiting assertions.

      Accessibility, visual tests, Storybook, coverage and TDD

      Automated accessibility checks

      ts
      // Playwright + axe
import AxeBuilder from "@axe-core/playwright";
test("home page has no detectable a11y violations", async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto("/");
  const results = await new AxeBuilder({ page }).analyze();
  expect(results.violations).toEqual([]);
});

      Automated tools catch roughly a third to half of issues. Still test manually with the keyboard and a screen reader (NVDA, VoiceOver).

      Storybook

      A workshop for building components in isolation: each "story" renders a component in a given state (loading, error, long text, dark mode). Doubles as living documentation and can run interaction, accessibility and visual tests (Chromatic) on every story.

      jsx
      // Button.stories.jsx
export default { component: Button };
export const Primary = { args: { variant: "primary", children: "Save" } };
export const Disabled = { args: { disabled: true, children: "Save" } };

      Coverage

      vitest --coverage reports which lines/branches ran during tests. Use it to find untested paths, not as a target to game; 100% coverage with weak assertions proves little. Many teams aim for ~70–80% on business logic.

      TDD (test-driven development)

      Red → Green → Refactor: write a failing test for the next small behaviour, write the simplest code to pass, clean up, repeat. Especially useful for utilities, reducers and bug fixes (write a test that reproduces the bug first).

      What to test — a practical checklist

      • Pure logic: formatters, validators, reducers, price/discount calculations, date handling.
      • Custom hooks/composables with tricky state.
      • Components: renders the right content for props; user interactions; form validation; loading/empty/error states; conditional UI (logged in vs out).
      • Critical flows E2E: auth, payment, core feature.
      • Every bug fix gets a regression test.
      • Don't test: third-party libraries, framework internals, CSS details, private implementation.

      Testing interview questions

      Unit vs integration vs E2E?

      Unit tests one piece in isolation (fast, precise). Integration tests pieces working together (e.g. component + hook + mocked API). E2E tests the real app in a browser from the user's view (slow, highest confidence).

      Why prefer getByRole over getByTestId?

      It queries the way users and assistive technology perceive the UI, so tests survive refactors and double as accessibility checks. Test ids are invisible to users.

      What's the difference between a mock, a stub and a spy?

      A stub returns canned data; a spy records calls to a real or fake function; a mock is a fake with expectations about how it's called. In Vitest, vi.fn() and vi.spyOn() cover all three.

      How do you test code that uses setTimeout?

      Use fake timers (vi.useFakeTimers()) and advance time with vi.advanceTimersByTime(ms).

      How do you deal with flaky tests?

      Remove fixed sleeps, use auto-waiting/findBy queries, isolate test data, mock unstable external services, avoid shared state between tests, and use traces to find the race.

      Part

      Tooling & workflow

      How code gets from your editor to production in a team: Git and pull requests, package managers, bundlers, linting, CI/CD and DevTools.

      Git essentials

      bash
      git init                         # new repo          | git clone <url>   copy a repo
git status                       # what changed
git add file.js / git add .      # stage changes
git commit -m "feat: add cart badge"
git log --oneline --graph        # history
git diff                         # unstaged changes  | git diff --staged

git branch feature/cart          # create branch
git switch feature/cart          # move to it  (git switch -c name = create + switch)
git merge main                   # bring main's changes into current branch
git rebase main                  # replay your commits on top of main (linear history)

git remote -v                    # remotes
git push -u origin feature/cart  # publish branch
git pull                         # fetch + merge (git pull --rebase to rebase)
git fetch                        # download without merging

git stash / git stash pop        # shelve work in progress
git restore file.js              # discard changes to a file
git reset --soft HEAD~1          # undo last commit, keep changes staged
git revert <hash>                # new commit that undoes a commit (safe on shared branches)
git commit --amend               # fix the last commit (only if not pushed)
git cherry-pick <hash>           # copy one commit onto current branch
      mergerebase
      Keeps history as it happened, adds a merge commitRewrites your commits on top of the target: clean linear history
      Safe for shared branchesNever rebase commits others have pulled

      Merge conflicts

      Git marks the conflict in the file between <<<<<<<, ======= and >>>>>>>. Edit to the correct final version, remove the markers, git add, then git commit (or git rebase --continue).

      Good habits

      • Small, focused commits with clear messages. Conventional Commits: feat:, fix:, refactor:, docs:, test:, chore:.
      • .gitignore: node_modules/, dist/, .next/, .env*, coverage.
      • Never commit secrets. If you do, rotate the key immediately; deleting the commit isn't enough.

      Team workflow: branches, pull requests, code review

      1. Pick a ticket (Jira/Linear/GitHub Issues). Create a branch: feat/123-cart-badge.
      2. Commit in small steps; push; open a Pull Request (PR) early as a draft if you want feedback.
      3. PR description: what and why, screenshots/video for UI, how to test, linked issue.
      4. CI runs lint, type-check, tests, build; a preview deployment appears (Vercel/Netlify).
      5. Reviewers comment; you respond and push fixes. Keep PRs small (< ~400 lines) so reviews are fast.
      6. Squash-merge into main; delete the branch; deploy.

      Reviewing others' code

      Check correctness, edge cases, naming, accessibility, tests, and whether it matches the design. Be specific and kind: suggest, explain why, separate blocking issues from nitpicks.

      Branching models

      Trunk-based (short-lived branches off main, feature flags) is most common now. GitFlow (develop/release/hotfix branches) appears in older or release-heavy teams.

      Agile basics you'll hear daily

      Sprints (1–2 weeks), stand-up, backlog, story points, sprint review, retro, "definition of done" (code reviewed, tested, deployed, documented).

      Package managers and semantic versioning

      npmpnpmyarnbun
      Default with NodeFast, saves disk via a shared store, strictClassic alternativeVery fast runtime + package manager
      bash
      npm install            # install from package-lock.json   (CI: npm ci — exact, clean)
npm i react            # dependency      npm i -D vitest   # devDependency
npm run build          # run a script from package.json
npx some-cli           # run a package binary without installing globally
npm outdated / npm update / npm audit

      Semver: MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH

      19.2.1 → MAJOR (breaking changes), MINOR (new features, compatible), PATCH (bug fixes). In package.json: ^19.2.1 allows minor+patch updates, ~19.2.1 patch only, 19.2.1 exact. The lock file pins exact versions so every machine installs the same tree: always commit it.

      Build tools: what happens on npm run build

      • Transpiling — TypeScript/JSX/modern syntax → JavaScript browsers understand (esbuild, SWC, Babel).
      • Bundling — follow imports and combine modules into a few files (Vite uses Rollup/Rolldown; Next uses Turbopack; older apps use webpack).
      • Tree shaking — remove exports you never import (works with ES modules).
      • Code splitting — separate chunks per route / dynamic import(), loaded on demand.
      • Minification — shorten names, strip whitespace/comments.
      • Asset handling — hash file names for caching, optimise images, process CSS (PostCSS, Tailwind, autoprefixer).
      • Source maps — map minified code back to your source for debugging.
      • Environment variables — replaced at build time (import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL in Vite, NEXT_PUBLIC_* in Next).

      Vite in dev serves native ES modules with instant hot module replacement (HMR); for production it bundles. Polyfills add missing features for older browsers; browserslist decides which browsers to target.

      Code quality: ESLint, Prettier, hooks

      • ESLint finds bugs and bad patterns (unused vars, missing hook deps, a11y issues with eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y). Config lives in eslint.config.js (flat config).
      • Prettier formats code consistently; stop arguing about semicolons. Enable format-on-save.
      • Biome is a fast all-in-one alternative to both.
      • Husky + lint-staged run lint/format on staged files before each commit.
      • TypeScript tsc --noEmit and tests in CI as the final gate.
      json
      {
  "scripts": {
    "dev": "vite", "build": "vite build", "preview": "vite preview",
    "lint": "eslint .", "format": "prettier --write .", "typecheck": "tsc --noEmit",
    "test": "vitest", "test:e2e": "playwright test"
  }
}

      CI/CD and deployment

      CI (continuous integration) runs checks on every push/PR. CD (continuous delivery/deployment) ships passing builds automatically.

      yaml
      # .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI
on: [push, pull_request]
jobs:
  check:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with: { node-version: 22, cache: npm }
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm run lint
      - run: npm run typecheck
      - run: npm test -- --run
      - run: npm run build
      - run: npx playwright install --with-deps && npm run test:e2e
      HostingBest for
      VercelNext.js, previews per PR
      Netlify, Cloudflare PagesStatic sites, SPAs, many frameworks
      GitHub PagesFree static portfolios
      AWS (S3 + CloudFront, Amplify), Render, Railway, Fly.ioMore control, full-stack apps, Docker

      Production concerns: environment-specific variables, error monitoring (Sentry), analytics, feature flags, rollbacks, and SPA fallback (serve index.html for unknown routes so client-side routing works on refresh).

      Debugging with DevTools

      PanelUse it to
      ElementsInspect/edit DOM and CSS live, see the box model, flex/grid overlays, force :hover/:focus states
      ConsoleLogs, errors, run JS; $0 = selected element; copy(obj)
      SourcesBreakpoints (line, conditional, DOM, XHR, event listener), step through code, watch values; debugger;
      NetworkRequests, status, headers, payload, timing, throttling, block requests, disable cache
      ApplicationlocalStorage, cookies, IndexedDB, service workers, manifest
      PerformanceRecord interactions: long tasks, layout thrashing, INP causes
      LighthouseAudit performance, accessibility, SEO, best practices
      Device toolbarEmulate phones, touch, DPR
      React / Vue DevToolsComponent tree, props/state, re-render highlighting, Profiler

      A debugging method that works

      1. Reproduce reliably. Write down the exact steps.
      2. Read the full error message and stack trace; click through to the source line.
      3. Form a hypothesis, then check it (breakpoint or log the actual values).
      4. Narrow down: comment out halves, git bisect to find the commit that broke it.
      5. Fix, add a test, and explain the root cause in the PR.

      Working with AI coding tools

      Teams expect you to use AI assistants (Copilot, Claude, Cursor) productively: generate boilerplate, explain unfamiliar code, draft tests. You're still responsible for every line: read it, run it, test it, and be able to explain it in review and in interviews.

      Part

      Performance, accessibility & security

      The non-functional qualities that separate a demo from a product. Interviewers love asking about all three.

      Web performance and Core Web Vitals

      Core Web Vitals (Google's user-centred metrics)

      MetricMeasuresGoodUsual fixes
      LCP Largest Contentful PaintLoading: when the main content (hero image/heading) appears≤ 2.5 sOptimise/preload hero image, fetchpriority="high", SSR/SSG, CDN, fewer render-blocking resources
      INP Interaction to Next PaintResponsiveness: delay from click/tap/key to the next frame≤ 200 msBreak up long tasks, less JS, debounce, transitions, web workers, avoid huge re-renders
      CLS Cumulative Layout ShiftVisual stability: content jumping around≤ 0.1Width/height or aspect-ratio on media, reserve space for ads/embeds, font-display + size-adjust

      Other metrics: TTFB (server response time), FCP (first content), TBT (blocked main thread, lab proxy for INP). Measure with Lighthouse and PageSpeed Insights (lab), and real-user data from the Chrome UX Report or the web-vitals library (field).

      Optimisation checklist

      Load less

      Code-split routes and heavy widgets; tree-shake; check bundle size (vite-bundle-visualizer, @next/bundle-analyzer); replace heavy libraries (moment → date-fns/Intl); server components; remove unused CSS.

      Load smarter

      Modern image formats (AVIF/WebP), responsive srcset, lazy-load below the fold; preload critical fonts/images; preconnect to API/CDN; HTTP caching + CDN; compression (Brotli).

      Render faster

      SSR/SSG/streaming; inline critical CSS; defer scripts; avoid layout thrashing; animate transform/opacity; content-visibility: auto for long pages.

      Stay responsive

      Virtualise long lists; memoise expensive work; debounce/throttle; useTransition; move heavy computation to Web Workers; limit third-party scripts (load them after interaction).

      "This page is slow. What do you do?" Measure first (Lighthouse, Performance panel, Network waterfall), identify which metric is bad and why (big image? blocking JS? long task?), fix the biggest bottleneck, measure again. Mention real-user monitoring and performance budgets in CI.

      Accessibility (a11y)

      Accessibility means everyone can use your UI: keyboard-only users, screen reader users, people with low vision, colour blindness, tremors or cognitive disabilities, and anyone on a bright screen with one hand. It's also a legal requirement in many countries (WCAG 2.2 AA is the usual target).

      WCAG principles: POUR

      • Perceivable — text alternatives, captions, sufficient contrast (4.5:1 for normal text, 3:1 for large text and UI parts), don't rely on colour alone.
      • Operable — everything works with a keyboard, visible focus, no keyboard traps, enough time, no flashing, targets big enough (24×24 px minimum).
      • Understandable — clear labels and errors, predictable navigation, lang attribute.
      • Robust — valid semantic HTML that assistive tech can interpret.

      ARIA: the five rules in short

      1. If a native element exists (button, a, input, dialog), use it instead of ARIA.
      2. Don't change native semantics (<h2 role="tab"> ✗).
      3. All interactive ARIA controls must be keyboard-operable.
      4. Don't hide focusable elements (aria-hidden="true" on a focusable button ✗).
      5. Every interactive element needs an accessible name.

      "No ARIA is better than bad ARIA."

      html
      <button aria-label="Close dialog"><svg aria-hidden="true">…</svg></button>   <!-- icon-only button -->
<button aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="menu">Menu</button>              <!-- disclosure state -->
<input id="email" aria-describedby="email-err" aria-invalid="true">
<p id="email-err">Enter a valid email</p>                                       <!-- error linked to input -->
<div role="status" aria-live="polite">3 results found</div>                    <!-- announce updates -->
<a href="#main" class="skip-link">Skip to content</a>                           <!-- first focusable element -->
<span class="visually-hidden">(opens in new tab)</span>                         <!-- screen-reader-only text -->

      Keyboard and focus management

      • Tab/Shift+Tab move focus in DOM order; Enter/Space activate; Escape closes; arrows move within widgets (tabs, menus, radios).
      • Never remove focus outlines without a replacement; style :focus-visible.
      • tabindex="0" makes custom elements focusable; tabindex="-1" allows programmatic focus; avoid positive tabindex.
      • Modals: move focus into the dialog, trap it inside, close on Escape, return focus to the trigger. Native <dialog> with showModal() handles most of this.
      • SPA route changes: move focus to the new page's heading or announce the change.

      Testing accessibility

      Unplug the mouse and use the site; run axe DevTools / Lighthouse; zoom to 200%; check contrast; try a screen reader (VoiceOver on Mac/iOS, NVDA on Windows, TalkBack on Android); use getByRole in tests.

      Frontend security

      ThreatWhat it isDefence
      XSS (cross-site scripting)Attacker's script runs in your page (steals cookies/tokens, acts as the user). Via innerHTML, dangerouslySetInnerHTML, v-html, javascript: URLs, unescaped server templatesLet frameworks escape output; sanitise any HTML with DOMPurify; validate URLs; CSP; HttpOnly cookies
      CSRF (cross-site request forgery)Another site makes the user's browser send an authenticated request (cookies go automatically)SameSite=Lax/Strict cookies, CSRF tokens, check Origin; never change data on GET
      ClickjackingYour site loaded invisibly in an iframe to trick clicksContent-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'none' / X-Frame-Options: DENY
      Token theftTokens in localStorage readable by any XSSHttpOnly Secure cookies; short-lived tokens
      Exposed secretsAPI keys in frontend bundles or GitEverything in the browser is public; keep secrets on the server (server actions, API routes, proxies)
      Supply chainMalicious or vulnerable npm packagesnpm audit, Dependabot/Renovate, lock files, fewer dependencies, review new packages
      Open redirects?redirect=https://evil.com after loginOnly allow relative/whitelisted redirects
      Broken access controlHiding a button isn't authorisationCheck permissions on the server for every request

      Content Security Policy

      text
      Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' 'nonce-r4nd0m'; img-src 'self' https://cdn.shop.com data:;
                         connect-src 'self' https://api.shop.com; frame-ancestors 'none'; object-src 'none'

      A CSP tells the browser which sources may load scripts, styles, images and connections, so injected scripts don't run. Other helpful headers: Strict-Transport-Security, X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff, Referrer-Policy, Permissions-Policy. Links opening new tabs: rel="noopener noreferrer" (modern browsers default to noopener).

      Frontend validation is UX, not security. Anyone can edit your JS or call your API directly. The server must validate and authorise everything.

      SEO, internationalisation, PWAs and browser support

      SEO for developers

      • Server-render or pre-render important pages (SPAs with empty HTML index worse and slower).
      • Unique <title> and meta description per page; one <h1>; semantic headings.
      • Clean URLs, <link rel="canonical">, sitemap.xml, robots.txt.
      • Open Graph / Twitter card tags for link previews.
      • Structured data (JSON-LD) for products, articles, FAQs → rich results.
      • Real <a href> links (crawlers follow them), descriptive alt text, fast Core Web Vitals, mobile-friendly.

      Internationalisation (i18n)

      • Never concatenate translated strings; use message keys with placeholders and plural rules (i18next, react-intl, next-intl, vue-i18n).
      • Format with Intl: new Intl.NumberFormat("en-IN", { style: "currency", currency: "INR" }), Intl.DateTimeFormat, Intl.RelativeTimeFormat.
      • Support RTL with logical CSS properties and dir="rtl"; leave room for longer text.

      Progressive Web Apps

      A web app manifest (name, icons, colours) + a service worker (offline caching, background sync) + HTTPS = installable app with offline support and push notifications. Tools: Workbox, vite-plugin-pwa.

      Cross-browser support

      Check support on caniuse.com / MDN Baseline; test on Chrome, Safari (especially iOS Safari) and Firefox; use feature detection (@supports, "IntersectionObserver" in window) and progressive enhancement rather than browser sniffing.

      Part

      UX for engineers

      At 3 YOE you're expected to spot usability problems before users do, fill the gaps designs leave (loading, empty, error states), write clear interface copy, and discuss trade-offs with designers. This part covers the UX a frontend engineer actually uses.

      UX principles every frontend engineer should know

      UX (user experience) is whether people can achieve their goal easily, confidently and without frustration. Engineers shape it constantly: speed, feedback, states, error handling, keyboard support, copy. Many UX problems are engineering problems.

      Nielsen's 10 usability heuristics (the checklist behind most UX reviews)

      HeuristicFrontend example
      1. Visibility of system statusShow saving/saved, upload progress, loading states, "3 new messages"
      2. Match the real worldUse users' words ("Delivery address", not "Shipping entity"); familiar icons
      3. User control and freedomUndo, cancel, back works, close modals with Esc, stop a running AI response
      4. Consistency and standardsSame button styles and wording everywhere; follow platform conventions
      5. Error preventionDisable invalid actions, sensible defaults, confirm destructive actions, input masks
      6. Recognition over recallShow recent searches, keep filters visible, autocomplete
      7. Flexibility and efficiencyKeyboard shortcuts, bulk actions, saved views for power users
      8. Aesthetic and minimalist designRemove what doesn't help the task; progressive disclosure
      9. Help users recover from errorsPlain-language errors next to the field, with how to fix it
      10. Help and documentationInline hints, tooltips, empty-state guidance

      Laws of UX worth remembering

      • Fitts's law: bigger and closer targets are faster to hit. Primary buttons large; touch targets at least 44×44 px (WCAG minimum 24×24); destructive actions away from common ones.
      • Hick's law: more choices → slower decisions. Limit options, highlight a recommended one, group the rest.
      • Jakob's law: users spend most time on other sites and expect yours to work the same way. Don't reinvent checkboxes, carts or navigation.
      • Miller's law / cognitive load: people hold only a few items in working memory. Chunk long forms, phone numbers, steps.
      • Doherty threshold: responses under ~400 ms keep people in flow; respond instantly (optimistic UI) and do slow work in the background.
      • Peak-end rule: people remember the most intense moment and the end. Make success moments (order placed) and error recovery feel good.
      • Progressive disclosure: show basics first, reveal advanced options on demand ("Advanced filters").

      Visual design basics for engineers

      You don't need to be a designer, but you should be able to make an undesigned screen look intentional and to notice when something is off.

      Hierarchy

      Make the most important thing the most prominent (size, weight, colour, position). One primary action per screen; secondary actions look secondary (outline/ghost buttons); tertiary actions as links. Squint at the screen: the right things should still stand out.

      Spacing

      • Use a spacing scale (4 or 8 px based: 4, 8, 12, 16, 24, 32, 48, 64) instead of arbitrary values.
      • Proximity (Gestalt): related items closer together, groups further apart. A label must be closer to its input than to the previous field.
      • More whitespace usually looks more polished than borders and boxes.

      Typography

      • A type scale (e.g. 12, 14, 16, 20, 24, 32, 48) with two or three weights; body text 16 px+ on the web.
      • Line length 45–75 characters (max-width: 65ch), line height ~1.5 for body, tighter for headings.
      • Left-align body text; avoid long centred paragraphs and all-caps text blocks.

      Colour

      • Neutral greys for most UI, one brand/accent colour for primary actions and focus, semantic colours for success/warning/error/info.
      • Contrast: 4.5:1 for normal text, 3:1 for large text, icons and input borders. Check with DevTools' contrast picker.
      • Never rely on colour alone (add icons/text for errors and status; colour-blind users exist in every user base).
      • Dark mode isn't inverted colours: use darker surfaces (not pure black), lighter surfaces for elevation, desaturated accents.

      Alignment and consistency

      Align to a grid; consistent corner radii, shadows, icon sizes and button heights; reuse design-system components instead of one-offs. Inconsistent details are what make a UI feel "cheap" even when each piece is fine.

      Designing every state: loading, empty, error, success

      Designs usually show the happy path with perfect data. Engineers ship the other 80%. For every data-driven view, handle:

      StateGood practice
      LoadingUnder ~300 ms show nothing (avoid flicker); up to a few seconds use skeletons that match the layout; longer tasks show progress and let users continue elsewhere
      Empty (first use)Explain what will appear here and give the action to start: "No invoices yet. Create your first invoice."
      Empty (no results)Say what was searched, suggest fixes: "No results for 'kyboard'. Check the spelling or clear filters."
      ErrorWhat happened, in plain words, plus what to do (retry, contact support); keep the user's input
      PartialSome widgets failed: show the rest, isolate the failure
      Offline / slow networkTell users, queue actions, retry automatically
      SuccessConfirm clearly and say what's next ("Order placed. Arriving Thursday.")
      Edge contentVery long names, 0/1/1000 items, missing images, RTL, huge numbers, slow-loading avatars
      PermissionsExplain why something is disabled or hidden, and how to get access

      Perceived performance

      • Response-time thresholds: ~0.1 s feels instant, ~1 s keeps flow, ~10 s is the limit of attention (show progress beyond that).
      • Optimistic UI for likely-to-succeed actions (likes, toggles, reordering), with rollback and a message on failure.
      • Prefetch on hover/visibility, keep previous data visible while refetching, animate transitions so changes are understandable.
      • Avoid layout shift: reserve space for images, ads and late-loading content.

      Destructive actions: confirm or undo?

      Prefer undo (delete immediately, show "Deleted. Undo" for a few seconds) for frequent, reversible actions; it's faster and less annoying. Use a confirmation dialog for rare, irreversible, high-impact actions, with a specific button label ("Delete project") and, for the most dangerous, typing the name to confirm.

      Form UX

      • Ask for less. Every field costs completions. Remove optional fields or mark optional ones "(optional)" rather than marking required ones with asterisks everywhere.
      • Visible labels above inputs. Placeholders disappear while typing and have low contrast; use them for examples only.
      • One column for most forms; group related fields with headings/fieldsets; logical order.
      • Right input types and attributes: type="email", inputmode="numeric", autocomplete="one-time-code", autocomplete="street-address" — mobile keyboards and autofill do the work.
      • Validation timing: validate a field when the user leaves it (blur), not on every keystroke while they're still typing; re-validate live once a field has shown an error so users see it resolve; validate everything on submit and move focus to the first error (plus an error summary for long forms).
      • Error messages next to the field, specific and helpful: "Enter a 10-digit mobile number" not "Invalid input".
      • Don't clear the form on error. Preserve what users typed, including after a failed submission or refresh (drafts).
      • Buttons: label with the outcome ("Create account", "Pay ₹1,999"), disable only while submitting (show a spinner inside), prevent double submission.
      • Formatting: accept input flexibly (spaces in card numbers, +91 prefixes) and normalise it yourself.
      • Multi-step forms: show progress ("Step 2 of 4"), allow going back without losing data, summary before submit.
      • Passwords: show/hide toggle, show rules up front, allow paste and password managers; consider passkeys/OTP.

      UX writing: the words in the interface

      Interface copy is part of the UI. Engineers often write it (error messages, empty states, tooltips), so write it well.

      • Be specific and use the user's words. "Save changes" beats "Submit"; "Remove from cart" beats "Delete item entity".
      • Buttons say what happens, ideally verb + noun: "Send invoice", "Download report". Dialog buttons repeat the action ("Delete project" / "Cancel"), never "Yes / No" or "OK".
      • Error messages: what happened + why (if useful) + how to fix. No blame, no jargon, no codes alone. "Your card was declined. Try another card or contact your bank." not "Error 402: PAYMENT_FAILED".
      • Empty states: what this area is for + how to start.
      • Confirmations: say exactly what will happen, especially if irreversible: "Delete 'Q3 report'? This can't be undone."
      • Consistency: pick one term per concept (don't mix "workspace", "project" and "team" for the same thing) and one style for capitalisation (sentence case is easiest to read).
      • Front-load the important words; keep it short; remove filler like "Please note that", "Simply", "Oops!".
      • Tone: friendly and calm; no jokes in error or payment flows.
      • Accessible text: link text that makes sense alone ("Read the refund policy", not "click here"); alt text that conveys purpose.
      • i18n-ready: no string concatenation, room for longer translations, plural rules.
      Instead ofWrite
      Something went wrongWe couldn't load your orders. Check your connection and try again.
      Invalid dateEnter a date in the future, like 25/12/2026
      No dataNo payments yet. Payments you receive will appear here.
      Are you sure?Remove Priya from this team? She'll lose access to its projects.
      SubmitRequest refund

      Interaction, motion, navigation and mobile UX

      Feedback and affordance

      • Everything interactive must look interactive (buttons look like buttons, links look like links) and respond on hover, press and focus.
      • Every action gets feedback within ~100 ms: pressed state, spinner, toast, inline change.
      • Don't hide essential actions behind hover (touch devices have no hover).

      Motion

      • Use motion to explain change (where did this panel come from, what got added), not for decoration.
      • Durations: ~100–200 ms for small UI feedback, ~200–300 ms for panels and modals; exits faster than entrances. Ease-out for entering, ease-in for leaving.
      • Animate transform/opacity; respect prefers-reduced-motion; never block input while animating.

      Navigation and information architecture

      • Users should always know where they are (active nav item, page title, breadcrumbs for deep hierarchies) and how to go back.
      • Keep URLs meaningful and shareable; back/forward must work (store filters and tabs in the URL).
      • Search for large content sets; filters with visible active state and "Clear all".
      • Modals only for focused, short tasks; don't stack them; long tasks deserve a page.

      Mobile and responsive UX

      • Thumb zone: primary actions reachable at the bottom on mobile (sticky bottom bars).
      • Touch targets ≥ 44 px with spacing; no tiny close icons.
      • Content first: collapse secondary navigation, not the core content; avoid horizontal scrolling except for intentional carousels.
      • Respect safe areas (env(safe-area-inset-bottom)), on-screen keyboards covering inputs, and slow networks on low-end Android devices.

      Notifications and interruptions

      Toasts for non-critical confirmations (auto-dismiss, but not for errors that need action); inline messages for errors tied to content; banners for system-wide status; modals only when the user must decide now. Don't ask for notification permission on first load; ask in context when the value is clear.

      UX process and working with designers

      How UX work happens (and where engineers fit)

      1. Research: user interviews, analytics, support tickets. Engineers can pull data (funnels, error rates, slow pages).
      2. Define: problem statement, user journeys, success metrics.
      3. Design: flows, wireframes, prototypes in Figma. Engineers review feasibility and missing states early.
      4. Test: usability tests with ~5 users find most major problems; prototypes or staging builds.
      5. Build and measure: ship behind a flag, A/B test when uncertain, watch funnels, drop-off points and Web Vitals.

      Metrics you'll hear

      Task success rate, time on task, conversion and funnel drop-off, error rate, retention, NPS/CSAT, and the UX side of performance (Core Web Vitals, rage clicks, dead clicks from session-replay tools like Microsoft Clarity or PostHog).

      Collaborating well

      • Join design reviews early; ask "what happens when…?" for loading, empty, error, long content and mobile.
      • Propose alternatives with cost: "This custom dropdown is 3 days; the design-system Select is 2 hours and accessible. Can we use it?"
      • Build a quick prototype when a written debate stalls.
      • Do a design QA pass on your own PR against Figma before asking the designer.

      UX checklist for your pull requests

      • All states handled (loading, empty, error, success, long content)?
      • Keyboard-only usable, visible focus, labels, contrast?
      • Works at 320 px width and 200% zoom? Touch targets big enough?
      • Copy clear and consistent? Errors actionable?
      • No layout shift; feedback within 100 ms; double-submit prevented?
      • Back button, refresh and shared links behave as expected?

      UX questions in frontend interviews

      How do you handle a design that's missing loading, empty and error states?

      Raise it early with the designer, propose defaults from the design system (skeletons, empty-state pattern, inline errors), build them, and get sign-off. Mention perceived performance thresholds and keeping user input on errors.

      Skeleton screen or spinner?

      Skeletons for content with a known layout (lists, cards, profiles) loading for more than ~300 ms; they reduce perceived wait and layout shift. Spinners for small, unknown or action-level waits (inside a button). For long operations, show progress.

      When do you validate form fields?

      On blur for the first time, then live once an error is shown so users see it resolve, and all fields on submit with focus moved to the first error. Server errors mapped back to fields.

      Confirmation dialog or undo?

      Undo for frequent, reversible actions (faster, less friction); confirmation for rare, irreversible, high-impact ones, with a specific action label.

      How would you improve the UX of a slow page without making the backend faster?

      Stream or SSR the shell, skeletons, show cached/stale data while revalidating, prefetch likely next pages, optimistic updates, paginate/virtualise, lazy-load below the fold, defer non-critical widgets, and reserve space to avoid layout shift.

      A PM wants a feature the designer thinks hurts UX. What do you do?

      Clarify the goal behind both positions, bring data (analytics, usability findings), propose a variant or an A/B test with a clear success metric, and help the team decide; then commit.

      Machine coding follow-up: "What would you improve in your component's UX?"

      Keyboard support and focus, loading/empty/error states, debounce and cancellation, better copy, mobile touch targets, reduced motion, remembering state in the URL, and testing with a screen reader.

      Part

      Architecture & senior skills

      The judgement layer expected at 3+ years: choosing rendering and state strategies, design systems, monorepos and micro-frontends, production observability, deep performance debugging, AI features, and how mid-level engineers work.

      Frontend architecture decisions

      Choosing a rendering strategy

      ProductGood fitWhy
      Marketing site, docs, blogSSG (Astro, Nuxt/Next prerender)Fast, cheap, SEO; content changes rarely
      E-commerce listing/product pagesSSR/ISR + caching, client islands for cartSEO + fresh prices; fast LCP
      Logged-in dashboard / adminSPA (Vite + Vue/React) or SSR shellNo SEO need; highly interactive
      Social feed, newsSSR first page + client pagination, streamingFast first view, then app-like
      Real-time collaborationSPA + WebSocket/CRDT, local-firstLatency and offline matter most

      Types of state and where each lives

      StateExamplesHome
      Server stateProducts, orders, user profileQuery cache (TanStack Query / Pinia Colada / useFetch / server components)
      URL stateFilters, sort, page, tab, searchRoute query params (shareable, survives refresh)
      Global client stateSession, theme, cart draft, feature flagsPinia / Zustand / context
      Local UI stateIs modal open, hover, input textComponent (ref/useState)
      Form stateValues, touched, errorsForm library (VeeValidate, React Hook Form)
      Derived stateTotals, filtered listsComputed; never stored
      Persistent client stateDrafts, offline datalocalStorage / IndexedDB

      Most "state management problems" come from putting server state in a global store and syncing it by hand.

      API layer and BFF

      • A typed API client (generated from OpenAPI with openapi-typescript/Orval, or tRPC/GraphQL codegen) removes a whole class of bugs.
      • Normalise errors into one shape ({ code, message, fieldErrors }) and handle 401, 403, 429, network errors centrally.
      • A Backend-for-Frontend (Nuxt server routes, Next route handlers, a small Node service) aggregates several backend calls, hides secrets, shapes data for the UI and handles auth cookies.

      Error-handling strategy

      Decide per layer: validation errors shown inline; recoverable errors as toasts with retry; component-level failures inside error boundaries (onErrorCaptured/ErrorBoundary) so one widget doesn't blank the page; route-level error pages; global handlers that report to monitoring. Every error message tells the user what happened and what to do next.

      Writing down decisions

      Record significant choices as short ADRs (Architecture Decision Records): context, decision, alternatives considered, consequences. Being able to say "we chose X over Y because…" is exactly what interviewers probe at 3 YOE.

      Design systems and Figma handoff

      Layers of a design system

      1. Design tokens — named decisions: color.bg.surface, space.4, radius.md, font.size.lg, shadow.2, motion.fast. Exported from Figma Variables (e.g. via Tokens Studio / Style Dictionary) to CSS variables.
      2. Primitives — Box, Stack, Text, Icon, VisuallyHidden.
      3. Components — Button, Input, Select, Dialog, Tabs, Toast, Table, built on accessible headless behaviour (Reka UI, Radix, Headless UI, Ark UI).
      4. Patterns — forms, empty states, page layouts.
      5. Docs & tooling — Storybook, usage guidelines, visual regression tests, a changelog, semantic versioning.

      Designing component APIs

      • Small, predictable props: variant, size, disabled, loading; avoid boolean explosions (isPrimary, isDanger…).
      • Composition over configuration: slots/children and compound components instead of 30 props.
      • Forward attributes and refs; support controlled and uncontrolled usage (v-model / value+onChange).
      • Accessibility built in (roles, focus management, keyboard), themeable via tokens, documented with examples of do/don't.

      Working from Figma

      • Use Dev Mode: inspect spacing, typography, tokens/variables, and export assets (SVG for icons, WebP/AVIF for images at 1x/2x).
      • Figma Auto Layout maps to flexbox (direction, gap, padding, alignment, hug = fit-content, fill = flex: 1).
      • Check what the design doesn't show: hover/focus/disabled/error states, loading and empty states, long text, small screens, dark mode, RTL. Ask before building.
      • Match the spacing scale and tokens rather than pixel values; flag inconsistencies to the designer instead of hard-coding one-offs.

      Monorepos and micro-frontends

      Monorepos

      One repository containing several apps and packages (web app, admin app, design system, shared utils, API types), with shared tooling.

      text
      repo/
├─ apps/web          (Nuxt)     apps/admin (Vite + Vue)
├─ packages/ui       (design system)   packages/api-client   packages/config (eslint, tsconfig)
├─ pnpm-workspace.yaml
└─ turbo.json        (task pipeline + caching)

      Tools: pnpm workspaces (linking), Turborepo or Nx (task graph, only rebuild/test what changed, remote caching), Changesets (versioning and changelogs for published packages).

      Micro-frontends

      Splitting one product's frontend into independently built and deployed apps owned by different teams (e.g. search, checkout, account).

      TechniqueNotes
      Build-time packagesSimple, but every change needs a host redeploy
      Module Federation (webpack/Rspack, Vite plugins)Load remote modules at runtime, share dependencies
      single-spa / route-based compositionDifferent apps own different routes, can mix frameworks
      Web ComponentsFramework-agnostic integration boundary
      iframesStrong isolation, weaker UX and communication

      Micro-frontends solve an organisational problem (many teams, independent deploys), at the cost of complexity, bundle duplication and inconsistent UX. For one or two teams, a well-structured monorepo is almost always better. Saying this in an interview shows maturity.

      Production: monitoring, feature flags, incidents

      • Error tracking (Sentry, Datadog, Bugsnag): capture unhandled errors and promise rejections with user context, release version and breadcrumbs; upload source maps privately so stack traces point to your code.
      • Real-user monitoring (RUM): send Core Web Vitals from real users (web-vitals library → analytics) and track them per route and release.
      • Logging: structured client logs for key events (checkout failed, payment timeout), sampled to control cost; never log tokens or personal data.
      • Feature flags (LaunchDarkly, Unleash, GrowthBook, PostHog): ship code dark, roll out gradually, kill-switch risky features, run A/B tests.
      • Product analytics: event naming conventions, consent (cookie banners, GDPR/DPDP Act in India).
      • Release safety: preview deploys per PR, canary/gradual rollouts, instant rollback, cache-busting hashed assets, handling users on old bundles after deploy (chunk-load errors → prompt reload).

      Handling an incident

      1. Acknowledge, assess impact (how many users, which flows).
      2. Mitigate first: roll back or flip the feature flag.
      3. Find root cause with monitoring data, logs and reproduction.
      4. Fix with a test that would have caught it.
      5. Write a blameless postmortem: timeline, cause, what went well, action items.

      Deep performance debugging

      Reading the Performance panel

      • Record the slow interaction with CPU throttling (4×). Look for long tasks (> 50 ms, red corner) on the Main track.
      • Expand to see which functions take time: scripting (yellow), rendering/layout (purple), painting (green).
      • "Forced reflow" warnings = layout thrashing (reading layout after writing styles in a loop).
      • The Interactions track shows INP candidates: input delay, processing time, presentation delay.

      Fixing long tasks

      js
      // Break work into chunks and yield to the browser between them
async function processAll(items) {
  for (let i = 0; i < items.length; i++) {
    process(items[i]);
    if (i % 200 === 0) await (globalThis.scheduler?.yield?.() ?? new Promise(r => setTimeout(r)));
  }
}
// Heavy pure computation → Web Worker
const worker = new Worker(new URL("./parse.worker.js", import.meta.url), { type: "module" });
worker.postMessage(bigCsvText);
worker.onmessage = e => rows.value = e.data;
// Visual updates → requestAnimationFrame; idle work → requestIdleCallback

      Memory leaks

      Symptoms: the tab gets slower over time, memory keeps growing after navigating back and forth. Take heap snapshots (Memory panel) before and after repeating an action; compare and look for detached DOM nodes and growing arrays/maps. Usual causes: listeners/intervals/observers not removed on unmount, global caches without limits, closures holding big objects, third-party instances not destroyed (charts, maps, editors).

      Network and loading

      Use the Network waterfall to spot request chains (A waits for B waits for C): parallelise, preload, or fetch on the server. Check the coverage tab for unused JS/CSS, and set performance budgets (JS per route, LCP) enforced in CI with Lighthouse CI or bundle-size checks.

      Building AI features in the frontend (2026)

      Many product teams now ship chat assistants, summarisation, smart search and generation features. Frontend engineers own the streaming UI, state and UX around them.

      The architecture

      1. The browser sends the user message to your server (route handler / Nuxt server route). API keys never go to the browser.
      2. The server calls the model provider with a system prompt, context (retrieved documents for RAG) and tool definitions, with rate limits and auth.
      3. The response is streamed back token by token (SSE or a streamed fetch body) so users see text immediately.
      js
      // Reading a streamed response in the browser
const res = await fetch("/api/chat", { method: "POST", body: JSON.stringify({ messages }), signal: ctrl.signal });
const reader = res.body.pipeThrough(new TextDecoderStream()).getReader();
while (true) {
  const { value, done } = await reader.read();
  if (done) break;
  assistantMessage.value += value;          // Vue ref / React setState(prev => prev + value)
}
// Stop button: ctrl.abort()

      The Vercel AI SDK (ai package, with @ai-sdk/vue, @ai-sdk/react, Svelte adapters) handles streaming protocols, message state, tool calls and structured output across providers.

      UX patterns that matter

      • Stream text; show a typing state; let users stop, retry and edit their last message.
      • Render Markdown safely (sanitise; never v-html/dangerouslySetInnerHTML raw model output); syntax-highlight code with copy buttons.
      • Show sources/citations for RAG answers; make uncertainty visible; give feedback buttons.
      • Structured output (JSON matching a Zod schema) to fill forms or render UI components instead of plain text.
      • Tool calls ("the assistant is searching orders…") as visible steps; confirmations before actions with side effects.
      • Accessibility: announce streamed responses politely (aria-live="polite", not every token), keep focus stable.

      Risks to handle

      • Prompt injection: untrusted content (web pages, user files) can contain instructions. Never let model output directly trigger privileged actions without checks.
      • Cost and abuse: auth, rate limits, max tokens, caching repeated queries.
      • Privacy: don't send personal data the provider shouldn't see; tell users what's sent.
      • Latency: stream, show partial results, use smaller/faster models for simple tasks.

      How mid-level engineers work

      Break down and estimate

      Split a feature into vertical slices that each deliver something testable (API contract → skeleton UI with mocked data → real data → edge states → tests → analytics). Estimate slices, add buffer for unknowns, and raise risks early instead of on the deadline.

      Write a short tech spec before big work

      text
      Title · Author · Reviewers · Status
1. Problem & goals (and non-goals)
2. User flows / designs (links)
3. Proposed solution: components, state, API contract, rendering strategy
4. Alternatives considered and why not
5. Risks, performance, accessibility, security, analytics
6. Rollout: feature flag, migration, testing plan
7. Open questions

      Code review

      Review for correctness, edge cases, readability, tests, accessibility and performance, not personal style (let linters handle that). Explain the "why", offer alternatives, mark nitpicks as optional, and approve when it's good enough rather than perfect. As an author: small PRs, clear description, screenshots, self-review first.

      Mentoring and influence

      Pair with juniors, write the internal docs you wished existed, improve the developer experience (faster CI, better lint rules, templates), and propose improvements with data ("this change cuts our LCP from 3.4s to 2.1s").

      Working with others

      • Design: clarify states and edge cases early; propose simpler alternatives when something is expensive.
      • Backend: agree on API contracts first; mock them so both sides work in parallel.
      • Product: translate trade-offs into user and business terms (time, risk, impact).

      These are exactly the stories you'll tell in behavioural rounds. Keep a running "brag document" of them.

      Part

      Beyond the DOM

      Platform skills that come up in real work and senior interviews: Web Components, SVG and Canvas, backend basics for frontend engineers, and a one-page Angular primer.

      Web Components and Shadow DOM

      Browser-native components that work in any framework (or none). Used for design systems shared across Vue/React/Angular apps, embeddable widgets and micro-frontend boundaries.

      js
      class RatingStars extends HTMLElement {
  static observedAttributes = ["value"];
  constructor() {
    super();
    this.attachShadow({ mode: "open" });           // encapsulated DOM + styles
  }
  attributeChangedCallback() { this.render(); }
  connectedCallback() { this.render(); }             // added to the page
  disconnectedCallback() { /* cleanup */ }           // removed
  render() {
    const v = Number(this.getAttribute("value") ?? 0);
    this.shadowRoot.innerHTML = `
      <style>:host { display:inline-flex; gap:2px } span { color: var(--star-color, gold) }</style>
      ${[1,2,3,4,5].map(i => `<span aria-hidden="true">${i <= v ? "★" : "☆"}</span>`).join("")}
      <slot></slot>`;
    this.setAttribute("aria-label", `${v} out of 5`);
  }
}
customElements.define("rating-stars", RatingStars);
// <rating-stars value="4">(120 reviews)</rating-stars>
      • Custom elements (names need a hyphen), Shadow DOM (style/DOM encapsulation; styled from outside via CSS variables and ::part()), slots, templates.
      • Events out: this.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent("change", { detail, bubbles: true, composed: true })).
      • Lit is the popular library for writing them; Vue can build them with defineCustomElement().
      • Using them in Vue: tell the compiler with compilerOptions.isCustomElement: tag => tag.includes("-"); pass complex data as properties (.prop modifier).
      • Trade-offs: SSR is harder (Declarative Shadow DOM helps), form participation needs ElementInternals, accessibility across shadow boundaries needs care.

      SVG, Canvas and 3D

      SVGCanvas 2DWebGL / WebGPU
      ModelDOM elements (vector)Pixel drawing API (immediate mode)GPU-rendered 2D/3D
      Good forIcons, logos, charts with few hundred elements, diagramsThousands of moving objects, games, image editing, heatmaps3D scenes, huge datasets, visual effects
      Interaction/a11yEasy (events, CSS, <title>)Manual hit-testing; add accessible fallbackManual
      LibrariesD3, Vue/React chart libsChart.js, Konva, Fabric, PixiJSthree.js, TresJS (Vue), React Three Fiber, Babylon.js
      html
      <svg viewBox="0 0 24 24" width="24" height="24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" role="img" aria-label="Search">
  <circle cx="11" cy="11" r="7" /><path d="m20 20-3.5-3.5" />
</svg>
<!-- currentColor = inherits text colour; viewBox makes it scale; path "d" commands: M move, L line, C curve, Z close -->
      js
      const ctx = canvas.getContext("2d");
const dpr = devicePixelRatio;                         // crisp on retina screens
canvas.width = 400 * dpr; canvas.height = 200 * dpr; ctx.scale(dpr, dpr);
function frame(t) {
  ctx.clearRect(0, 0, 400, 200);
  ctx.fillStyle = "#4f46e5";
  ctx.beginPath(); ctx.arc(200 + Math.sin(t / 500) * 150, 100, 20, 0, Math.PI * 2); ctx.fill();
  requestAnimationFrame(frame);                       // ~60fps, pauses in background tabs
}
requestAnimationFrame(frame);

      Animate SVG with CSS (stroke-dasharray/stroke-dashoffset for line drawing), optimise exported SVGs with SVGO, and use an OffscreenCanvas in a worker for heavy canvas work.

      Backend basics for frontend engineers

      At 3 YOE you're expected to read backend code, design API contracts with backend engineers, and build a BFF or full-stack side project.

      Node.js essentials

      • Same JavaScript, different environment: no DOM; has fs, path, process, Buffer, streams, http.
      • Single-threaded event loop with a libuv thread pool for I/O; CPU-heavy work blocks every request (use worker threads or a queue).
      • ES modules ("type": "module"), built-in fetch, test runner, --watch; alternatives Bun and Deno.

      A small REST API (Hono; Express looks almost the same)

      js
      import { Hono } from "hono";
import { z } from "zod";
const app = new Hono();

const Todo = z.object({ text: z.string().min(1).max(200) });
app.get("/api/todos", async (c) => c.json(await db.todo.findMany({ where: { userId: c.get("user").id } })));
app.post("/api/todos", async (c) => {
  const parsed = Todo.safeParse(await c.req.json());
  if (!parsed.success) return c.json({ error: parsed.error.issues }, 422);
  const todo = await db.todo.create({ data: { ...parsed.data, userId: c.get("user").id } });
  return c.json(todo, 201);
});
export default app;

      REST API design checklist

      • Nouns for resources, HTTP verbs for actions, correct status codes, consistent error shape.
      • Pagination (cursor), filtering and sorting via query params; idempotency keys for payments.
      • Validation on the server, authentication + authorisation on every route, rate limiting.
      • Versioning strategy, OpenAPI docs, CORS configured for your origins.

      SQL in 10 lines

      A quick taste; the SQL & databases part covers what you need properly.

      sql
      SELECT id, name, price FROM products WHERE category = 'keyboards' AND price < 5000 ORDER BY price LIMIT 20;
SELECT o.id, u.email, o.total FROM orders o JOIN users u ON u.id = o.user_id WHERE o.created_at > now() - interval '7 days';
SELECT category, COUNT(*) AS n, AVG(price) FROM products GROUP BY category HAVING COUNT(*) > 5;
INSERT INTO todos (user_id, text) VALUES (1, 'Learn SQL');
UPDATE todos SET done = true WHERE id = 42;
DELETE FROM todos WHERE id = 42;
-- indexes speed up WHERE/JOIN/ORDER BY columns; transactions keep multi-step writes consistent

      Also worth knowing at a conceptual level: SQL vs NoSQL trade-offs, ORMs (Prisma, Drizzle), caching with Redis, background jobs/queues, Docker basics, and how your frontend's requests turn into database queries (N+1 problems show up as slow pages).

      Angular in one page

      Common in large enterprises, banks and service companies in India. If a job post asks for it, your Vue knowledge maps across quickly.

      ts
      import { Component, signal, computed, input, output } from "@angular/core";

@Component({
  selector: "app-counter",
  template: `
    <button (click)="inc()">Clicked {{ count() }} times</button>
    @if (count() > 5) { <p>That's a lot</p> }
    @for (item of items(); track item.id) { <li>{{ item.name }}</li> }
    <input [value]="label()" (input)="onInput($event)" />
  `,
})
export class CounterComponent {
  label = input("Counter");            // like props
  changed = output<number>();          // like emits
  count = signal(0);                   // like ref
  double = computed(() => this.count() * 2);
  items = signal([{ id: 1, name: "A" }]);
  inc() { this.count.update(c => c + 1); this.changed.emit(this.count()); }
  onInput(e: Event) { /* … */ }
}
      VueAngular
      ref / computed / watchsignal / computed / effect
      :prop, @event, v-model[prop], (event), [(ngModel)]
      v-if / v-for@if / @for (…; track id)
      props / emitsinput() / output()
      provide/inject, composablesDependency injection with services (inject(UserService))
      Vue Router, PiniaAngular Router, services/signals or NgRx
      NuxtAngular SSR (built in)

      Also expect RxJS (Observables, pipe, switchMap, debounceTime) in existing Angular codebases, the HttpClient, reactive forms, and the Angular CLI (ng generate, ng serve).

      Part

      SQL & databases

      What a 3-YOE frontend engineer needs: read and write real queries, understand joins, aggregates and indexes, design a sensible schema, avoid SQL injection and N+1 queries, and answer the SQL questions that come up in full-stack and product-company interviews.

      Why SQL matters for frontend engineers, and a practice schema

      You don't need to be a database administrator. You do need to read the queries behind your API, write queries for side projects, BFFs and server routes (Nuxt/Next), understand why a page is slow, and discuss data with backend engineers as an equal.

      What "enough SQL" means at 3 YOE

      Must knowShould knowNice to know
      SELECT, WHERE, ORDER BY, LIMIT, JOINs, GROUP BY + aggregates, INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE, primary/foreign keys, parameterised queriesSubqueries, CTEs, basic window functions, indexes and EXPLAIN, transactions, normalisation, pagination strategies, ORMsIsolation levels, query planner details, partitioning, replication, full-text search tuning

      Where to practise

      • PostgreSQL is the default choice for new projects (Neon, Supabase, local Docker). SQLite is a single file, great for learning and small apps. MySQL is common in existing companies.
      • Browser playgrounds: DB Fiddle, SQLite online; exercises: SQLBolt, pgexercises.com, LeetCode's SQL 50, HackerRank SQL.
      • GUI clients: TablePlus, DBeaver, pgAdmin, or psql in the terminal.

      The schema used in this part

      sql
      CREATE TABLE users (
  id          SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  email       TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  city        TEXT,
  created_at  TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
CREATE TABLE categories (id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE);
CREATE TABLE products (
  id          SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  category_id INT REFERENCES categories(id),
  name        TEXT NOT NULL,
  price       NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL CHECK (price >= 0),
  stock       INT NOT NULL DEFAULT 0
);
CREATE TABLE orders (
  id          SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id     INT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id),
  status      TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pending',   -- pending | paid | shipped | cancelled
  created_at  TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
CREATE TABLE order_items (                       -- many-to-many between orders and products
  order_id    INT REFERENCES orders(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
  product_id  INT REFERENCES products(id),
  quantity    INT NOT NULL CHECK (quantity > 0),
  unit_price  NUMERIC(10,2) NOT NULL,            -- price at time of purchase
  PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id)
);

      Relationships: a user has many orders (one-to-many); an order has many products and a product appears in many orders (many-to-many through order_items); a product belongs to one category.

      Querying: SELECT, filtering, sorting

      sql
      SELECT name, price FROM products;                         -- choose columns (avoid SELECT * in app code)
SELECT * FROM products WHERE price < 1000 AND stock > 0;  -- AND, OR, NOT
SELECT * FROM products WHERE category_id IN (1, 3, 5);
SELECT * FROM products WHERE price BETWEEN 500 AND 2000;  -- inclusive
SELECT * FROM products WHERE name ILIKE '%keyboard%';     -- ILIKE = case-insensitive (Postgres); LIKE is case-sensitive
SELECT * FROM users WHERE city IS NULL;                   -- NOT "= NULL"
SELECT DISTINCT city FROM users;                          -- unique values
SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY price DESC, name ASC;     -- sort by several columns
SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY id LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40;    -- page 3 of 20 (see pagination topic)

SELECT name,
       price,
       price * 0.9 AS sale_price,                         -- computed column with alias
       CASE WHEN stock = 0 THEN 'Out of stock'
            WHEN stock < 5 THEN 'Only a few left'
            ELSE 'In stock' END AS availability
FROM products;

SELECT name, COALESCE(city, 'Unknown') AS city_label FROM users;   -- first non-null value

      How a query is logically evaluated

      FROM/JOIN → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → DISTINCT → ORDER BY → LIMIT. That's why you can't use a SELECT alias inside WHERE, but you can in ORDER BY.

      NULL is "unknown": NULL = NULL is not true, comparisons with NULL yield NULL, and WHERE city <> 'Pune' silently excludes rows where city is NULL. Use IS NULL, IS DISTINCT FROM and COALESCE.

      JOINs

      JoinReturnsTypical use
      INNER JOINOnly rows with a match on both sidesOrders with their user
      LEFT JOINAll rows from the left table, matched right rows or NULLsAll users, with orders if any; "users without orders"
      RIGHT JOINMirror of LEFT (rarely used; swap the tables instead)
      FULL OUTER JOINAll rows from both, NULLs where no matchReconciling two lists
      CROSS JOINEvery combinationGenerating size × colour variants
      Self joinA table joined to itselfEmployee → manager, comment → parent comment
      sql
      -- Orders with customer names
SELECT o.id, u.name, o.status, o.created_at
FROM orders o
JOIN users u ON u.id = o.user_id;                 -- JOIN = INNER JOIN

-- Order details: order → items → products (many-to-many)
SELECT o.id AS order_id, p.name, oi.quantity, oi.unit_price, oi.quantity * oi.unit_price AS line_total
FROM orders o
JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
JOIN products p     ON p.id = oi.product_id
WHERE o.id = 42;

-- Users who have never ordered (anti-join)
SELECT u.id, u.email
FROM users u
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id
WHERE o.id IS NULL;
-- same with NOT EXISTS (often clearer and fast):
SELECT u.id, u.email FROM users u
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.user_id = u.id);

      A condition on the right table placed in WHERE turns a LEFT JOIN into an inner join (NULL rows get filtered out). Put it in the ON clause instead: LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.user_id = u.id AND o.status = 'paid'.

      Joining a one-to-many relationship multiplies rows (one row per order item). That's expected; aggregate or use DISTINCT deliberately, not as a band-aid.

      Aggregates, GROUP BY, subqueries, CTEs and window functions

      Aggregates and GROUP BY

      sql
      SELECT COUNT(*) FROM orders;                                   -- all rows
SELECT COUNT(city) FROM users;                                 -- non-null values only
SELECT MIN(price), MAX(price), AVG(price), SUM(stock) FROM products;

-- Revenue per category (dashboard card)
SELECT c.name AS category, SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) AS revenue, COUNT(DISTINCT o.id) AS orders
FROM order_items oi
JOIN orders o     ON o.id = oi.order_id AND o.status = 'paid'
JOIN products p   ON p.id = oi.product_id
JOIN categories c ON c.id = p.category_id
GROUP BY c.name
HAVING SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) > 10000              -- filter groups (WHERE filters rows)
ORDER BY revenue DESC;

-- Orders per day for a chart
SELECT date_trunc('day', created_at) AS day, COUNT(*) AS orders
FROM orders WHERE created_at >= now() - interval '30 days'
GROUP BY day ORDER BY day;

      Every selected column must be either inside an aggregate or listed in GROUP BY.

      Subqueries and CTEs

      sql
      -- Products priced above the average
SELECT name, price FROM products WHERE price > (SELECT AVG(price) FROM products);

-- CTE: name intermediate results for readability
WITH user_totals AS (
  SELECT o.user_id, SUM(oi.quantity * oi.unit_price) AS spent
  FROM orders o JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id
  WHERE o.status = 'paid'
  GROUP BY o.user_id
)
SELECT u.name, t.spent
FROM user_totals t JOIN users u ON u.id = t.user_id
WHERE t.spent > 50000
ORDER BY t.spent DESC;

-- Recursive CTE: nested comments / category tree
WITH RECURSIVE thread AS (
  SELECT id, parent_id, body, 0 AS depth FROM comments WHERE id = 1
  UNION ALL
  SELECT c.id, c.parent_id, c.body, t.depth + 1 FROM comments c JOIN thread t ON c.parent_id = t.id
)
SELECT * FROM thread;

      Window functions (aggregates without collapsing rows)

      sql
      SELECT name, category_id, price,
       RANK()       OVER (PARTITION BY category_id ORDER BY price DESC) AS price_rank,  -- ties share a rank
       ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY category_id ORDER BY price DESC) AS rn,          -- unique 1,2,3…
       AVG(price)   OVER (PARTITION BY category_id) AS category_avg
FROM products;

-- Running total of revenue by day, and change from the previous day
SELECT day, revenue,
       SUM(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY day) AS running_total,
       revenue - LAG(revenue) OVER (ORDER BY day) AS change
FROM daily_revenue;

      Window functions answer the classic "top N per group", "running total" and "compare with previous row" questions.

      Writing data and transactions

      sql
      INSERT INTO users (email, name, city) VALUES ('a@b.com', 'Harsh', 'Bhopal') RETURNING id;   -- get the new id
INSERT INTO categories (name) VALUES ('Keyboards'), ('Mice');                                  -- multiple rows

UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7 AND stock > 0;   -- ⚠️ always have a WHERE
DELETE FROM orders WHERE status = 'cancelled' AND created_at < now() - interval '1 year';

-- Upsert: insert, or update if the unique key already exists (Postgres / SQLite)
INSERT INTO cart_items (user_id, product_id, quantity) VALUES (1, 7, 1)
ON CONFLICT (user_id, product_id) DO UPDATE SET quantity = cart_items.quantity + EXCLUDED.quantity;

      Transactions and ACID

      sql
      BEGIN;
  INSERT INTO orders (user_id, status) VALUES (1, 'paid') RETURNING id;      -- say 101
  INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, quantity, unit_price) VALUES (101, 7, 2, 1999);
  UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 2 WHERE id = 7 AND stock >= 2;
  -- if any step fails (or stock was insufficient), ROLLBACK; otherwise:
COMMIT;
      ACIDMeaning
      AtomicityAll statements in a transaction succeed, or none do
      ConsistencyConstraints (keys, CHECKs) hold before and after
      IsolationConcurrent transactions don't see each other's half-done work (levels: read committed, repeatable read, serializable)
      DurabilityOnce committed, data survives crashes

      Why frontend engineers care: a checkout clicked twice, two tabs updating the same record, stock going negative. Solutions: transactions, unique constraints, idempotency keys from the client, conditional updates (WHERE stock >= 2), optimistic locking with a version column.

      DELETE vs TRUNCATE vs DROP: DELETE removes chosen rows (can be filtered, fires triggers); TRUNCATE empties the whole table fast; DROP removes the table itself.

      Schema design

      Keys and constraints

      • Primary key: unique id per row (SERIAL/BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY, or UUID when ids are created client-side or exposed publicly).
      • Foreign key: REFERENCES another table, keeps data consistent; choose ON DELETE CASCADE / SET NULL / RESTRICT deliberately.
      • NOT NULL, UNIQUE, CHECK, DEFAULT: let the database reject bad data, not just the UI.

      Normalisation in one paragraph

      Store each fact once. 1NF: no lists in a column (not "tag1,tag2"; use a tags table). 2NF/3NF: every column depends on the key, the whole key and nothing but the key (don't store the customer's city in every order). Normalise first; denormalise deliberately for read performance (e.g. a cached order_total, comment_count) and keep it in sync.

      Practical choices

      • Money: NUMERIC or integer paise, never floats.
      • Time: TIMESTAMPTZ in UTC; format in the user's timezone on the frontend with Intl.
      • Enums/statuses: a CHECK constraint, a Postgres enum, or a lookup table.
      • Flexible attributes: JSONB columns (queryable, indexable) for settings or product specs; don't turn the whole schema into JSON.
      • created_at/updated_at on every table; soft delete (deleted_at) when you need undo/audit.
