      • Schema changes via migrations (Prisma Migrate, Drizzle Kit, Knex, Flyway), committed to Git and run in CI/CD. Never edit production by hand.

      SQL vs NoSQL

      Relational (Postgres, MySQL, SQLite)Document / key-value (MongoDB, DynamoDB, Firestore, Redis)
      Structured data with relationships, joins, transactions, strong consistency; flexible queryingFlexible or nested documents, simple access patterns at huge scale, caching (Redis), real-time sync (Firestore)
      Default for most appsChoose for a specific access pattern, not to avoid schema design

      Indexes, EXPLAIN, N+1 and pagination

      Indexes

      An index is a sorted lookup structure (usually a B-tree) that lets the database find rows without scanning the whole table, like a book's index.

      sql
      CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_id ON orders (user_id);                    -- foreign keys you join/filter on
CREATE INDEX idx_orders_user_created ON orders (user_id, created_at DESC); -- composite: "this user's latest orders"
CREATE UNIQUE INDEX idx_users_email ON users (lower(email));             -- expression index + uniqueness
CREATE INDEX idx_products_name_trgm ON products USING gin (name gin_trgm_ops);  -- fast ILIKE '%x%' (pg_trgm)
      • Index columns used in WHERE, JOIN and ORDER BY of frequent queries.
      • Composite indexes work left to right: (user_id, created_at) helps "where user_id = ? order by created_at", not "where created_at > ?" alone.
      • Trade-off: indexes speed up reads but slow writes and use storage. Don't index everything.
      • Functions on columns (WHERE lower(email) = …) need a matching expression index.

      EXPLAIN

      sql
      EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM orders WHERE user_id = 7 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 10;
-- "Seq Scan" on a big table = reading every row → probably missing an index
-- "Index Scan" / "Index Only Scan" = using an index; compare "actual time" before/after

      The N+1 problem

      js
      // ❌ 1 query for orders + N queries for users (slow page, DB overloaded)
const orders = await db.query("SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 50");
for (const o of orders) o.user = await db.query("SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = $1", [o.user_id]);

// ✅ one JOIN, or one batched query
SELECT o.*, u.name FROM orders o JOIN users u ON u.id = o.user_id LIMIT 50;
-- or: SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = ANY($1)   with all user ids at once
// ORMs: prisma.order.findMany({ include: { user: true } }); GraphQL: DataLoader batching

      Pagination: offset vs keyset (cursor)

      sql
      -- Offset: simple, supports "jump to page 7", but slow on deep pages and skips/duplicates rows when data changes
SELECT * FROM products ORDER BY id LIMIT 20 OFFSET 2000;

-- Keyset / cursor: fast at any depth, stable for infinite scroll
SELECT * FROM products WHERE (created_at, id) < ($last_created_at, $last_id)
ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20;
-- API returns nextCursor = encoded (created_at, id) of the last row

      This is why APIs return nextCursor and why your infinite-scroll component should use it.

      Search

      ILIKE '%term%' is fine for small tables (with a trigram index for larger). For real search use Postgres full-text search (to_tsvector/to_tsquery) or a search engine (Meilisearch, Typesense, Algolia, Elasticsearch) with typo tolerance and facets.

      SQL in application code: injection, ORMs, Supabase

      SQL injection

      js
      // ❌ NEVER build SQL with string concatenation from user input
db.query(`SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = '${email}'`);
// email = "' OR '1'='1"  → returns every user;  "'; DROP TABLE users; --" → disaster

// ✅ parameterised queries: values are sent separately from the SQL text
db.query("SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = $1", [email]);
// ORMs and query builders (Prisma, Drizzle, Knex) parameterise for you;
// tagged templates like sql`… ${email}` in postgres.js / Drizzle are also safe

      Other rules: least-privilege database users, never expose the database directly to the browser, validate input with Zod, and don't leak raw DB errors to clients.

      ORMs and query builders

      ts
      // Prisma
const orders = await prisma.order.findMany({
  where: { userId, status: "paid" },
  include: { items: { include: { product: true } } },
  orderBy: { createdAt: "desc" },
  take: 20,
});
// Drizzle (SQL-like, typed)
const rows = await db.select({ id: orders.id, name: users.name })
  .from(orders).innerJoin(users, eq(users.id, orders.userId))
  .where(eq(orders.status, "paid")).limit(20);

      ORMs give type safety and migrations; you still need SQL to understand what they generate, spot N+1 queries, and write complex reports (use raw SQL when it's clearer).

      Backend-as-a-service

      Supabase (Postgres + auth + storage + realtime) lets the frontend query tables directly through an API, protected by Row Level Security policies written in SQL (CREATE POLICY … USING (user_id = auth.uid())). If RLS is off or wrong, any user can read everyone's data — a common real-world leak. Firebase/Firestore uses security rules for the same purpose.

      Caching

      Expensive queries behind hot pages: cache results (Redis, Next/Nuxt data cache, HTTP cache/CDN), use materialised views for heavy dashboards, and invalidate on writes.

      SQL interview questions

      Find the second highest price.
      sql
      SELECT MAX(price) FROM products WHERE price < (SELECT MAX(price) FROM products);
-- or, general Nth highest (distinct values):
SELECT DISTINCT price FROM products ORDER BY price DESC LIMIT 1 OFFSET 1;
      Find duplicate emails.
      sql
      SELECT lower(email) AS email, COUNT(*) FROM users GROUP BY lower(email) HAVING COUNT(*) > 1;
      Top 3 most expensive products in each category.
      sql
      SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT p.*, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY category_id ORDER BY price DESC) AS rn FROM products p
) ranked WHERE rn <= 3;
      Customers who placed an order in each of the last 3 months.
      sql
      SELECT user_id FROM orders
WHERE created_at >= date_trunc('month', now()) - interval '2 months'
GROUP BY user_id
HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT date_trunc('month', created_at)) = 3;
      Each user's most recent order.
      sql
      SELECT DISTINCT ON (user_id) user_id, id, created_at      -- Postgres
FROM orders ORDER BY user_id, created_at DESC;
-- portable: ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY created_at DESC) = 1
      Delete duplicate rows keeping the lowest id.
      sql
      DELETE FROM users a USING users b
WHERE a.id > b.id AND lower(a.email) = lower(b.email);
      WHERE vs HAVING?

      WHERE filters rows before grouping; HAVING filters groups after aggregation (it can use COUNT, SUM…).

      INNER vs LEFT JOIN?

      INNER returns only matched rows; LEFT returns all rows from the left table with NULLs where there's no match (useful for "find missing" queries).

      UNION vs UNION ALL?

      UNION removes duplicates (extra sort/hash work); UNION ALL keeps everything and is faster. Use UNION ALL unless you need deduplication.

      What is an index and when would you not add one?

      A data structure that speeds up lookups on columns. Skip it on small tables, rarely queried columns, low-selectivity columns (like a boolean) alone, or write-heavy tables where the write cost outweighs reads.

      What is the N+1 problem and how do you fix it?

      One query for a list plus one query per item. Fix with a JOIN, a batched WHERE id IN (…), ORM eager loading (include), or DataLoader in GraphQL.

      Explain ACID and give a frontend-relevant example.

      Atomicity, consistency, isolation, durability. Example: placing an order must create the order, its items and decrement stock together, or not at all, even if the user double-clicks "Pay".

      Offset vs cursor pagination?

      Offset is simple and allows page jumps but gets slower with depth and duplicates/skips rows when data changes. Cursor (keyset) is fast and stable, ideal for infinite scroll, but doesn't support jumping to an arbitrary page.

      How do you prevent SQL injection?

      Parameterised queries or an ORM/query builder, never string concatenation; validate input; least-privilege DB users; don't expose raw DB errors.

      Normalisation: why, and when would you denormalise?

      To store each fact once and avoid update anomalies. Denormalise for read-heavy hot paths (counts, totals, search documents) when the cost of joins is too high, keeping copies in sync via transactions, triggers or jobs.

      Part

      Interview prep

      The rounds you'll actually face: JavaScript polyfills and utilities, output-prediction questions, UI machine coding, frontend system design, DSA and behavioural questions.

      Write it yourself: polyfills and utilities

      Classic "implement X" questions. Try each for 10–15 minutes before opening the answer. Say your approach out loud and mention edge cases.

      Array.prototype.map, filter and reduce
      js
      Array.prototype.myMap = function (cb, thisArg) {
  const out = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < this.length; i++) {
    if (i in this) out[i] = cb.call(thisArg, this[i], i, this);   // skip holes like the real one
  }
  return out;
};
Array.prototype.myFilter = function (cb, thisArg) {
  const out = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < this.length; i++) if (i in this && cb.call(thisArg, this[i], i, this)) out.push(this[i]);
  return out;
};
Array.prototype.myReduce = function (cb, initial) {
  let i = 0, acc = initial;
  if (arguments.length < 2) {
    while (i < this.length && !(i in this)) i++;
    if (i >= this.length) throw new TypeError("Reduce of empty array with no initial value");
    acc = this[i++];
  }
  for (; i < this.length; i++) if (i in this) acc = cb(acc, this[i], i, this);
  return acc;
};
      Function.prototype.bind (and call/apply)
      js
      Function.prototype.myCall = function (ctx, ...args) {
  ctx = ctx ?? globalThis;
  const key = Symbol();
  ctx[key] = this;                 // "this" is the function being called
  const result = ctx[key](...args);
  delete ctx[key];
  return result;
};
Function.prototype.myApply = function (ctx, args = []) { return this.myCall(ctx, ...args); };
Function.prototype.myBind = function (ctx, ...preset) {
  const fn = this;
  return function (...later) { return fn.apply(ctx, [...preset, ...later]); };
};
      debounce (with leading option) and throttle
      js
      function debounce(fn, wait, { leading = false } = {}) {
  let timer = null;
  return function (...args) {
    const callNow = leading && !timer;
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => { timer = null; if (!leading) fn.apply(this, args); }, wait);
    if (callNow) fn.apply(this, args);
  };
}
function throttle(fn, wait) {
  let last = 0, trailing = null;
  return function (...args) {
    const now = Date.now(), remaining = wait - (now - last);
    if (remaining <= 0) { last = now; fn.apply(this, args); }
    else { clearTimeout(trailing); trailing = setTimeout(() => { last = Date.now(); fn.apply(this, args); }, remaining); }
  };
}
      Promise.all, Promise.allSettled, Promise.race, Promise.any
      js
      function promiseAll(items) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const results = []; let done = 0;
    if (items.length === 0) return resolve(results);
    items.forEach((p, i) => {
      Promise.resolve(p).then(v => {
        results[i] = v;                      // keep original order
        if (++done === items.length) resolve(results);
      }, reject);                            // first rejection rejects all
    });
  });
}
function allSettled(items) {
  return promiseAll(items.map(p => Promise.resolve(p).then(
    value => ({ status: "fulfilled", value }),
    reason => ({ status: "rejected", reason }))));
}
function race(items) {
  return new Promise((res, rej) => items.forEach(p => Promise.resolve(p).then(res, rej)));
}
function any(items) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const errors = []; let failed = 0;
    if (!items.length) return reject(new AggregateError([], "All promises were rejected"));
    items.forEach((p, i) => Promise.resolve(p).then(resolve, e => {
      errors[i] = e; if (++failed === items.length) reject(new AggregateError(errors, "All promises were rejected"));
    }));
  });
}
      Build a basic Promise class
      js
      class MyPromise {
  #state = "pending"; #value; #handlers = [];
  constructor(executor) {
    const settle = (state, value) => {
      if (this.#state !== "pending") return;
      if (state === "fulfilled" && value && typeof value.then === "function") return value.then(v => settle("fulfilled", v), e => settle("rejected", e));
      this.#state = state; this.#value = value;
      this.#handlers.forEach(h => h()); this.#handlers = [];
    };
    try { executor(v => settle("fulfilled", v), e => settle("rejected", e)); }
    catch (e) { settle("rejected", e); }
  }
  then(onOk, onErr) {
    return new MyPromise((resolve, reject) => {
      const run = () => queueMicrotask(() => {
        const cb = this.#state === "fulfilled" ? onOk : onErr;
        if (typeof cb !== "function") return (this.#state === "fulfilled" ? resolve : reject)(this.#value);
        try { resolve(cb(this.#value)); } catch (e) { reject(e); }
      });
      this.#state === "pending" ? this.#handlers.push(run) : run();
    });
  }
  catch(onErr) { return this.then(undefined, onErr); }
  finally(fn) { return this.then(v => { fn(); return v; }, e => { fn(); throw e; }); }
  static resolve(v) { return new MyPromise(r => r(v)); }
}
      Deep clone (with nested objects, arrays, Dates, cycles)
      js
      function deepClone(value, seen = new WeakMap()) {
  if (value === null || typeof value !== "object") return value;
  if (value instanceof Date) return new Date(value);
  if (value instanceof RegExp) return new RegExp(value);
  if (seen.has(value)) return seen.get(value);                 // circular references
  const copy = Array.isArray(value) ? [] : Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(value));
  seen.set(value, copy);
  for (const key of Reflect.ownKeys(value)) copy[key] = deepClone(value[key], seen);
  return copy;
}
// In real code: structuredClone(value)
      Deep equal
      js
      function deepEqual(a, b) {
  if (Object.is(a, b)) return true;
  if (typeof a !== "object" || typeof b !== "object" || !a || !b) return false;
  if (Array.isArray(a) !== Array.isArray(b)) return false;
  const ka = Object.keys(a), kb = Object.keys(b);
  if (ka.length !== kb.length) return false;
  return ka.every(k => Object.hasOwn(b, k) && deepEqual(a[k], b[k]));
}
      Flatten an array (to any depth) and flatten an object
      js
      function flat(arr, depth = Infinity) {
  return depth < 1 ? arr.slice() : arr.reduce((acc, v) =>
    acc.concat(Array.isArray(v) ? flat(v, depth - 1) : v), []);
}
function flattenObject(obj, prefix = "", out = {}) {
  for (const [k, v] of Object.entries(obj)) {
    const key = prefix ? `${prefix}.${k}` : k;
    if (v && typeof v === "object" && !Array.isArray(v)) flattenObject(v, key, out);
    else out[key] = v;
  }
  return out;
}
flattenObject({ a: { b: 1, c: { d: 2 } } });   // { "a.b": 1, "a.c.d": 2 }
      curry, compose/pipe, memoize, once
      js
      const curry = fn => function curried(...args) {
  return args.length >= fn.length ? fn.apply(this, args) : (...more) => curried.apply(this, [...args, ...more]);
};
const sum3 = curry((a, b, c) => a + b + c);
sum3(1)(2)(3); sum3(1, 2)(3);   // 6

const pipe = (...fns) => x => fns.reduce((v, f) => f(v), x);
const compose = (...fns) => x => fns.reduceRight((v, f) => f(v), x);

function memoize(fn, keyFn = (...a) => JSON.stringify(a)) {
  const cache = new Map();
  return function (...args) {
    const key = keyFn(...args);
    if (!cache.has(key)) cache.set(key, fn.apply(this, args));
    return cache.get(key);
  };
}
function once(fn) { let done = false, result; return (...a) => done ? result : (done = true, result = fn(...a)); }

// infinite currying: sum(1)(2)(3)() → 6
const sum = a => b => b === undefined ? a : sum(a + b);
      Event emitter (on, off, once, emit)
      js
      class EventEmitter {
  #events = new Map();
  on(name, fn) { (this.#events.get(name) ?? this.#events.set(name, new Set()).get(name)).add(fn); return () => this.off(name, fn); }
  off(name, fn) { this.#events.get(name)?.delete(fn); }
  once(name, fn) { const w = (...a) => { this.off(name, w); fn(...a); }; return this.on(name, w); }
  emit(name, ...args) { [...(this.#events.get(name) ?? [])].forEach(fn => fn(...args)); }
}
      get(obj, "a.b[0].c", default) — safe path access
      js
      function get(obj, path, fallback) {
  const keys = Array.isArray(path) ? path : path.replace(/\[(\w+)\]/g, ".$1").split(".").filter(Boolean);
  let cur = obj;
  for (const k of keys) { if (cur == null) return fallback; cur = cur[k]; }
  return cur === undefined ? fallback : cur;
}
      Retry with exponential backoff, sleep, timeout, promisify
      js
      const sleep = ms => new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, ms));

async function retry(fn, { retries = 3, base = 300 } = {}) {
  for (let attempt = 0; ; attempt++) {
    try { return await fn(); }
    catch (e) {
      if (attempt >= retries) throw e;
      await sleep(base * 2 ** attempt + Math.random() * 100);   // 300, 600, 1200… + jitter
    }
  }
}
const withTimeout = (p, ms) => Promise.race([p, new Promise((_, rej) => setTimeout(() => rej(new Error("Timeout")), ms))]);
const promisify = fn => (...args) => new Promise((res, rej) => fn(...args, (err, data) => err ? rej(err) : res(data)));
      Run async tasks with a concurrency limit
      js
      async function mapLimit(items, limit, worker) {
  const results = new Array(items.length);
  let next = 0;
  async function run() {
    while (next < items.length) { const i = next++; results[i] = await worker(items[i], i); }
  }
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: Math.min(limit, items.length) }, run));
  return results;
}
      groupBy, chunk, unique, counting
      js
      const groupBy = (arr, fn) => arr.reduce((acc, x) => ((acc[fn(x)] ||= []).push(x), acc), {});
const chunk = (arr, n) => Array.from({ length: Math.ceil(arr.length / n) }, (_, i) => arr.slice(i * n, i * n + n));
const unique = arr => [...new Set(arr)];
const countBy = arr => arr.reduce((m, x) => m.set(x, (m.get(x) ?? 0) + 1), new Map());
      Implement a simple LRU cache
      js
      class LRUCache {
  constructor(capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; this.map = new Map(); }   // Map keeps insertion order
  get(key) {
    if (!this.map.has(key)) return -1;
    const v = this.map.get(key); this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, v);   // mark as most recent
    return v;
  }
  put(key, value) {
    this.map.delete(key); this.map.set(key, value);
    if (this.map.size > this.capacity) this.map.delete(this.map.keys().next().value); // evict oldest
  }
}
      getElementsByClassName / a tiny querySelector-free DOM walk
      js
      function byClass(root, cls) {
  const out = [];
  (function walk(node) {
    if (node.classList?.contains(cls)) out.push(node);
    for (const child of node.children) walk(child);
  })(root);
  return out;
}

      Predict the output

      Common tricky snippets. Reason step by step: hoisting → scope → this → coercion → event loop order.

      console.log(a); var a = 1; console.log(b); let b = 2;

      undefined, then ReferenceError (b is in the TDZ).

      for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i));

      3 3 3. One shared var i. With let: 0 1 2.

      Event loop order
      js
      console.log(1);
setTimeout(() => console.log(2));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log(3)).then(() => console.log(4));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log(5));
(async () => { console.log(6); await null; console.log(7); })();
console.log(8);
// 1 6 8 3 5 7 4 2
// sync first (1, 6, 8), then microtasks in queue order (3, 5, 7, then 4 queued by 3), then the timer (2)
      this in methods and arrows
      js
      const obj = {
  name: "A",
  regular() { return this.name; },
  arrow: () => this?.name,
  nested() { return [1].map(function () { return this?.name; })[0]; },
  nestedArrow() { return [1].map(() => this.name)[0]; },
};
obj.regular();     // "A"
obj.arrow();       // undefined (arrow takes this from module/global scope)
obj.nested();      // undefined (plain function call)
obj.nestedArrow(); // "A"
const f = obj.regular; f();   // undefined in strict mode / modules
      Coercion round
      js
      [] == false        // true   ([] → "" → 0, false → 0)
[] == ![]          // true
null == 0          // false  (null only == undefined)
null >= 0          // true   (relational converts null to 0)
"b" + "a" + +"a" + "a"  // "baNaNa"
0.1 + 0.2 === 0.3  // false
typeof typeof 1    // "string"
[1, 2, 3] + [4]    // "1,2,34"
{} + []            // in console as a statement: 0; as an expression ({} + []): "[object Object]"
      Closures in a counter factory
      js
      function make() { let c = 0; return { inc: () => ++c, get: () => c }; }
const a = make(), b = make();
a.inc(); a.inc(); b.inc();
console.log(a.get(), b.get());   // 2 1 — each call to make has its own c

      React: how many times does "effect" log on mount in development?

      Twice with <StrictMode> (mount → cleanup → mount, to catch missing cleanups); once in production.

      UI machine coding rounds

      You get 60–90 minutes to build a working component in React (or vanilla JS/Vue). Interviewers judge: does it work, is the code organised, are edge cases and accessibility handled, and how you communicate.

      A repeatable approach

      1. Clarify (5 min): requirements, data source, must-haves vs nice-to-haves, keyboard support, mobile?
      2. Plan (5 min): sketch components, state shape, events. Say it out loud.
      3. Build the core working end to end first (ugly is fine).
      4. Handle states: loading, empty, error, disabled, edge inputs.
      5. Polish: accessibility (roles, labels, focus, keys), basic styling, cleanup (timers, listeners).
      6. Explain trade-offs and what you'd do with more time (tests, performance, virtualisation).

      Frequently asked builds and what they test

      BuildKey points
      Todo list with filtersState updates, derived state, keys, edit mode, localStorage
      Autocomplete / typeaheadDebounce, cancel stale requests, cache, keyboard nav (↑↓ Enter Esc), highlight match, aria-activedescendant
      Modal / dialogPortal, focus trap, Escape, backdrop click, return focus, scroll lock
      Tabs / accordionCompound components, ARIA roles, arrow keys
      Star ratingHover preview vs selected, keyboard, half stars
      Infinite scroll / paginationIntersectionObserver, loading guard, cursor pagination, dedupe
      Image carouselIndex wrap-around, autoplay with cleanup, pause on hover, lazy images
      Progress bar / stopwatch / countdownIntervals, refs, cleanup, drift correction with timestamps
      Nested comments / file explorerRecursive components, tree state updates
      Multi-step formValidation per step, shared state, back/next, summary
      Kanban drag and dropDrag events, reorder immutably
      Tic-tac-toe / memory game / SnakeGame state, winner detection, reset
      Data tableSort, filter, paginate, select rows, memoisation
      OTP inputRefs array, auto-advance, backspace, paste

      Worked example: autocomplete in React

      jsx
      import { useEffect, useId, useRef, useState } from "react";

export default function Autocomplete({ fetchSuggestions, onSelect }) {
  const [query, setQuery] = useState("");
  const [items, setItems] = useState([]);
  const [status, setStatus] = useState("idle");      // idle | loading | error | done
  const [active, setActive] = useState(-1);
  const [open, setOpen] = useState(false);
  const cache = useRef(new Map());
  const listId = useId();

  useEffect(() => {
    const q = query.trim();
    if (!q) { setItems([]); setStatus("idle"); return; }
    if (cache.current.has(q)) { setItems(cache.current.get(q)); setStatus("done"); return; }
    const ctrl = new AbortController();
    const t = setTimeout(async () => {                // debounce 300ms
      setStatus("loading");
      try {
        const data = await fetchSuggestions(q, ctrl.signal);
        cache.current.set(q, data);
        setItems(data); setStatus("done"); setActive(-1);
      } catch (e) { if (e.name !== "AbortError") setStatus("error"); }
    }, 300);
    return () => { clearTimeout(t); ctrl.abort(); };  // cancel stale timer + request
  }, [query, fetchSuggestions]);

  function choose(item) { setQuery(item); setOpen(false); onSelect?.(item); }

  function onKeyDown(e) {
    if (e.key === "ArrowDown") { e.preventDefault(); setOpen(true); setActive(i => Math.min(i + 1, items.length - 1)); }
    if (e.key === "ArrowUp")   { e.preventDefault(); setActive(i => Math.max(i - 1, 0)); }
    if (e.key === "Enter" && active >= 0) choose(items[active]);
    if (e.key === "Escape") setOpen(false);
  }

  return (
    <div className="ac">
      <input
        role="combobox" aria-expanded={open} aria-controls={listId} aria-autocomplete="list"
        aria-activedescendant={active >= 0 ? `${listId}-${active}` : undefined}
        value={query} placeholder="Search cities"
        onChange={e => { setQuery(e.target.value); setOpen(true); }}
        onKeyDown={onKeyDown} onBlur={() => setTimeout(() => setOpen(false), 100)}
      />
      {open && query && (
        <ul id={listId} role="listbox">
          {status === "loading" && <li aria-live="polite">Searching…</li>}
          {status === "error" && <li>Couldn't load suggestions. Keep typing to retry.</li>}
          {status === "done" && items.length === 0 && <li>No matches for “{query}”</li>}
          {items.map((item, i) => (
            <li key={item} id={`${listId}-${i}`} role="option" aria-selected={i === active}
                onMouseDown={() => choose(item)} className={i === active ? "active" : ""}>
              {item}
            </li>
          ))}
        </ul>
      )}
    </div>
  );
}

      Frontend system design

      For mid-level and above (sometimes juniors at product companies): "Design Instagram's feed", "Design an autocomplete", "Design Google Docs' collaborative editor". There's no single right answer; they assess structured thinking and trade-offs.

      A framework: RADIO

      StepCover
      Requirements (≈10%)Functional (what users do), non-functional (performance, offline, devices, a11y, i18n, scale), out of scope
      Architecture (≈20%)Component diagram: server, client store, views, controllers; rendering strategy (CSR/SSR/SSG); where state lives
      Data model (≈10%)Entities and fields; client-side store shape (normalised by id); what's server vs client state
      Interface / API (≈15%)Endpoints or GraphQL queries, parameters, pagination (cursor), real-time channel, component props APIs
      Optimisations (≈45%)Performance, network, rendering, accessibility, security, error handling, i18n, testing, monitoring

      Worked outline: news feed

      • Requirements: infinite scrolling feed of posts (text, images), like/comment, create post; fast on mobile; works on slow networks.
      • Architecture: SSR the first page for fast LCP/SEO; client takes over with a feed store; components: Feed → PostCard (Header, Media, Actions, Comments), Composer.
      • Data: Post { id, author, content, media[], createdAt, likeCount, viewerHasLiked }; normalise posts and users by id in the store.
      • API: GET /feed?cursor=abc&limit=10 → { posts, nextCursor }; POST /posts; POST /posts/:id/like; SSE/WebSocket for new-post notifications.
      • Optimisations: cursor pagination + IntersectionObserver sentinel; virtualised list for long sessions; responsive lazy images with blur placeholders and fixed aspect ratio (no CLS); optimistic likes with rollback; prefetch next page; cache and dedupe requests; stale-while-revalidate; "N new posts" banner instead of shifting content; skeleton loaders; keyboard navigation and alt text; sanitise user content (XSS); retry with backoff; error boundaries per card; analytics on impressions.

      Topics to have ready

      Rendering strategies (CSR/SSR/SSG/ISR/streaming, islands), state management and normalisation, caching layers (HTTP, CDN, service worker, in-memory), pagination styles, real-time (polling vs SSE vs WebSocket), offline/PWA, optimistic updates and conflict handling, image/asset strategy, bundle splitting, micro-frontends (module federation) and monorepos, design systems and component APIs, accessibility, i18n, security (XSS, CSRF, CSP, auth tokens), observability (logging, error tracking, Web Vitals), A/B testing and feature flags.

      Practice prompts: autocomplete, image carousel, chat app, e-commerce product page, video player (YouTube), Google Sheets, Trello board, photo gallery (Pinterest), notification system, travel booking flow, poll widget, analytics dashboard.

      DSA for frontend interviews

      Service companies and startups often skip it or ask easy problems; product companies (FAANG-style) ask easy–medium problems in JavaScript. Focus on patterns, not memorising solutions.

      PriorityTopics
      HighArrays and strings, hash maps/sets, two pointers, sliding window, sorting, stack/queue, recursion, Big-O
      MediumBinary search, trees (DOM is a tree!) and BFS/DFS, linked lists, intervals, prefix sums
      LowerHeaps, graphs, dynamic programming, tries (autocomplete!)

      Big-O cheat sheet

      O(1) Map/Set lookup, array index · O(log n) binary search · O(n) one loop, includes, indexOf, spread · O(n log n) sort · O(n²) nested loops. Array push/pop O(1); shift/unshift/splice O(n).

      Classic warm-ups

      js
      // Two Sum — hash map, O(n)
function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const seen = new Map();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (seen.has(need)) return [seen.get(need), i];
    seen.set(nums[i], i);
  }
  return [];
}
// Valid parentheses — stack
function isValid(s) {
  const pairs = { ")": "(", "]": "[", "}": "{" }, stack = [];
  for (const ch of s) {
    if ("([{".includes(ch)) stack.push(ch);
    else if (stack.pop() !== pairs[ch]) return false;
  }
  return stack.length === 0;
}
// Anagram — frequency count
const isAnagram = (a, b) => a.length === b.length && [...a].sort().join("") === [...b].sort().join("");
// Longest substring without repeating characters — sliding window
function longestUnique(s) {
  const last = new Map(); let start = 0, best = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) {
    if (last.get(s[i]) >= start) start = last.get(s[i]) + 1;
    last.set(s[i], i); best = Math.max(best, i - start + 1);
  }
  return best;
}
// Binary search
function bs(arr, x) {
  let lo = 0, hi = arr.length - 1;
  while (lo <= hi) { const mid = (lo + hi) >> 1; if (arr[mid] === x) return mid; arr[mid] < x ? (lo = mid + 1) : (hi = mid - 1); }
  return -1;
}

      Practice list: reverse a string/words, palindrome, FizzBuzz, max subarray (Kadane), merge sorted arrays, move zeros, rotate array, first non-repeating char, group anagrams, top-K frequent, merge intervals, level-order traversal, max depth of tree, fibonacci with memo. Use LeetCode's "Top Interview 150" or GreatFrontEnd's JS problems.

      Behavioural and project questions

      Answer with STAR: Situation (context), Task (your responsibility), Action (what you did, specifically), Result (outcome, ideally measurable, plus what you learned). Prepare 5–6 stories you can adapt.

      QuestionWhat they're checking
      Walk me through a project you built.Depth: why these choices, hardest part, what you'd change
      Tell me about a difficult bug.Debugging method, persistence, root-cause thinking
      A time you disagreed with someone.Communication, respect, commitment after a decision
      A time you failed or missed a deadline.Ownership, honesty, learning
      How do you learn a new technology?Self-direction (docs, building, reading source)
      Why frontend? Why this company?Motivation, research on their product
      How do you handle feedback in code review?Openness, collaboration

      Questions to ask them

      • What does the frontend stack and deployment process look like?
      • How are code reviews and testing handled?
      • What would success look like for me in the first 3 months?
      • How do juniors get mentored here?

      For every project on your resume, be ready to explain: the architecture, one technical decision and its trade-off, one bug you fixed, how you'd scale or test it, and exactly which parts you wrote.

      System design: more worked outlines

      Chat application (WhatsApp Web / Slack)

      • Requirements: 1:1 and group chats, real-time messages, typing indicators, read receipts, unread counts, history scrolling, attachments, offline sending; fast on mobile.
      • Architecture: SPA shell; WebSocket connection manager (auth, heartbeat, reconnect with backoff, resubscribe); normalised store: conversations, messages by conversation id, users; outbox queue in IndexedDB for offline sends.
      • Data: Message { id, clientId, conversationId, senderId, body, attachments[], createdAt, status: sending|sent|delivered|read|failed }.
      • API: REST for history (GET /conversations/:id/messages?before=cursor), WebSocket events (message.new, typing, receipt, presence).
      • Optimisations: optimistic send with clientId for dedupe and retries; reverse infinite scroll keeping scroll position; virtualised message list; throttle typing events; batch receipts; lazy-load media with placeholders; notification badge and title updates; accessibility with aria-live for incoming messages; sanitise message content; end-to-end encryption as a stretch topic.

      E-commerce product listing page (PLP)

      • Requirements: filters (price, brand, rating), sort, search, pagination/infinite scroll, add to cart/wishlist; SEO and fast LCP matter.
      • Architecture: SSR (or ISR) for the first page with filters in the URL (?brand=x&sort=price) so pages are shareable and crawlable; client hydration for filter interactions; cart in client store synced to server.
      • API: GET /products?filters…&cursor= returning products + facet counts; debounced filter changes; cancel stale requests.
      • Optimisations: responsive images with fixed aspect ratio; prioritise the first row's images; skeletons; prefetch product detail on hover; cache filter results; keep filter state on back navigation (restore scroll); accessible filter controls (fieldsets, labels, live region "124 results"); analytics on impressions.

      Designing a reusable component (e.g. a Select/Combobox for a design system)

      • API: v-model/value, options or slot-based items, multiple, searchable, async loading, custom rendering via slots, disabled/error states, form integration.
      • Behaviour: WAI-ARIA combobox pattern, keyboard (arrows, Home/End, typeahead, Escape), focus management, positioning (floating-ui / anchor positioning), portal/top layer.
      • Quality: virtualisation for 10k options, RTL, theming via tokens, tests (keyboard flows, screen reader roles), Storybook docs, versioning and deprecation policy.

      More prompts to practise out loud (45 minutes each, draw boxes on paper): Google Docs, YouTube player page, Instagram stories, Swiggy/Zomato order tracking, Jira board, Google Sheets grid, notification centre, image editor, flight booking flow, analytics dashboard with live data.

      The experience deep-dive round

      For 3 YOE, this round often decides the level you're offered. Interviewers pick one or two projects from your resume and go deep. Prepare each major project on one page:

      1. Context: product, users, team size, your role.
      2. Architecture: rendering strategy, folder structure, state management, API layer, auth, deployment. Be able to draw it.
      3. Your key decisions: two or three choices you made or influenced, the alternatives, and why.
      4. Hardest problem: a bug, performance issue or migration; how you investigated and fixed it.
      5. Impact: measurable results.
      6. Hindsight: what you'd change now and why.

      Questions to rehearse

      Walk me through the architecture of your current app.

      Start from the user request: how the app is served (SPA/SSR, CDN), routing, how data is fetched and cached, where state lives, how components are organised, how auth works, how it's tested and deployed. Draw it. Mention one weakness honestly and how you'd fix it.

      Tell me about the biggest performance improvement you made.

      Metric before → investigation (tools, what you found) → fixes (prioritised by impact) → metric after → how you kept it from regressing (budgets, monitoring). If you don't have one at work yet, do the "performance case study" project.

      How do you decide what goes in Pinia versus component state versus the URL?

      Server data in a query cache or useFetch; shareable view state (filters, tabs) in the URL; truly global client state (session, cart draft, preferences) in Pinia; everything else local. Derived values computed, never stored.

      Describe a production bug you handled.

      STAR: how it was detected, impact, immediate mitigation (rollback/flag), root cause, the fix and the regression test, and the process change afterwards (monitoring, review checklist).

      How do you ensure quality in your team's frontend?

      TypeScript strict + lint rules in CI, component tests for business logic, E2E for critical flows, preview deploys, code review guidelines, accessibility checks, performance budgets, error monitoring with alerts.

      Tell me about a disagreement with a designer, PM or backend engineer.

      Show that you understood their goal, brought data or alternatives (cost, risk, user impact), reached a decision, and committed to it, keeping the relationship good.

      How have you helped other engineers grow?

      Onboarding docs, pairing, thoughtful code reviews, internal talks, templates or lint rules that prevent common mistakes. Name a specific person and outcome.

      Why are you leaving, and why this company?

      Positive framing: growth you're seeking (scale, ownership, product domain, engineering practices) that this company offers, based on research into their product and stack. Never criticise your current employer.

      9Testing + Tooling (Vitest, component tests, MSW, Playwright, CI)Add a full test suite and GitHub Actions to project 1
      10Architecture & senior skills + Beyond the DOM + SQL & databasesWrite a tech spec and ADRs for project 2; design its schema and write its queries; SQL 50 on LeetCode (a few a day); 2 system design prompts out loud
      11Interview prep (system design, experience deep-dive, behavioural)Mock interviews (peers, Pramp-style platforms); prepare 6 STAR stories from work
      12Revision part weekly from now onUpdate resume/LinkedIn, start applying and asking for referrals

      Your biggest advantage over a fresher is real production experience. Every week, connect what you study to your work: "where does our app have this bug / this pattern / this performance issue?" Those connections become your best interview answers.

      Project ideas for 2026

      At 3 YOE, side projects aren't about proving you can code; they prove depth in areas your day job might not show (architecture, performance, testing, real-time, AI, a second framework). Build two of these well rather than five halfway. Each lists what it proves.

      1. AI document assistant (RAG) with streaming UI

      Upload PDFs/notes, ask questions, get streamed answers with citations that jump to the source passage.

      • Proves: streaming UI, server-side AI integration with keys protected, file handling, state for long conversations, safe Markdown rendering, cost/rate limiting.
      • Stack: Nuxt or Next.js, Vercel AI SDK (@ai-sdk/vue or react), a vector store (pgvector/Supabase), Zod structured outputs.
      • Stretch: tool calls ("create a summary doc"), conversation branching, evaluation page comparing answers, accessibility of streamed content.

      2. Local-first collaborative board (Trello/Notion-lite)

      Full step-by-step guide: Project 2: SyncBoard.

      Real-time multi-user kanban or document editor that works offline and syncs when back online.

      • Proves: CRDTs/sync (Yjs, Automerge, or a sync engine like Zero/Electric/Liveblocks), WebSockets, optimistic UI, conflict handling, IndexedDB, drag and drop, presence cursors.
      • Stretch: undo/redo, permissions, PWA install, E2E tests with two browser contexts editing at once.

      3. Design system + component library, published to npm

      A themeable, accessible component library (Button, Input, Select, Dialog, Tabs, Toast, Table, DatePicker) consumed by a demo app.

      • Proves: component API design, design tokens from Figma variables, accessibility (headless primitives), Storybook docs, visual regression tests, semantic versioning with Changesets, monorepo tooling.
      • Stretch: ship both Vue and Web Component builds, dark mode and RTL, bundle-size budget in CI.

      4. Performance case study: make a slow app fast

      Take an open-source or deliberately heavy app (10k-row table, big images, heavy libraries) and optimise it, documenting before/after metrics.

      • Proves: Core Web Vitals, profiling, virtualisation, code splitting, image strategy, caching, workers. This is gold for "tell me about a performance improvement".
      • Deliverable: a write-up with Lighthouse/Web Vitals numbers, flame-chart screenshots and the reasoning behind each fix.

      5. Real-time operations dashboard

      Live metrics (orders, deliveries, stock prices, IoT sensors) with charts, alerts and filters.

      • Proves: WebSocket/SSE with reconnection, throttled rendering of high-frequency updates, canvas/WebGL charts, URL-driven filters, virtualised tables, role-based access.
      • Stretch: web worker for aggregation, CSV export, offline snapshot, alert rules builder.

      6. Full-stack SaaS starter with multi-tenancy

      Invoicing, booking or habit-tracking SaaS with organisations, roles, billing and emails.

      • Proves: auth and authorisation, server actions/server routes, database design, payments (Stripe/Razorpay webhooks), email, feature flags, error monitoring, CI/CD.
      • Stack: Next.js or Nuxt + Drizzle/Prisma + Postgres + Better Auth/Auth.js.

      7. Same app, two frameworks (Vue ↔ React)

      Build a medium feature (e.g. an e-commerce listing with filters, cart and checkout) in Nuxt and in Next.js, sharing a typed API client and design tokens in a monorepo.

      • Proves: you're not "just a Vue developer"; you understand both reactivity models and rendering strategies. Great talking point for React-only job posts.
      • Deliverable: a README comparing bundle size, Web Vitals, DX and code for the same features.

      8. Micro-frontend demo

      A shell app loading a Vue "catalogue" and a React "checkout" via Module Federation, with shared auth and design tokens.

      • Proves: runtime integration, shared dependencies, cross-app communication, independent deploys, and the judgement to explain when not to use micro-frontends.

      9. Browser extension with AI

      A Chrome/Firefox extension (Manifest V3) that summarises pages, extracts tables to CSV, or flags accessibility issues on any site.

      • Proves: extension architecture (service worker, content scripts, messaging), permissions and security, working with arbitrary DOMs; publishable to the Chrome Web Store (real users).
      • Tools: WXT framework (supports Vue/React), on-device models where available or your own API.

      10. Accessible, offline-first PWA for a real local need

      Clinic appointments, society maintenance tracking, tuition attendance, or shop inventory, used by real people around you.

      • Proves: service workers and background sync, installability, WCAG 2.2 AA, i18n (Hindi/English), low-end Android performance, real user feedback.

      11. Open-source contribution in the Vue ecosystem

      Fix issues or add features in VueUse, Nuxt modules, Reka UI, Vitest, Pinia Colada, or a library your company uses.

      • Proves: reading large unfamiliar codebases, writing tests to a maintainer's standard, communication in public. Merged PRs in known repos stand out on a resume.

      12. Interactive 3D or data-visualisation piece

      A 3D product configurator (TresJS/three.js), an interactive explainer, or a D3 visualisation of an open dataset (Indian census, cricket stats, air quality).

      • Proves: graphics fundamentals, animation performance, responsive and accessible data presentation. Especially useful for agency, e-commerce and media roles.

      What every showcase project needs

      • TypeScript strict, tests (unit + component + at least one E2E flow), CI on every PR, deployed URL.
      • Lighthouse 90+ and keyboard-accessible; loading/empty/error states everywhere.
      • A README with a demo GIF, architecture diagram, key decisions and trade-offs, and what you'd do next.
      • A short write-up or blog post about the hardest problem you solved. Link it on LinkedIn.

      Presenting three years of experience

      With 3 YOE, your work experience is the headline and projects support it. The challenge: company code is private. Show it through stories and numbers, not repos.

      Mine your work history (do this before updating your resume)

      For each company, list: features you owned end to end, performance or quality improvements (with numbers if you have them), migrations (Vue 2 → 3, Vuex → Pinia, webpack → Vite), components or tooling you built that others used, incidents you handled, people you mentored or onboarded, and processes you improved (tests, CI, code review). If you don't have numbers, estimate carefully or measure now (bundle size, Lighthouse, build time).

      Public proof

      • GitHub: 2 pinned showcase projects from the 2026 list, plus any open-source PRs.
      • A simple portfolio site with those projects and 2–3 short case studies of work projects (anonymised: problem, your approach, result).
      • LinkedIn: headline like "Frontend Engineer · Vue, Nuxt, React, TypeScript · Performance & Design Systems"; post write-ups of what you build and learn.

      Resume for a 3-YOE frontend engineer

      • One page (two only if truly necessary), single column, ATS-friendly PDF.
      • Top: name, title ("Frontend Engineer"), links (GitHub, portfolio, LinkedIn), email, phone, city, notice period if short.
      • Summary (2 lines): "Frontend engineer with 3 years building Vue/Nuxt applications for [domain]; focus on performance, component architecture and testing. Also shipping React/Next.js."
      • Experience first, with 4–6 impact bullets per role; Skills; 1–2 Projects; Education.

      Impact bullets: action + what + how + measurable result

      WeakStrong
      Worked on the dashboard using Vue.jsRebuilt the analytics dashboard in Vue 3 + Pinia with virtualised tables and shallowRef data, cutting interaction latency from ~800 ms to under 150 ms for 20k-row views
      Did Vue 2 to Vue 3 migrationLed the incremental Vue 2 → 3 migration of a 180-component app using the compat build and Playwright regression suite, with zero production incidents
      Made reusable componentsBuilt a 30-component internal design system (Storybook, tokens, visual tests) adopted by 3 product teams, reducing UI bugs reported by QA by ~40%
      Improved performanceImproved mobile LCP from 4.1 s to 2.2 s via route-level code splitting, image CDN and SSR caching with Nuxt route rules

      Use real numbers from your work. If a number is an estimate, be ready to explain how you got it. Interviewers will ask about every bullet.

      The job switch

      Targeting

      • Titles to search: Frontend Engineer / SDE-2 (Frontend) / Senior Frontend Developer (at smaller companies) / UI Engineer / Software Engineer II.
      • Vue roles: search "Vue", "Nuxt" explicitly; common at SaaS companies, agencies, Laravel shops, and in Europe/Asia remote roles. React roles are the majority; apply once you've built one solid React/Next.js project and can do machine coding in React.
      • Product companies usually run DSA + system design; startups focus on machine coding and projects; service companies on framework knowledge and experience.

      Where and how

      • LinkedIn, Naukri, Instahyre, Wellfound, Cutshort, Hirist, company career pages, remote boards (We Work Remotely, Remote OK, Arc).
      • Referrals first: message engineers at target companies with a short note, your strongest project/case study, and the job link.
      • Keep your Naukri/LinkedIn profiles updated weekly during a search (recruiters sort by recency), with skills matching the job descriptions.
      • Track applications (company, role, date, contact, stage, next step). Aim for several interview loops at once so offers overlap.

      Notice period and offers

      • In India, 30–90 day notice periods are common and affect offers; know your buyout options and be upfront about dates.
      • Research ranges for your city, company type and level (AmbitionBox, Glassdoor, levels.fyi, peers). Compare total compensation: fixed, variable, joining bonus, ESOPs/RSUs (with vesting), benefits, remote policy, learning budget.
      • Negotiate politely with data: "Based on my experience with X and the range for this level, I was expecting around Y. Is there flexibility?" Competing offers help most.

      After you join

      First 30 days: set up, ship small fixes, learn the architecture and the people. Days 30–60: own a feature end to end. Days 60–90: propose one improvement (performance, tests, DX) with data. Keep your brag document going from day one.

      Part 5

      Revision

      Confusing pairs, rapid-fire interview questions and cheat sheets for JavaScript, React, Next.js and Vue.

      JavaScript: commonly confused pairs

      PairDifference
      var / let / constFunction vs block scope; const can't be reassigned; var hoists as undefined, others sit in TDZ.
      == / ===Loose (converts types) vs strict (type and value).
      null / undefinedEmpty on purpose vs no value assigned yet.
      || / ??Fallback on any falsy vs only on null/undefined.
      Parameter / argumentPlaceholder in the definition vs real value in the call.
      Rest / spreadSame ...; gathers in parameters and destructuring, expands everywhere else.
      Declaration / expression / arrowHoisted vs not hoisted; arrow has no own this/arguments.
      Pure / impure functionSame input same output, no side effects vs depends on/changes outside state.
      HOF / callbackThe function that takes/returns a function vs the function being passed.
      Closure / lexical scopeLexical scope is the rule (access by where code is written); a closure is a function keeping that access after its parent returned.
      for…of / for…inValues of iterables vs keys of objects.
      map / forEachReturns a new array vs returns undefined.
      slice / spliceCopies, no change vs removes/inserts, changes original.
      push/pop / unshift/shiftEnd of array vs start of array.
      Shallow / deep copyTop level only (nested shared) vs every level new.
      getElementById / querySelectorId only, no # vs any CSS selector, first match.
      NodeList / HTMLCollectionStatic, has forEach vs live, no forEach.
      textContent / innerText / innerHTMLAll text (fast) vs visible text (slow) vs HTML markup (XSS risk).
      appendChild / append / prependOne node at end vs many nodes/strings at end vs at start.
      input / change eventEvery keystroke vs when the value is committed.
      target / currentTargetWhere the event started vs where the listener is.
      Bubbling / capturingUp from target (default) vs down to target (true / {capture:true}).
      preventDefault / stopPropagationStops the browser's default action vs stops the event travelling to parents.
      setTimeout / setIntervalOnce after a delay vs repeatedly.
      localStorage / sessionStorage / cookiesForever vs until tab closes vs until expiry and sent to the server.
      Microtask / macrotaskPromises (run first) vs timers and events.
      call / apply / bindRun now with list vs run now with array vs return a bound function.
      Debounce / throttleRun after activity stops vs run at most every N ms.

      JavaScript: interview questions

      Try to answer out loud before opening each one.

      What are the data types in JavaScript?

      7 primitives (string, number, boolean, undefined, null, symbol, bigint) and objects (including arrays and functions).

      Why is typeof null "object"?

      A bug in the first implementation of JS. Fixing it would break old websites, so it stays.

      Explain hoisting.

      During the memory phase, declarations are registered before code runs. var becomes undefined, function declarations are fully available, let/const/class exist but are in the TDZ until their line.

      What is the Temporal Dead Zone?

      The period between entering a scope and the line where a let/const is initialised. Accessing it there throws a ReferenceError.

      What is a closure? Give a use case.

      A function that remembers the variables from the scope it was created in, even after that scope has finished. Use cases: private counters, data hiding, function factories, debounce.

      What is a higher-order function?

      A function that takes a function as an argument or returns one. map, filter, addEventListener.

      What is an IIFE and why use it?

      A function expression called immediately: (function(){ … })(). Creates a private scope so variables don't pollute the global scope.

      Difference between map, filter and reduce?

      map transforms every item (same length), filter keeps items passing a test (≤ length), reduce combines all items into one value.

      How does this work?

      It depends on the call: method call → the object; plain call → undefined (strict) / window; new → new object; call/apply/bind → what you pass; arrow → inherited from surrounding scope.

      What is the prototype chain?

      Every object links to a prototype object. Missing properties are looked up along these links until null. It's how inheritance and built-in methods work.

      Explain the event loop.

      JS runs code on a single call stack. Async work is handled by browser APIs; their callbacks queue up. When the stack is empty, the loop runs all microtasks (promises) first, then the next macrotask (timer/event).

      Output of: console.log(1); setTimeout(()=>console.log(2)); Promise.resolve().then(()=>console.log(3)); console.log(4);

      1 4 3 2.

      Promise vs async/await?

      Same thing underneath. async/await is syntax on top of promises that reads like synchronous code and uses try/catch for errors.

      What is event delegation?

      One listener on a parent handles events from its children using e.target, thanks to bubbling. Efficient and works for dynamically added elements.

      What is the DOM?

      A tree of objects the browser builds from HTML, which JS can read and modify to update the page.

      How do you store an object in localStorage?

      localStorage.setItem("k", JSON.stringify(obj)) and read with JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem("k")).

      How do you deep copy an object?

      structuredClone(obj), or JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj)) for plain data.

      What's the output of [1,2,3] + [4]?

      "1,2,34" — arrays become strings, then + concatenates.

      Why is 0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3?

      Numbers are binary floating point; 0.1 and 0.2 can't be represented exactly. Compare with a tolerance (Number.EPSILON) or work in integers (paise, not rupees).

      Difference between Object.freeze and const?

      const stops reassigning the variable. freeze stops changing the object's properties (shallowly).

      React & Next.js: commonly confused pairs

      PairDifference
      Props / stateInputs passed in by the parent (read-only) vs data the component owns and updates.
      Element / component<Card /> is an element (a description object); Card is the function that produces it.
      Controlled / uncontrolledValue lives in React state (value + onChange) vs in the DOM (defaultValue, ref, FormData).
      onClick={fn} / onClick={fn()}Pass the function vs call it during render.
      setX(x + 1) / setX(x => x + 1)Uses this render's snapshot vs always the latest value.
      state / refRe-renders on change, snapshot per render vs no re-render, mutable .current.
      useEffect / useLayoutEffectAfter paint vs before paint (layout measurement only).
      useMemo / useCallback / memoCache a value vs cache a function vs skip re-rendering a component.
      useState / useReducerSimple independent values vs many related updates through actions.
      Context / state libraryPass values down the tree (all consumers re-render) vs a store with selective subscriptions.
      Server state / client stateData owned by a backend (cache it) vs UI-only data.
      Virtual DOM / real DOMJS objects describing the UI vs the browser's actual nodes; React diffs the first to patch the second.
      Server Component / Client ComponentRuns only on the server, no JS shipped, can be async vs "use client", interactive, hydrates in browser.
      SSR / SSG / ISR / CSRPer request on server vs at build vs build + refresh later vs in the browser.
      Server Action / Route HandlerFunction called by your own UI for mutations vs HTTP endpoint for anyone.
      layout.tsx / template.tsxPersists across navigation vs remounts every navigation.
      loading.tsx / <Suspense>Automatic boundary for a whole segment vs manual boundary around any part.
      next/navigation / next/routerApp Router hooks vs Pages Router hook.
      updateTag / revalidateTagExpire and show fresh data now (in actions) vs mark stale and refresh in the background.
      proxy.ts / middleware.tsSame feature; renamed in Next.js 16.
      redirect() / router.push()Server-side (components, actions) vs client-side navigation.
      NEXT_PUBLIC_X / XExposed to the browser vs server-only.

      React & Next.js: interview questions

      Answer out loud first, then open.

      What is the virtual DOM and how does reconciliation work?

      A lightweight JS representation of the UI. On each update React creates a new tree, diffs it against the previous one (same type at same position → update; different type → replace; keys to match list items), and applies only the needed DOM operations.

      Why do lists need keys? Why not use the index?

      Keys identify items between renders so React can reorder and preserve state correctly. Indexes change when items are inserted, removed or sorted, attaching state to the wrong item.

      Why must state be updated immutably?

      React detects changes by comparing references. Mutating keeps the same reference, so React may skip the re-render, and memoisation/time-travel debugging break.

      Why does calling setCount(count + 1) three times only add 1?

      count is a snapshot for that render; all three calls compute the same value. Use setCount(c => c + 1).

      Explain useEffect's dependency array and cleanup.

      Effects run after render when dependencies change (none = every render, empty = mount only). The returned cleanup runs before the next effect and on unmount, to clear timers, listeners and subscriptions.

      What causes a component to re-render?

      Its state changes, its parent re-renders, or a context it reads changes. Props changing is really the parent re-rendering.

      When would you use useMemo/useCallback, and what does the React Compiler change?

      To avoid expensive recalculations or keep stable references for memoised children/effect dependencies. The compiler inserts this memoisation automatically, so manual use becomes rare.

      What is prop drilling and how do you avoid it?

      Passing props through components that don't need them. Fix with composition (children), context, or a state library.

      Controlled vs uncontrolled components?

      Controlled: React state holds the value and updates via onChange. Uncontrolled: the DOM holds it; read with refs or FormData.

      What are custom hooks?

      Functions starting with use that call other hooks to share stateful logic. Each caller gets independent state.

      What is hydration, and what's a hydration mismatch?

      Attaching React's event handlers and state to server-rendered HTML in the browser. A mismatch happens when the first client render differs from the server HTML (random values, dates, browser-only APIs, invalid nesting).

      Server Components vs Client Components?

      Server Components run only on the server, can be async and access data/secrets directly, and ship no JS. Client Components ("use client") support state, effects, events and browser APIs, and hydrate on the client. Server can render client, not import the other way (but can pass as children).

      How does data fetching differ in Next.js App Router vs plain React?

      Plain React fetches in effects or a library on the client after load. Next.js fetches in async server components before HTML is sent, can stream parts with Suspense, and caches results.

      Explain SSR, SSG, ISR and PPR.

      SSR renders per request; SSG renders at build; ISR serves static but regenerates after a time or on demand; PPR serves a static shell instantly and streams dynamic parts into Suspense holes.

      What are Server Actions and what must you always do inside them?

      Async server functions marked "use server", callable from forms and client code. They're public endpoints, so always authenticate, authorise and validate input inside them, then revalidate affected data.

      How do you cache and invalidate data in Next.js 16?

      Enable Cache Components, mark functions/components with "use cache", set cacheLife and cacheTag. Invalidate with updateTag (immediate, in actions), revalidateTag (background) or revalidatePath.

      What's the purpose of layout.tsx, loading.tsx and error.tsx?

      Shared persistent UI; automatic Suspense fallback while the segment loads; client error boundary with a reset option.

      How do you protect a route in Next.js?

      Optional early redirect in proxy.ts, plus a session check (e.g. a requireUser() helper) in the page, Server Actions and Route Handlers that touch private data.

      How do environment variables reach the browser?

      Only those prefixed NEXT_PUBLIC_, inlined at build time. Everything else stays on the server.

      How does next/image improve performance?

      Serves resized modern formats, lazy-loads offscreen images, reserves space to prevent layout shift, and supports blur placeholders and priority loading.
